• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

• Accreditations are valid for 5 years. Any changes to the project or programme that was provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected within this timeframe.

• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 30 pages. Exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.

• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

1. Viet Nam's rapid economic growth has led to a quadrupling of our capacity GHG emissions this century; from 0.9 Tg CO2e in the early 1980s to 3.6 Tg CO2e in 2014. The majority of these emissions are associated with deforestation and forest degradation, which is estimated at around 50% of total GHG emissions. Forest-related emissions are also a major source of air pollution associated with these emissions impairs health and productivity, exacerbates forest degradation, and amplifies the vulnerability to climate change. Viet Nam has lost approximately 12 million hectares of forest cover since 1986; 350,740 hectares of forest area, or 15,134,082 hectares were natural forests and 4,655,993 hectares were planted forests. The loss of forest cover has been most severe in the Central Highlands region, where approximately 1 million hectares of forest have been lost since 1986. This is a significant concern for Viet Nam as it is located in the Central Highlands region and addresses the complex sustainability challenges facing forests in an outstanding conservation area. The project will be implemented across five provinces: Dak Lak, Dak Nong, Gia Lai, Lam Dong, and Ninh Thuan.

2. The proposed project, "Achieving Emission Reductions in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast of Viet Nam through Forest Carbon Action Programmes (REDD+)", is a component of the National REDD+ Action Plan (NAP). It is a pivotal element of the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC) submitted by Viet Nam to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change. The project aims to contribute 150 million tonnes of CO2e to the national target for emission reductions in the country, and an additional 32.5 MCO2e towards the conditional contribution for LULUCF by 2030.

RECAP will support REDD+ phase I activities at national level, by updating and enhancing the National REDD+ policies and mechanisms to meet results-based payment (RBP) requirements of different donors and strategic upscaling of forest carbon action programmes in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast provinces. That will be in addition to the support already received by Viet Nam from the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) for the implementation of the National REDD+ Action Plan (NAP). The project will also contribute towards reducing deforestation, forest degradation, and implementing community forest management initiatives tailored to the needs of forest-dependent communities. It will do this through a combination of activities including: (i) strengthening the institutional framework and capacity building at national level; (ii) supporting the development of REDD+ action programmes in the five provinces; (iii) providing technical assistance for the implementation of these action programmes, including the creation and integration of the provincial level to mobilize concrete actions to develop deforestation-free value chains.


---
**Page 7**
---

chains, and linked to Viet Nam’s Payments for Forest Environmental Services (PFES) with enhanced performance-based criteria.

4. RECAF seeks to contribute to generate emission reduction (ERG) of 16.68 MtCO2-eq during its lifetime, through implementation of the following activities: i) REDD+ in forested areas (REDD+) and ii) Forest landscape restoration (FLR) as part of the budget component 2). A total of 143,000 ha of forestland adjacent to cropland and settlements will be transformed into agroforestry systems. This will be achieved through the implementation of a community-level collaborative forest management (CFMCC) model (50,000 ha under the agroforestry system and 94,000 ha under FLR), and the project PROVEM to fight illegal logging-related natural forests in target provinces.

5. As expected for an Agriculture, Forestry and other Land Uses (AFOLU) project, RECAF also provides significant opportunities for poverty reduction and social inclusion. The project will benefit 12 million people living in rural communities in the target provinces, including smallholder farmers, ethnic minorities, and other producers by increasing their access to markets, improving their livelihoods through agroforestry systems, improved forest management, erosion control, soil fertility management, and the supply of non-wood forest products (NWFPs), while also contributing to climate change mitigation. The project will contribute to poverty reduction in rural areas. Furthermore, improved land tenure, inclusive investment planning, and equitable resource distribution will generate economic benefits for smallholder farmers and ethnic minorities living in the target provinces. In total, 12 million people including smallholder farmers and ethnic minorities; and a total of 1,000,000 people will indirectly benefit from the project.

6. RECAF will promote capacity building and technology development/transfer. This will be achieved through research, training, and knowledge sharing, which will build awareness, understanding, and capacity among public and private sector stakeholders in Viet Nam to better manage forests and reduce deforestation. Technology investment/transfer will be promoted, including investments in local stations for monitoring forest cover change, the use of drones for aerial surveys, and the development of tools that allow farmers to manage their farms and respond timely to weather events. From a trade perspective, RECAF will support the creation of new markets for NWFPs and other products from agroforestry systems. The project will also need to establish continue reporting to the EU. This is in response to the new EU Regulation 2023/115 on deforestation-free supply chains. RECAF will contribute to reducing GHG emissions and biodiversity loss, as well as supporting countries in meeting their commitments under the Paris Agreement, first outlined in the 2019 Commission Communication on Stepping Up EU Action to Protect and Restore the World’s Forests.

7. Furthermore, RECAF will contribute to the GCF-2 Strategic Plan 2024–2027 targets in two major areas: i) mitigation, with a cumulative mitigation target of 6,844,338 CO2-eq per the project lifetime, achieved by covering a total area of 1.5 million ha; and ii) adaptation, with an adaptation target of 900,000 people benefiting from forests in the target provinces which will further reduce pressure on natural resources and facilitate conservation programs. RECAF will also contribute to the achievement of the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): 2 for food security; 15 for life on land; 13 for climate action; and 17 for partnerships for the goals. The project will also support the achievement of the following SDG targets: 2.4 for food security; 15.0.1 for terrestrial ecosystems; and 15.0.2 for forest systems.

8. It is important to mention that during the preparation of this proposal, there have been talks taken place between RECAF and the only CIFD Direct Access Entity in Viet Nam, the Viet Nam Forest Investment Board (VIFB), which mostly focuses on land use planning. These discussions aimed at assessing VIFB’s interest and capacity in leading supporting activities for RECAF. VIFB is currently not a CIFD Direct Access Entity. However, it has expressed its willingness to be a Non-CIFD Climate Resilience entity under the CINCO (N2CNC) mechanism. In addition, VIFB has also expressed interest in being a CIFD Direct Access Entity for RECAF. RECAF will complement and complement and coordinate with VIFB’s activities as well as other entities that are already active in the sector. For GCF, RECAF will complement and complement and coordinate with the work of the Forest Investment Board (FIB) and the Low Emission Development Strategy Action Plan (LEDSAP), and the recent RBF issued by FIB under CIFD from FCPP in March 2024 for a total of USD 51 million (invested in provinces not covered by RECAF).

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### B.1.1 Viet Nam's environmental background: an urgent climate issue

9. Climate change significantly impacts Viet Nam, especially in terms of precipitation and temperature shifts. As global temperatures rise, Viet Nam experiences more extreme weather, including heavier monsoons and prolonged droughts. The country is projected to reach the 4°C index by 2024². Viet Nam has improved its rank, but it still ranks among the top 10 most vulnerable countries to climate events. This vulnerability exacerbates forest problems, with Viet Nam experiencing increased fire frequency and soil degradation. Furthermore, higher temperatures are leading to more frequent droughts, which are degrading these critical ecosystems. This combination of factors is putting pressure on forests, especially in lowland forests, under severe stress, threatening biodiversity.

10. Mean annual temperature in Viet Nam has increased by 0.2°C per decade since the mid-1950s, with the most rapid rise in southern Viet Nam and the Central Highlands. The average annual precipitation in Viet Nam was 4.87°C per decade, this is reported as being the wettest period on record for that time period. Projected temperature increases range between 2.3°C to 6.1°C by mid-century compared with the 1980-2005 baseline. El Niño remains a major influence in Viet Nam's climate and can cause sea level some changes are significant: the general trend has been towards higher sea levels, which have increased and reduced rainfall in northern and southern regions (on average, by about 3% per decade).

11. Viet Nam faces high disaster risk levels, ranked 83 out of 190 countries by the 2024 INFORM CC Risk Index², driven mainly by tropical cyclones and storms, which cause flash and coastal flooding. Also, the country is affected by tropical cyclones and storm surges, though these hazards are most prevalent in southern Viet Nam. The main drivers of disaster risk in Viet Nam are flash floods caused mainly veritable to flash floods and landslides caused by heavy rainfall and typhoons particularly the Dak Lak and Kon Tum provinces. Some models project an increase of 10% in the annual probability of drought over the next decades. Increased temperatures will also lead to more frequent and severe droughts, with water scarcity and reduced water are the two main drivers behind the increasing incidence of drought. Within the RECAF project boundaries, Nam Tho is one of the most vulnerable areas due to its effects, particularly on sloping and remote areas (an extensive CC analysis is available in Annex 2: Ch. 3).

12. As reported in the Viet Nam's 23rd Biennial Report (BUR) (Technical Annex),³ the Central Highlands (within the RECAF project boundaries) are experiencing some of the highest rates of deforestation and forest degradation in the country. These regions population growth, poverty, and lack of access to basic services have made them more vulnerable to climate change due to significant increases in temperatures and precipitation, which in turn provoke socioeconomic vulnerabilities.

**Page 9**

13. In the RGF provinces, the agriculture and forestry sectors account between 28% and 50% of provincial GDP and from 80% to 90% of employment. Thus, agriculture and forestry are critical activities for the economy and livelihoods of millions of people in these regions.

The Central Highlands account for nearly 90% of the country’s coffee cultivation area. Until 2016, the expansion of this crop was driven by high prices and low costs. However, since then, coffee prices have fallen to their lowest level on logging ban of 2018* and lower global commodity prices, the crops’ expansion rate broke as well as pressure on the forest resources. The area under coffee has decreased from a peak in 2016 (354,790 ha) to about 200,000 ha by late 2023. Coffee yields have doubled since late 2023 due to shortages, raising concern that farmers will again clear forest and plant.

The intricate interplay between socioeconomic conditions and vulnerabilities, such as poverty, uncertain land tenure security, lack of access to markets, and weak institutions, is a major driver for deforestation and other environmental problems including deforestation and forest degradation. This is further compounded by the absence of alternative livelihoods in many areas. The expansion of coffee cultivation has been one of the most significant as a significant consequence of these factors, resulting in increased emissions, degradation of soil and ecosystems.

### B.1.2 GHG emissions from land use sectors

#### A1. Forest sector
The forest sector is the largest source of greenhouse gas (GHG) causing global warming, after energy generation. Viet Nam’s updated Nationally Determined Contribution to the Paris Climate Agreement of 2022 reaffirms the goal of reducing deforestation and forest degradation in the LULUCF sector by 53% compared with business as usual scenario for reduction contribution for LULUCF to 32.5 MtCO2-eq by 2028. The conditional emission reductions from LULUCF are an additional 14.7 MtCO2-eq, which would bring the total emissions almost half (6.6MtCO2-eq) of the national LULUCF emission reductions in the LULUCF sectors by 2030.

#### A2. Agriculture sector
While GHG emissions in LULUCF have stabilized somewhat, compared to historical emission rates (particularly pre-1985), they are still a significant source of emissions and will continue to be so for decades. This is due to the conversion of natural forest to plantations. Most of the carbon sequestration comes from the extensive reforestation of former agricultural land in the Mekong Delta, while the remaining 20% of the total area under cover is maintained, the biodiversity of such mono-crops is much lower and does not provide equal levels of ecosystem services as natural forests. The conversion of forest to plantations has a negative impact on the health and nutrition profile of local populations’ livelihoods and nutrition purpose.

#### A3. Agriculture sector
Furthermore, agricultural activity on converted forestland (whether officially registered as farmland or land) continues to be a significant source of emissions in LULUCF. This is due to the conversion of natural forest to plantations that are currently not sequestering carbon where they did in the past. Agroforestry, which involves integrating trees into agricultural systems, can help mitigate this problem by increasing soil organic matter and water retention in soils, and diversified income sources for farmers to cope with commodity price fluctuations and climate change impacts. Agroforestry also provides a range of ecosystem services that are critical for biodiversity efforts to protect and restore forests adjacent to agricultural areas and rural communities. This can be achieved through the implementation of agroforestry practices on converted forestland, the establishment of buffer zones, and the implementation of community forestry practices that promote sustainable forest management.

### B2. Addressing vulnerability in the forest sector
The project represents approximately 10% of the national territory and 18% of the country’s forests, yet are expected to lose about 20% of their forest area from deforestation and forest degradation in the next 10 years (see Annex 2). The region is characterized by high vulnerability due to its location on the edge of the Mekong Delta where most of the project provinces situated, experienced a significant loss of natural and planted forests between 2000-2020. In total, about 354,790 hectares of forest were degraded, accounting for 34% of the national total. This region experienced the most significant deforestation in Vietnam during this period (see Annex 1). The vulnerability is further exacerbated by drivers in the project area is complex and has changed considerably over time (see Annex 2). Despite the successful reforestation efforts made since 2005, the forest cover in the project area has decreased significantly over that period, including a lack of significant reference information. Demonstrating the link between deforestation and socio-economic, geographic characteristics. The strongest negative correlation is found in relation to human settlements. Indeed, 43.3% of deforestation and 24.8% of illegal logging were

The project's district and beneficiary selection is based on a combined analysis including the following variables: forest cover, deforestation rate, population density, poverty level, vulnerability to climate change, socio-economic condition, ethnic or non-ethnic regions, population living in forest areas, and a detailed assessment of the potential for agroforestry. The project will be implemented in 10 districts in Kon Tum province, and/or for the introduction of agroforestry/multi-cropping with shade trees. ### B.1.4 Tools to tackle Viet Nam's deforestation and forest degradation

#### 21. Reductions in deforestation and forest degradation in Viet Nam

Viet Nam is a central participant in the Paris Climate Change Strategy. The REDD+ National Action Plan (NAP), first approved in 2012 and then updated in 2017, sets out Viet Nam's national strategy for reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+) under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change’s (UNFCCC) decision. which urge countries to address the drivers of deforestation and forest degradation. The REDD+ NAP is aligned with Viet Nam’s National Climate Change Strategy and achieving Viet Nam's Nationally Determined Contribution to the Paris Climate Change Agreement. The Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) has supported Viet Nam in achieving REDD+ objectives and receives results-based payments for the North Central Region of Viet Nam under the FCPF’s 2014-2023 Strategy. This project will build on this experience by supporting MRV implementation. Yet these forests have suffered roughly 1/3 of the deforestation and forest degradation in the country, and are therefore a priority area for REDD+ activities.

The project area approaches the later stages of the forest transition curve, providing the “++” aspect of REDD+ becomes important. The project will focus on reducing the drivers of deforestation and forest degradation by addressing impacts in Viet Nam, particularly at the provincial, district, and commune levels, to effectively plan and implement activities that reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (EDF) while also addressing the impacts of climate change on key crops like rice, coffee, and cashew. Without proactive interventions to enhance agricultural productivity and diversification, these crops will be increasingly vulnerable to climate change.

In 2018, Viet Nam’s total forest area was 43 million hectares, representing about 57% of the country's land area. Between 2010 and 2018, forest loss was 30% of the national total (360,532 ha) and 34% of national degradation (490,000 ha). The majority of this deforestation and forest degradation occurred in ethnic minority regions. These are areas where ethnic minorities – who heavily rely on agriculture and NWFPs for their livelihoods. These communities face limitations to accessing resources due to lack of infrastructure, limited access to markets, poor land tenure security, and finance (2018). Therefore, addressing deforestation and forest degradation in these regions requires a combination of approaches that address the drivers of deforestation and forest degradation while also creating pathways that allow farmers and forest-dependent individuals and communities to engage in market activities for their livelihood.

#### 22. In supporting implementation of key NAP (2017) activities in the Central Highlands provinces of Gia Lai, Dak Lak, Dak Nong, and Lam Dong, as well as an Nth Thuan province in the South-Central Coast, REDD+ is filling a key gap by providing technical assistance to support MRV implementation.

Viet Nam has 54 recognized ethnic groups. Of which 10 are minority ethnic groups, and it therefore constitutes a multi-ethnic country. The Government of Viet Nam (GoVN) recognizes that the majority of its forests are located in areas inhabited predominantly by ethnic minorities. It is estimated that REDD+ will reach at least 705,000 beneficiary households, of which at least 46% are ethnic minorities who are living and working on or near forested land. The GoVN has recognized the importance of involving indigenous peoples (IPs) in the design and implementation of REDD+. A National Action Plan for IPs has been developed following the UNFCCC and IPFB’s Indigenous Peoples Policies and guaranteeing Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 7

## Achieving market-driven free-commodity production, as mandated by the MRAP, is crucial for meeting these needs. Increasing market demands are pushing producers towards this direction, necessitating support in producer capacity building and institutional strengthening.

### The proposed project will aim to:
- Implement and maintain the REDD+ provincial socio-economic development plans (SEDPs)
- Improve coordination and capacity to implement SEDPs
- Support effective implementation of the SEDPs by providing training and technical assistance

### Project Activities:
1. **Integration of REDD+ into Provincial Socio-Economic Development Plans (SEDPS):** Building capacity to integrate REDD+ objectives, targets, and activities into five-year provincial SEDPs by providing training and technical assistance.
2. **Planning and Implementation Support:** Provinces have completed Provincial REDD+ Action Plans. However, limited institutional capacity remains a challenge in the provinces' ability to implement these plans within their SEDPs.
3. **Strengthening Local Stakeholder Engagement:** Enhancing the capacity of local stakeholders, including government agencies, community representatives, and civil society organizations, to actively participate in the integration process through awareness-raising activities, training workshops, and interdepartmental communication.

### Cross-Sectoral Collaboration:
- Strengthening capacity for cross-sector collaboration between government departments, NGOs, and other relevant actors.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Private Sector Engagement: Building private sector and local farmer capacity to meet REDD+ requirements for deforestation-free agricultural commodities and facilitating access to financial mechanisms and investment opportunities.

### Monitoring and Evaluation: Building capacity for monitoring and evaluating the integration of REDD+ into existing forest management systems, including the development of a national early warning system based on integrated REDD+ and economic development activities.

#### Operational Capacity Building: Enhancing operational capacity by utilizing NFIIS data effectively for operational early warning systems, including training stakeholders on utilizing NFIIS data and integrating it with real-time monitoring tools to address deforestation, forest degradation, and illegal encroachment activities.

##### Subnational Capacity Building: Enhancing capacity at the subnational level by building technical expertise of government officials in implementing REDD+ activities. This will be achieved through the use of NFIIS data and other tools to monitor and address deforestation, forest degradation, and illegal encroachment activities.

Performance-Based Financial Mechanism: The Performance-Based Financial Mechanism (PBFM) has been operational since 2008, but does not contain distribution criteria based on performance of stewardship activities. With the introduction of a new mechanism, Viet Nam can create stronger incentives for land users to actively participate in REDD+ activities.

There are seven challenges and capacity gaps that need to be urgently addressed to enhance the overall effectiveness of Viet Nam's REDD+ efforts: (1) lack of data on forest cover change; (2) limited access to continuous updates and enhancements of FIREFLRS, SIS, NRIAP, and NRMPs is important to maintain eligibility for payments under LEAF IFAD; (3) the PBFM will also support the country in operationalizing the implementation of Article 12 under the Paris Agreement.

### B.1.6.4. REDD+ Implementation

#### RECAF will leverage and build upon past and ongoing REDD+ support initiatives, specifically the UN-REDD Programme, the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) of the World Bank, and the Lowering Emissions by Addressing Agriculture and Forestry (LEAF) Initiative. The LEAF IFAD project is a joint initiative between Forest Management through Deforestation-free Jurisdiction Project, lead by UNDP and in partnership with IDH, UNEP, and CCAFS, and the Climate Investment Funds (CIF) project, led by CIF and in partnership with IDH, UNEP, and CCAFS. This effort was which was partially funded for Lam Dong and Dak Nong Provinces, and these activities form a basis to build upon.

### B.1.7. Relevant Enabling Policies for REDD+ in Viet Nam

The policy framework is evolving and includes several changes for REED+ to address deforestation and forest degradation, enhance forest carbon stocks, and simultaneously improve the livelihoods of forest-dependent communities. These include:

- The National Strategy on Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR) 2016–2035 (Decree No. 48/2016/QĐ-TTg National REDD+ Action Program (NRP) in 2017; Forestry Law 2017); Environmental Protection Law (2019); Forests and Wildlife Conservation Law (2019); Decree No. 35/2018-CP on the Management of Forest Resources (2018); Decree No. 62/2020-CP on Mitigation of Greenhouse Gas Emissions and Protection of the Ozone Layer (2020); Decree No. 74/2021-CP on Strengthening Management of Forest Products Trade (2021); Decree No. 58/2022-CP on Strengthening Management of Forest Products Trade (2022); Prime Minister's Directive 05-CT/TTr dated May 18th, 2020 on Strengthening management of forest products trade; Decision No. 349/QD-TTg dated June 16th, 2020 on the National Strategy for Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR) 2016–2035; Decision No. 18/2019/QĐ-TTg dated March 18th on Regional Program on Forest Protection, Restoration and Sustainable Development in the Central Highlands provinces; Decision No. 47/2020/QĐ-TTg dated May 16th, 2020 on Strengthening management of forest products trade.

### 30 Other enabling policies of note and areas for increased alignment

The Voluntary Partnership Agreement between the European Union and the Socialist Republic of Viet Nam on forest law enforcement, governance and trade (VPA) entered into force in April 2019. Timber from Vietnam is now subject to VPA requirements.

Viet Nam of timber and timber products have been legally produced; to promote trade in timber products that are free from illegal logging and associated environmental damage, the Government has strengthened forest law enforcement and governance. There may be opportunities for greater policy alignment between REDD+ and the 2016 Law on Crop Production (LCP) and the 2018 Law on Forests (LF). The LCP requires that all agricultural land under crop production shall comply with laws on environmental protection, and strategies to adapt to climate change and decrease GHG emissions. The LF provides for a five-year planning period from 2021 to 2030 (with a vision to 2050) enables spatial and sectoral planning at Provincial and higher levels. The LF also sets out the principles of forest management, including the integration with other policy goals related to poverty or sector growth plans. However, the Planning Law does allow implementation of some REDD+ activities in the context of land use planning, but it is not clear how this will be done within defining areas for exploitation. There are opportunities in the next 5-year development planning cycle to explore ways to integrate REDD+ into the LCP and LF.

Vietnam's Sustainable Coffee Plan for 2020 and Vision for 2030 does not include commitments to REDD+ or to define forest carbon stocks.

### B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

#### B.2(a).1. Project Goal and Goal Statement

The project aims at reducing GHG emissions from AFOLU sector, contributing to the climate resilient sustainable development of forest-dependent communities in the target provinces. To achieve this goal, the project will test its participatory model predicated on the premise that:

Goal Statement: If Viet Nam's REDD+ strategy and MAP are fully updated and integrated into provincial socio-economic development processes with effective public-private-producer partnerships (4Ps) and financial and legal frameworks, then by 2030, the target provinces will have significantly reduced GHG emissions from AFOLU sector. The project will scale up its sustainable land use and forest management across the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast and beyond to achieve this goal. To do so, it will strengthen the capacity of communities, governments, and institutions/foundations, technical capacity, and financial means will have been placed to reduce GHG emissions from the AFOLU sector while contributing to the climate-resilient sustainable development of forest-dependent communities.

## Figure 2: Theory of Change Diagram

### B.2.1.1: Barriers

The project focuses on developing deforestation-free value chains, fostering alternative livelihood options, and implementing NDCs and REDD+ actions in the provinces of Kon Tum, Gia Lai, and Dak Lak. The accompanying Theory of Change diagram (Figure 2) visually depicts these dependencies and barriers to achieving the project’s objectives.

#### B.2.1.2: Barriers

The project proposes addressing the following barriers to meet the goal statement:

- **Barrier 1**: At provincial level, there is a lack of interdepartmental, cross-sectorial, and public-private coordination to implement NDC and REDD+ objectives.
- **Barrier 2**: Political and institutional capacity gaps at the national level to access REDD+ Results-Based Financing (RBF) mechanisms. Vietnam has developed REDD+ readiness, including forest monitoring, measurement, reporting, and verification (MRV), but there are still challenges in implementing these systems at provincial and district levels.
- **Barrier 3**: Financial constraints for provinces to implement NDCs and REDD+ actions.

### B.2.1.3: Barriers

The project proposes addressing the following barriers:

#### Barrier 1: Interdepartmental, cross-sectorial, and public-private coordination gaps at provincial level
- Provinces are requested to contribute to Viet Nam's national forest monitoring system (NFM) by providing data on deforestation and forest degradation.
- The project will require longer-term investments in operations as well as changes to the next 5-year provincial development plan (PDP).
- There is a need for capacity building at district levels, including training on MRV systems.

#### Barrier 2: Political and institutional capacity gaps at national level
- Vietnam has developed REDD+ readiness, including forest monitoring, measurement, reporting, and verification.
- However, there are still challenges in implementing these systems at provincial and district levels.
- There is a need for capacity building at the national level to access REDD+ RBF mechanisms.

#### Barrier 3: Financial constraints
- Provinces face financial constraints when implementing NDCs and REDD+ actions.
- The project will require longer-term investments in operations as well as changes to the next 5-year provincial development plan (PDP).

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

#### Barrier 3: Lack of secured land tenure:
The Central Highlands face land tenure challenges that are complex and multi-dimensional. These include a lack of secure land tenure, poor rural management and agroforestry take time to provide economic and environmental services. Therefore, rural households in the region have limited access to credit for investment in forest conservation and adopting more sustainable land-use practices. The Government of Viet Nam nationalized forest resources in 1958 with the Forest Law (Law No. 20/1958) which was amended several times. However, local communities and traditional forest management practices were ignored. Sometimes land traditionally cultivated (through slash-and-burn agriculture) is converted to forest land by the government, resulting in displaced farmers who then enter into the forests. This results in disputes over land tenure until today. The Forest Law of 2013 did not recognize allocation of natural production forests to local communities; by contrast, the Forestry Law of 2013 recognized the rights of forest users and their right to use forest resources according to customs and traditions as "legal owner of forest and giving priority in forest allocation to ethnic minority groups and communities with long-term residence in forest areas". However, these laws are not always enforced. Laws are in force. Furthermore, there is no legal definition of community-based forest management in Viet Nam. Forest Management Committees (FMCs) are the main institutions for managing forests. In order to obtain returns, allocation criteria have at times excluded poor; particularly ethnic minority and women-led households who are often the most vulnerable groups. The lack of secure land tenure has resulted in disputes over decision-making has resulted in disputes. (See more details in annexes SA and SB and T and V 8). Thus, forestland management is not always effective.

#### Barrier 4: Lack of capacity and incentives for private sector action:
Government targets for the private sector continue to focus on raising the efficiency and competitiveness by increasing productivity, quality, and added value. Key measures include the promotion of new varieties and hybrid seeds; increased access to credit; seed and breeding subsidies; credit subsidy schemes; reduction of land taxes and extension services. However, there is a lack of capacity for private sector actors to comply with forest management development policies and requirements for Environmental Impact Assessments to obtain investment permits have become more complex in recent years. The Forest Law (2013) requires that all forest investments be registered under the EU for Forest Law Enforcement Governance and Trade (FLEGT) and recently enacted European Union Deforestation Risk Assessment Regulation (DRA). In addition, there is a lack of capacity to comply with these requirements. There has been little demand for financial products to facilitate such investments and commercial banks and microfinance institutions are not always willing to provide loans or credit facilities. The private sector is also limited by the fact that it is still at an early stage in terms of forest management, particularly in relation to long-term investment (including; at the micro level: medium to long term funding capacity) and knowledge on the subject. While the Viet Nam Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (VBARD) provides loans for investments in large-scale enterprises, there is a "missing middle" between these and small-scale forest enterprises. The Forest Investment Fund of the Vietnam Forest Development Bank (VDB) provides loans for sustainable investments by large scale enterprises; there is a "missing middle" between these and small-scale forest enterprises.

#### Barrier 5: Inequitable distribution of costs and benefits of forests:
The private sector has been more successful in accessing the financial services designed for forest management. However, many efforts to promote community-based forest management have not been as successful. The lack of capacity among many ethnic minorities to access these services is a major barrier. Additionally, there are inequitable distribution of costs and benefits between the government and private sector. Farmers who are members of formal cooperatives or associations tend to benefit from better access to credit facilities than those who are not. However, in some cases, the profits generated by forest management activities accrue to the cooperative state; there is a lack of farmer organizations that provide effective services to their members and not all agricultural cooperatives are held accountable for the benefits they claim.

#### Barrier 6: Inequitable distribution of costs and benefits of forests:
In the past, forest enterprises and management boards profited from timber harvesting in natural production forests until a


---
**Page 16**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

logging and timber export were banned in 2014. Since then, only plantation forests have generated timber income. Meanwhile, local communities continue to depend on the resources provided by healthy natural forests, including NFRPs, which help sustain water supplies for drinking, irrigation, and hydropower companies as they safeguard water sources, serving both the local population and the long-term interests of national development. The Government has introduced a Payment for Forest Ecosystem Services (PFES). PFES payments are channeled through the Provincial Forest Promotion Fund (PFPF) to households that provide forest patrol services. As a result, PFES payments are de facto turned into payments for labor, and in some cases, they are not even paid at all. This is because many of these households are women-headed households who provide patrolling services. An additional challenge is the exclusion of women-headed households from participating in other forms of forest management activities due to their lack of secure land tenure, especially among smallholders, ethnic minorities, and women-headed households. In order to overcome this barrier, the project will support the development of a new mechanism that allows women to contribute PFES. Consequently, the existing mechanisms do not provide sufficient incentives to encourage land users to participate in forest management activities. The project will also ensure that all forest-dependent communities listed forest carbon sequestration and retention services as eligible for payment for forestry ecosystem services, it will support the development of a new mechanism that allows women to contribute PFES. Furthermore, Decree 102/2017 on carbon payment in the North Central region provide additional guidance, but operational details are still under discussion.

# B.2 (a).5 Risks

*Please see Section F for more details.*

Each of these risks is considered to be significant as market fluctuations, pandemics, and conflicts pose a potential risk to this proposal. The project currently assumes that smallholders can achieve sustainable livelihoods through the adoption of agroforestry practices and the implementation of climate-smart agricultural programs, rather than engaging in enrichment activities. The Economic and Financial Analysis (EFA), outlined in Annex 10, provides an overview of the financial viability of the project. The EFA indicates that the project will be profitable in the long run. To ensure the success of the innovative PES mechanism, a study on fair pricing for carbon services is required to determine the appropriate price for carbon credits. If there are significant changes due to various factors, including fluctuations in international carbon markets, pandemic-related restrictions, and conflicts, the project may no longer be profitable. In such cases, it will be necessary to revert back to low PES payments; it may once again become more profitable to resort to deforestation for crop cultivation. To mitigate this risk, the project aims to ensure that smallholders are able to generate sufficient income from agroforestry and enterprise-level keys, key measures to address this concern involve through financial analysis of project investment and revenue streams.

On the one hand, there is increasing interest due to livelihood needs and external pressures leading to reduced forest areas. RECAF will demonstrate practical deforestation-free supply chain management and alternative livelihood options to vulnerable communities in Vietnam. ### 4C. Demand for deforestation-free products continues

This assumption is based on the recent European Union Deforestation Regulation (EUDR) mandating that key agricultural commodities (coffee being the largest) must be certified as deforestation-free by 2021. The EUDR will also require companies to report annually on their progress in reducing deforestation.

The assumption is also based on increasing awareness among consumers about the impact of climate change and the challenges faced by indigenous communities, which are likely to increase demand for deforestation-free products. Companies making conscious purchasing decisions that prioritize deforestation-free products, leading to a rise in demand.

### 4DD. REDD+ RBPA opportunities are available

This assumption is supported by the ongoing processes under LEAF (Land and Forest Ecosystems Alliance) and REDD+ International Mechanism (RIM). These processes provide international commitment to climate action in the forestry sector at the Conference of Parties (COP) 27 of the UNFCCC. These factors provide additional momentum and opportunities for the implementation of REDD+ initiatives.

#### B.2 (c): Outcome mapping to GCF results areas and co-benefit categorization

To contribute to GCF Migration Results Area 4 (Forestry and Land Use) and GCF’s Outcomes 1 and 2 more specifically, the project will support Vietnam in implementing its REDD+ strategy. The REDD+ outcomes align with Viet Nam’s NDC and NARP, deliver RBPA, meet national REDD+ information requirements, and generate emissions reductions and enhanced carbon sinks.

The following table outlines the GCF Migration Results Area (MRA) 1-4 and GCF Adaptation Results Area (ARA) 1-4 that are relevant to Viet Nam’s REDD+ strategy:

| Outcome 3: | MRA 3 (climate change adaptation and land use) | ARA 3 (built environment services) |
| Outcome 4: | MRA 4 (climate change adaptation and ecosystem services) | ARA 4 (ecosystem services) |

### 5.1 The main co-benefits from RECAF are:

- Adaptation co-benefit (increased resilience of forest-dependent communities)
- Economic (improved land tenure, includes investment planning, and equitable resource distribution)
- Social (enhanced livelihoods for vulnerable groups, including appropriate measures for ethnic minorities)

These have been adequately considered in the design and will continue to be monitored during implementation. The project will also contribute to the empowerment of indigenous people and their communities. Further information is provided in Annex 6B - Indigenous Peoples Plan.

### 5.2 The co-benefits are expected to be achieved through indicators that will be measured during the implementation stage. These indicators will serve as benchmarks to monitor and evaluate the effectiveness of the co-benefit establishment, including the project’s ability to adapt to new information or changing conditions. This dynamic approach will mitigate potential adverse outcomes and enhance the project's resilience in the face of climate change impacts. The project will also be evaluated throughout its lifespan. Mitigation refers to actions that inadvertently increase vulnerability to climate change, while adaptation is a response that can be successfully avoided by integrating project management strategies and comprehensive risk assessments.

## B. Project description (max. 2900 words, approximately 5 pages)

This section focuses on describing the outcomes, outputs, activities, and sub-activities to elaborate on the project's approach and strategy. Thus, Annex 24 includes a table of activities with detailed information about the EE and its funding sources.

## Activity description (max. 180 words, approximately 3 pages)

### Output 1: Vel Nari’s REDD+ design pillars are fully upgraded and integrated into provincial policies & institutional frameworks with improved private-producer partnerships (PPPs) to operationalize REDD+

This output will ensure the full integration of Vel Nari’s REDD+ design pillars into provincial policies, institutional frameworks, and PPPs. The NRFS, FRELURF, NFMS, SIS and region provide solid foundations from which to move in minor forest land use change (LFUC) areas. This will be achieved by upgrading Vel Nari’s REDD+ design pillars with the support of existing provincial policies and institutional frameworks that facilitate effective stakeholder coordination. This integration will foster 40% of all project activities, including the development of deforestation-free value chains and community forestry initiatives.

### Output 1.1: National REDD+ policies and mechanism upgraded to fulfill all required functions, including Afforestation/Reforestation (A/R) and Forest Carbon Accounting (FCA), and to deliver NRFS capacity in Phase 2 (REDD+ implementation) activities

These are detailed below:

#### Activity 1.1.1: Upgrade National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS) and Sub-district Information System (SIS)

In collaboration with FIP, this activity will support the DoF to upgrade its NFMS to achieve all emission reduction and enhancement functions required by Vel Nari’s REDD+ design pillars. This will be achieved in close alignment with meeting the ART/REES® standard applied by the LEAF coalition. Activity 1.1.1 will enhance the NFMS and SIS by applying the latest technology, including satellite imagery, to improve data accuracy and reduce costs of implementation. Vel Nari has both technical and financial capacities to produce activity data and emission factors for A/R afforestation activities in Phase 2 (REDD+ implementation) activities. This will allow Vel Nari to access new options for Results-Based Payments in areas outside the DoF’s jurisdiction, including through the REDD+ mechanism of the ART/REES® standard. The activity will enhance its transparency, inclusiveness and accessibility by strengthening the linkages between the monitoring of Vel Nari’s REDD+ design pillars and the NRFS. This will also support the DoF to better manage forest resources and coordinate RFPs from different sources in relation to Vel Nari NDCs. The activity will also support greater engagement of provincial institutions and civil society organizations in this provincial MICS (Sub-activity 1.2.3).

## Sub-activity 1.1.2: Implement the REDD+ Safeguards Information System

Despite government effort, the safeguard information system (SIS) and the operationalization of SIS to improve good forest governance at provincial and district levels has not been fully achieved due to lack of budget and low coverage of relevant stakeholders. The project will support the development of a national SIS and its operationalization in provinces and districts by implementing a training program related to good forest governance of forests. This program will focus on (a) Developing a Market Access Strategy for Vietnam’s Forest Products, (b) Promoting gender equality and women’s rights through guidelines on promoting gender equity and rights of indigenous peoples in forest development, and (c) Conducting baseline studies on the status of forest tenure and land use rights in provinces and districts. The project will also develop tools to support the identification and mapping of forest tenure and land use rights, as well as biodiversity status for their forests, staff reporting on SIS.

## Sub-activity 1.1.3: Develop a regional action and knowledge management RECAP will support the REDD+ communication strategy in the target provinces, to assist in public dissemination of project details beyond the provincial level. The project will also organize workshops and training sessions for key stakeholders to raise awareness about the project activities and on the importance and benefits of forest conservation, on relevant policies, or different approaches to forest management and land use planning. RECAP will support the development of a strategy to adapt farming systems to this change. MARD will also organize interprovincial exchange workshops with IP communities in provinces bordering Cambodia and Laos to share experiences and lessons learned from using the same watershed to exchange lessons and facilitate coordinated planning across provincial boundaries. This activity will also include the dissemination of information on REDD+ related topics, including from and with other provinces, countries and non-governmental stakeholders.

### Activity 1.1.3.1: Develop mechanisms for NAP/RIP implementation and coordination and equitable distribution mechanism

It is crucial to ensure that the project activities are aligned with the NAP/RIP goals of avoiding biodiversity loss and promoting sustainable development decisions. These efforts are essential for achieving successful and sustainable emissions reductions and removals, while maintaining forest cover and biodiversity in Vietnam. The project will also support the implementation of the NAP/RIP by developing a national mechanism to ensure that all activities are aligned with the NAP/RIP goals.

### Activity 1.1.3.2: Prepare a Regional NRAP Investment plan

There is a need to coordinate the range of funding sources that should be aligned (but are currently not) to implement NAP/RIP objectives across the priority carbon sink areas in Vietnam, including REDD+ activities and other forest conservation projects.

### Activity 1.1.3.3: Develop a domestic VnFIP

The project will also establish and operationalize a domestic Viet Nam Forest and Business Partnership (VnFIP) as a public-private platform to support the development of private sector investment in REDD+ activities, including afforestation/reforestation at national movement on the plantation of 1 billion trees. This activity will also support the Government to develop its legal and regulatory framework for VnFIP, including the establishment of a forest carbon market, as well as the development of a mechanism to ensure that all activities are aligned with the NAP/RIP goals.

### Activity 1.1.3.4: Conduct national multi-stakeholder policy dialogue on including carbon sequestration services in the VnFIP

The project will conduct a series of national multi-stakeholder policy dialogues, which will include performance-based measures of stewardship, holds great potential for reform to activate domestic incentives to promote forest conservation and sustainable management. The project will also support the development of carbon sequestration and removal services, and the contracting of households for panning under so-called “joint forest management” arrangements. This activity will also support the development of a national framework for performance. A series of national policy dialogues will be organized on the various facets of establishing carbon-related activities in Vietnam, including the development of a legal and regulatory framework to ensure that all activities are aligned with the NAP/RIP goals.

### Activity 1.1.3.5: Develop a National REDD+ Investment Plan

The project will also develop a national REDD+ investment plan (NRP) for the implementation of REDD+ activities in Vietnam, including afforestation/reforestation and other forest conservation projects. The NRP will be developed with the involvement of relevant stakeholders at all levels, including local communities.

## B

### Output 1.2: Provincial policies, legal and institutional frameworks, and stakeholder coordination mechanisms for the National Adaptation Plan (NAP)

#### Activity 1.2.1: Mainstream NAP into provincial policies and legal institutional frameworks

While Provincial REDD+ action plans (PRAPs) have been completed, systematic integration of the NAP in SEOP’s PRAPs is still pending. The PRAPs are considered as a key document for the work plan and budget allocation, and they provide the basis for the work planning and budgeting process. However, the PRAPs do not include specific actions on forest landscape restoration (FLR) or forest sector development plans (FSDPs) and programmes for ethnic minorities and mountainous areas need to be included in the PRAPs.

If they are to be funded and implemented at all levels of government, it is important that the PRAPs are updated with high-level managers in the provincial community-based organizations, people’s councils and line departments as well as FLR focal points. The PRAPs should also be integrated into the NAP, which will be developed by the national political- and operational step for implementation.

#### Sub-activity 1.2.1.1: Support the development of REDD+ action plans and their integration into sector plans

The PRAPs are considered as a key document for work planning and budget allocation and provincial REDD+ action plans (PRAP) and their integration in SEOP, and in the provincial sustainable forestry development plans (FSDPs) and programmes for ethnic minorities and mountainous areas need to be included in the PRAPs. The PRAPs should also be integrated into the NAP, which will be developed by the national political- and operational step for implementation.

#### Activity 1.2.2: Facilitate cross-sector coordination, policy dialogue and capacity building on zoning, FLA, FLM and land allocation

The project will work with provincial authorities to develop a zoning framework that provides effective land allocation, to address complicated dynamics of forest land being used by local villagers. It is estimated that this area alone could be 100,000 ha in the provinces of Loei, Phitsanulok and Chiang Rai, and up to 70,000-80,000 ha in Chaiyaphum. There is political will to embark on a process of land allocation for these areas to individual households, including small forest-dependent communities, indigenous and ethnic minorities, and smaller farmers.

The establishment of provincial zoning frameworks will be based on the principles of FLA and FLM, which are designed to support the successful development and implementation of strategies and action plans related to deforestation-free value chains and other land uses. The project will work with provincial authorities to develop a zoning framework that provides effective land allocation, to address complicated dynamics of forest land being used by local villagers.

## Activity 1.2.3: Enhance Public-Private-Producer Partnerships (4Ps) for deforestation-free economic development (the engagement with the stakeholders involved in the 4Ps is detailed in Annex B)

This activity will build on the existing 4Ps platforms to provide a more structured approach, directly involved in project activities and beyond. The 4Ps platforms will be required to plan or provide inputs to the planning of all these actions, including the development of deforestation-free value chains, as well as to monitor and evaluate their performance against agreed indicators.

### Sub-activity 1.2.3.1: Enhance knowledge: Participatory baseline studies to refine the understanding of drivers of deforestation and how to mitigate trade-offs between forest conservation and rural economic development will be conducted in collaboration with project partners, including local communities, indigenous peoples, and other stakeholders. The results of these studies will inform the design of the current status of formal and traditional forest management practices, including specific management of forest biodiversity and ecosystem services.

### Sub-activity 1.2.3.2: Identify socially and economically viable and dense agro-ecological systems adapted to agro-ecological zones The project begins by creating a typology of agro-ecological zones, taking into account socio-economic conditions, land use patterns, and forest cover. This will be done in collaboration with local communities, indigenous peoples, and other stakeholders.

### Sub-activity 1.2.3.3: Enhance knowledge with the wider population and advocacy To promote knowledge sharing and advocacy beyond the project participants, provincial forest and climate information hubs will be established at each FPO.

## Outcome 2: Emissions reductions from AFOLU consequently to 4Ps deforestation-free value chains & performance-based finance

### Activity 2.1.1: Develop deforestation-free commodity supply chains through 4P platforms The NRAP focuses on sustainable agriculture for export commodities like cocoa and pepper, promoting deforestation-free value chains. These are essential for successful REDD+ implementation. Developing deforestation-free value chains is vital to improve the livelihoods and resilience of marginalized ethnic minority groups and discourage further encroachment.

## Subactivity 3.6.1.5 Develop deforestation-free commodity supply chains (perennial crops, acacia-based timber, bamboo): At the district level, 4P platforms will create and execute action plans to develop deforestation-free commodity supply chains for perennial crops, acacia-based timber, and bamboo. The platforms will work with companies sourcing coffee, pepper, cashew, bamboo, and acacia in project districts, and work with farmers and processors to ensure that the commodities are sourced from areas where there is no deforestation. In the Vietnamese cocoa sector, there are very few purchase direct purchases by processor-exporters from farmers. Direct sales of cocoa beans from smallholder farmers to local cooperatives or processing companies are more common in the sector. It has also resulted in a value-chain where beans are sorted by quality just after leaving the farm-gate and sold at different prices depending on their quality. This has led to an increase in deforestation in the sector, which will guide multistakeholder dialogue on reducing emissions, sharing responsibilities, and mobilizing resources. Distribution of coffee is also dominated by large companies that source from smallholders.

For precommercial 4P platforms will adopt a jurisdictional approach, ensuring deforestation-free sourcing, reducing losses, and promoting agroforestry systems. Farmer training, traceability systems, and collaboration with local governments are key to the success of this approach in the coffee sector. In the cocoa sector, the 4P platform in Bac Duong district, Lam Dong in the coffee sector and expand to other communes.

Specialized action plans will be developed for each commodity to address climate change and enriching livelihoods. Different priorities will focus on specific crops, diversifying income and reducing risks. For example, in Ninh Thuan province, 4P platforms will develop a plan that includes the development of agroforestry systems based on coffee-based systems combined with pepper, fruits, and others, including macadamia, persimmon, and indigenous forest.

Insects, Cattle, and biodiversity systems, depending on shade tree species and density, can increase carbon storage from 5.8 to 10.4 tons/ha. Incorporating nitrogen-fixing trees can reduce greenhouse gas emissions by reducing the need for chemical fertilizers. The project will support companies in developing value chains for these products.

The project will also assist companies with emissions monitoring, traceability systems, and certification programs. It will support fair contracts with non-deforestation clauses, ensuring income stability for farmers and enabling knowledge sharing.

Sub-activity 2.1.1.2: Develop value chains for deforestation-free niche products (honey, mushrooms, medicinal plants, etc.) by engaging smallholder farmers, community groups, cooperatives, and SMEs. Farmers, CGIAs established under activity 2.1.1.2, and cooperatives will develop value-added activities, including serving as intermediaries between producers and buyers. The project will support CGIAs in developing products currently offered on forestland cultivation. Building on FAO's experience in supporting 49 agroforestry projects in Africa, CGIAs can help farmers diversify their income sources by targeting minority and poor communities. Potential options include beekeeping, mushroom cultivation, herbs and medicinal plants such as neem, and honey production. These products are currently sold to local markets but could be introduced into peri-urban crop systems, such as processed fruits and macadamia nuts, are also viable. These products can be marketed through existing value chains or new ones established by CGIAs. Farmers will benefit from related capacity building and promotion. Additionally, the project will adopt a holistic approach to address the challenges of deforestation-free production, including promoting agroforestry, organic and sustainable agriculture, identifying and prioritizing climate-resilient, nutritious foods, and improving their availability and affordability.

Activity 2.1.2: Develop the capacity of farmers, common interest groups (CGIs), cooperatives and small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) for deforestation-free value chains and alternative livelihood development CGIAs will be established to provide technical assistance and training in agroforestry practices and income. They will actively participate in districts-level 4P platforms and establish connections with buyers and financial service providers. CGIAs will also play a crucial role in farmer capacity building, establishing partnerships between farmers and buyers, and providing information on market opportunities. CGIAs represent farmers within the AP platforms. CGIAs will establish connections with financial service providers. RECA will provide technical assistance for CGIA establishment and capacity building. CGIAs will be established under the Empower of Ethnic Minorities (SEM) by Dak Nong and Agriculture, Farmers and Rural Areas Support Project (TRG). CGIAs will be established in each commune to represent farmers within AP platforms. CGIAs will represent farmers within the AP platforms. They will play a crucial role in farmer capacity building, establishing partnerships between farmers and buyers, and providing information on market opportunities.

Sub-activity 2.1.2.2: Train farmers, CGIAs, cooperatives and SMEs. Based on the training curricula developed under Activity 2.1.1., comprehensive training programs will be implemented for farmers, Community Interest Groups (CGIs), Cooperatives, and Small and Medium-sized Enterprises (SMEs). The project will use a Farmer-to-Farmer (F2F), and ICT-based platforms like YouTube and Facebook. The training aims to enhance their knowledge and skills, cater to their specific needs, and promote sustainable practices throughout the value chain.

Activities 2.1.3: Support financial providers to develop and promote financial products for deforestation-free value chain development

This activity will integrate REDD+ into relevant rural finance markets, investments in the AFOLU sector, such as agroforestry, and other forest-based enterprises (FBEs) that are not yet covered by existing financial services. Solutions to overcome barriers in the development of investment loans that are accessible to poor and smallholder farmers and women entrepreneurs will be developed. These solutions include: (i) a credit enhancement model that match smaller cash flows with finance; (ii) credit partnership models whereby the afters and/or input provider is a partner in the loan, which can provide additional collateral or reduce the risk of default; (iii) a “loan pool” or “baskets” model where lending goes directly to upstream suppliers and on-lend down the supply chain; and (iv) more flexible collateral requirements that allow access to credit for those who do not have traditional collateral. The solutions will be developed in four phases, based on best practices from past FIP projects in the region. The remaining solution will emerge and be tested during phase 4.

RECAF will build on the successes and lessons learnt of the microfinance services provided by the WDF, through savings and credit groups supported by the Women’s Union (WU) since 2012. Since 2013, IFAD has supported professionalization of these groups in order to provide more formalized financial services to their members. And they are still operating successfully and providing growing access to collateral-free and alternative collateral financing for women farmers. The WDF is currently being supported by the Cooperatives Development Project (CDP) implemented by the Cooperatives Union of Kenya (CUK), which have recently been designed to provide bridging loans in order to cover costs before they can apply for a loan from the CDP. These are different from the COCO project, which has developed a cooperative model that produces and markets agricultural products.

Sub-activity 2.1.3.1: Strengthen WDFs and CDFs to manage funds and develop products that support investments in deforestation-free value chains

A study will be conducted on the operational efficiency, regulatory context, and management of the WDFs and CDFs supported by the CUK. The study will also assess the capacity of the WDFs and CDFs to manage their funds and operationalizing the activities of the WDFs and CDFs for lending to RECAF smallholders. Agribank and other financial institutions will be supported to set up joint banks in collaboration with the FUI or WU acting as agent, expanding their existing pilots.

Sub-activity 2.1.3.2: Build capacity of commercial banks to develop products, partnerships and application of collateral requirements for deforestation-free value chain development

Commercial banks will be invited to participate in the 4P platform, supported by the State Bank of Vientiane (SBN) Central Bank (Vientiane). The banks will be provided with training on the business models and capacity of CCOs, cooperatives, and SMEs involved in deforestation-free value chains. This will include training to enhance their understanding of the business models and capacity of CCOs, cooperatives, and SMEs involved in deforestation-free value chains. The banks will be provided with training on the development of financial management strategies, and loan appraisal skills for providing collateral-free and alternative collateral financing and supporting investments in deforestation-free value chains. This will include training to enhance their understanding of the business models and capacity of CCOs, cooperatives, and SMEs involved in deforestation-free value chains. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 22

## Output 2: De-forestation-free value chains and forest restoration infrastructure upgraded and established.

### Activity 2.2.1: Develop infrastructure for de-forestation free value chains

In accordance with the new GCF policy of Vietnam (Decree 14/2016/QD-CP on management and use of ODA and concessional loans of foreign donors), the allocation of ILO loan financing will be exclusively directed towards supporting the development of infrastructure for de-forestation-free value chains. The project activities under this output, the investments will be driven by demand and prioritization, determined through the district and commune socio-economic development plans (SEDPs) and the provincial forest management plans (PFMPs). It is crucial that these investments adhere to a set of eligibility criteria, particularly pertaining to de-forestation-free practices and their implementation. The project activities will be implemented in close collaboration with relevant stakeholders and partners. Furthermore, the investments must be technically feasible and incorporate viable operation and maintenance strategies. The project activities are described below under Sub-activities 2.2.1.1 and 2.2.1.2.

*Project Implementation Manual* (PIM), section IV, Component 2, table X. Their focus will be on targeting investments in the following areas:

### Sub-activity 2.2.1.1: Promote sustainable agroforestry through small-scale and micro infrastructure investments

This sub-activity aims to promote sustainable agroforestry practices by investing in small-scale and micro irrigation systems, including small-scale lined irrigation canals and/or piped irrigation systems (upgraded from earth mounds) for production areas or linkages between production areas and water sources. The project investments include Small-scaled rural roads (upgraded from earth mounds to paved roads), drainage systems, and agroforestry infrastructure such as windbreaks, shelterbelts, and other forms of soil conservation structures (draught, storm, etc.). Pest monitoring systems for agricultural production; Ecotourism infrastructures and facilities; and the establishment of alternative business opportunities. These investments will enable farmers to adopt de-forestation-free practices and enhance the productivity of their agricultural activities while supporting the sustainable use of natural resources and the promotion of environmentally friendly farming methods.

### Sub-activity 2.2.1.2: Enhance the post-harvest facilities of selected value chains by incorporating value-added processing technologies

This sub-activity aims to improve the quality of agricultural produce through maximum utilization of agricultural waste, and improve the overall quality of their goods. Moreover, the establishment of alternative business opportunities will be supported in order to generate additional income-generating opportunities for local communities and promote sustainable tourism facilities.

### Activity 2.2.2: Invest in forest restoration and rehabilitation infrastructure (PFO Ad CAF)

RECAF will also invest in essential forest protection and rehabilitation infrastructure, with a focus on enhancing forest management systems through the planting of fast-growing tree varieties, and implementing other crucial forest protection activities. These actions include establishing of forest buffer zones at an appropriate scale for forest fire and illegal encroachment, the creation of fire breaks, and the provision of forest fire water points, among others.

B

**Sub-activity 2.2.1. Strengthen forest monitoring and protection infrastructure by implementing comprehensive forest fire management systems:**

* **Action under Output:** Implement a forest fire detection system to improve early detection and warning of forest fire and illegal activities, and bolstering the capacity of forest rangers and other forest users to detect and respond to forest fires.
* **Output 2.1:** Conduct livelihood assessments needed through participatory methods.

**Sub-activity 2.2.2. Establish public nurseries dedicated to tree species cultivation, creating essential infrastructure for reforestation and afforestation initiatives:**
These nurseries will serve as vital centers for propagating native tree species, providing seedlings and saplings, and training forest users on the use of tools and equipment; these establishments will support biodiversity conservation, ecosystem restoration, and the development of sustainable forestry practices.

**Output 2.3:** Collaborative forest management capacity enhanced through performance-based incentives.

* **Action under Output:**
    * Develop a results-based payment for ecosystem services (PES) mechanism for collaborative forest management.
    * Establish Payment for Ecosystem Services (PES) systems in collaboration with Vietnam by adapting its current legislation. PES, although successful in promoting forest conservation and sustainable land use in other countries, has not yet been widely adopted in Vietnam due to market-driven, and information process that aligns with international frameworks like REDD+. Key improvements include the development of a robust monitoring system, improved data collection methods for greater accuracy, and better information flow among stakeholders. An extensive analysis of Action 2.3.1 is available in Annex 20.

* **Sub-activity 2.3.1. Support Provincial Forest Protection Development Funds (PFPDFs) to design and implement a mechanism for performance-based payments for carbon sequestration services:**
Based on the national experience with PES, the PFPDFs will not only encompass forest areas but also extend to non-forest areas, hence referred to as PES instead of PFF. The PFPDFs will be established under the Forest Protection Fund (FPF), which will operate as a sub-fund under the Provincial Forest Protection Development Funds (PFPDFs). In its first phase, the PFPDFs will focus on developing and implementing a mechanism for performance-based payments to generate long-term funding resources. Lessons and insights from successful projects like the IFAD funded 3 PAF projects in Vietnam can be applied here. The PFPDFs will be established under the Forest Protection Fund (FPF) in Son La province by the Sustainable Natural Resource Management Project (SNMRP) by JICA.

* **Sub-activity 2.3.1.2 Build the capacity building of the PFPDFs:**
RECAF will assist IFAD in PFPDFS in managing the IFF and establishing an administrative unit to a trust fund that mobilizes funding and connects buyers and sellers of environmental services.

* **Sub-activity 2.3.2 Support multi-stakeholder collaboration on collaborative forest management (MCFCM):**
RECAF will support the development of the MCFCM group in developing and implementing effective forest management plans and efficient forest monitoring systems to facilitate collaborative zoning, FLA, and SFM practices, including the establishment of participatory committees, CPAs, and other mechanisms. The capacity building activity by capacity development program will be implemented to strengthen the MCFCM support system by providing training.

#### B

To communities and MCFM support staff from forest protection and extension departments, FMBS, SFEs, and relevant NGOs:

**Sub-activity 2.3.2.1. Facilitate a participatory land use planning and mapping process:** A participatory land-use planning exercise will be conducted (as part of the project implementation process) in each community prior to the commencement of any other project activity. FPIC will be obtained as necessary. This planning will identify forest areas that can be used for sustainable livelihoods, including agroforestry systems and other areas on which agroforestry systems can be developed. This will also generate ideas for the development of diversified livelihood activities in each community. The land-use plan will include information about the existing land category or value addition to sustainably harvested WFPB.

**Sub-activity 2.3.2.2. Develop a MCFM pilot:** For the selected communities for MCFM, a FLA will be provided to communities or adjustment of previous FLA will be supported following guidelines developed under Activity 1.1.2., so that the project can support the development and implementation of a community-managed forest management plan. The MCFM management structures will be established and/or strengthened and the project will support the community in developing a forest management plan (FMP) for each FLA. Communities will also be supported to establish nurseries established under Activity 2.2.2 so that they can deliver the necessary seedlings of selected tree species. For example, communities will be able to produce seedlings of trees such as Eucalyptus and Acacia. The seedlings of these trees will be planted, as well as organic management which would open the possibility of introducing beekeeping in certain areas. Communities will also be supported to establish nurseries for other tree species that are rich in nutritious nitrogen fixing species (e.g., pigeon pea and other beans) and multipurpose trees, which will control soil erosion and provide firewood and other forest products as reference. Within this IPF, the REGEAF will also support MCFM pilots to submit proposals for PES payments.

Based on the capacity of village-level MCFM groups, a training program will be designed and executed. Capacity building activities will include: (i) awareness raising; (ii) development of forest management plans; (iii) project plan, administrative processes and accounting; and (iv) technical on forest species monitoring forest health, agroforestry systems, soil fertility, and other related topics. The training program will be designed to address among ethnic groups the focus of the forest, NTFVPs and sustainable forest management. Livelihood improvement will be supported through capacity building in areas such as beekeeping, apiculture, and other income generating activities.

**Activity 2.3.1.2:** To avoid dependency of local community on project support for MCFM development, the project will focus on building the capacity of communities to manage their forests independently. A network will be established and maintained for learning and networking among community members to help follow-up after training.

**Sub-activity 2.3.2.4. Strengthen the MCFM support system:** To support smooth implementation of the MCFM project, a strong support system is required. The project will work with key stakeholders including representatives of key government agencies, FMBS and SFES to determine priorities for strengthening MCFM support systems.
