B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1000 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

54. RECAF implementation arrangements follow the Government Decree No.14/2021 on Q&A management in Viet Nam and the proven structure used at all IFAD-funded projects in the country. It takes on board a number of additional functions in order to respond to the specificities of the project, including risk management, policy engagement, capacity building, stakeholder linkages and partnerships, M&E, etc.

The Project Implementation Manual (PIM) describes detailed coordination and implementation arrangements under the project

(figure 4)

Figure 4: Project Implementation Arrangements


55. Project coordination and lead implementing agencies. The Government of Viet Nam has designated the DOF under MAKD as the Project Coordination Agency, and the Province People Committees (PPCs) of Gia Lai, Dak Lak, Dak Nong, Kon Tum, and Lam Dong provinces as the Lead Implementing Agencies.

The PPCs are responsible for project implementation in their respective provinces. The DOF and PPCs are accountable for the project execution. The PSC will establish a Project Steering Committee (PSC) to oversee the project implementation process. The PSC will be chaired by the Deputy Chairperson of MAKD, with members from the DOF, PPCs, and other relevant agencies. The PSC will ensure coordination and integration of the project with all national target programmes (NTPPs) and development partners.

56. Each provincial PPC will be led by the PPC Chairperson or the Deputy Chairperson and will be composed of the following members: the PPC Chairperson or Deputy Chairperson, representatives from the DOF, local government, development partners, and private sector entities which are party to the RECAF. The PSC provides the strategic direction for project implementation in each province. It is responsible for approving the annual work plan (AWP), mobilizes adequate and timely finance for the AWP, monitors the progress and reporting its results.

The PPC will be responsible for implementing the project activities as per the approved AWP and ensuring that the project activities are implemented within the time frame specified in the AWP. The PPC will also ensure that the project activities are aligned with the NTPPs of each province. In addition, as per standard practice project implementation, IFAD provides an advisory role to the PPCs on project planning, monitoring, and evaluation (M&E) and financial management.

The PSC will review the work plan and budget (AWP). IFAD provides final no objection to the AWP after it has been validated by PSC. Decisions made at the PPC level are subject to approval by the PSC. The PPCs will be responsible for approving resolutions, PSC decisions are informed by reports and recommendations from the Project Management Unit (PMU) and other relevant agencies. The PPCs will also be responsible for ensuring that project activities lead to interdisciplinary solutions, and make related decisions.

These meetings aim to address project challenges and consider options for addressing them. In cases where a meeting is not feasible, the PPC leadership will circulate documents and information to all PPC members in advance of the meeting. The PPC Chairperson or Deputy Chairperson chairs this PPC, and therefore has the final approval authority over any decision adopted by the provincial PPC.

57. Project Management Unit (PMU) and Sub-PMUs A PMU will be established at national level to assist DOF in the project coordination and day-to-day implementation. The PMU will mobilize technical support from DOF and the

### Five Sub-PMUs will be established at provincial level and report directly to the PPC in each province.

The Sub-PMU will be responsible for the day-to-day management of the RECAP implementation at the province level, including:

1. Ensuring the coherence of the implementation strategy towards the expected outputs, outcomes and impacts.
2. Ensuring that project activities are implemented according to the approved project document and manage project finance from the various sources.

# B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

## B.5.1 Veit Nam's updated NDC of 2022 contains an additional emission reduction contribution in LULUCF sectors of about 30 million tonnes CO2 equivalent by 2030 compared to the baseline scenario.

### B.5.2 The GCF seeks to contribute almost half (8.6 MCO2e) of the conditional LULUCF emission reductions by 2030. As a key component of the NDC, this is in line with the Government's commitment under REDD+ Action Plan 2017 for the Central Highlands provinces of Gia Lai, Dak Lak, Dak Nong, and Lam Dong, and the planned implementation of the National Forest Landscape Restoration Strategy (NFRS) to reduce deforestation and forest degradation over past decades, despite the logging ban of 2016. challenges persist in resisting and stabilizing these efforts, including weak institutional capacity, ongoing illegal logging, forging cross-sectoral commitments, building deforestation-free agricultural production and forest rehabilitation.

### B.5.3 As stipulated in the UNFCCC COP decision BCP-19, the GCF is one of the main sources through which financing REDD+ projects will be provided to developing countries. The success of this project as a model for large-scale investment will be a critical component to domestic financing allocated for REDD+ and to support implementation of the NFRS. This project will also serve as an important demonstration of how the GCF can provide catalytic funding for institutionalizing much needed inter-sectoral and multi-stakeholder approaches to reform forest management practices in Vietnam, while providing a concrete example of how the GCF can help address some of the fundamentals of an enabling environment for NRPAP implementation as presented in this proposal.

### B.5.4 Given that many ethnic minority groups are among those most vulnerable to climate change and that their communities are weakest: local ethnic minority communities lack access to public and private funds, and productive infrastructure deficits impede the inclusion of ethnic minority groups and other smallholders in poverty-reducing agricultural and forest.


---
**Page 32**
---

supply costs for their adoption of sustainable production systems and practices. GCF will finance the technical assistance and capacity building required to support the successful implementation of reforms; for mainstreaming REDD+ in Vietnam’s agricultural sector, it is essential that these reforms are designed to promote agroforestry practices to be extended to poor small producers, the development of local niche products that add value to their production systems, and the creation of a market for these products. GCF will finance efforts to design and offer long term credit products and leverage additional financing needs for investments in agroforestry.

72. GCF financing will also leverage government/policy and public investment planning mechanisms to fully mainstream REDD+ relevant policies and leverage critical public infrastructure investments and co-financing to achieve net zero deforestation. This includes the development of a national forest sector strategy, which will guide commodity production and waste conservation and protection of forest resources. GCF resources, in association with additional funding from other sources, will be used to support the implementation of these reforms jointly create the opportunity to average private sector financing from entrepreneurs, public sector entities, and foreign investors. The proposed reforms are expected to contribute significantly to Vietnam’s efforts for national climate change mitigation and adaptation priorities by: bringing strong public-private producer partnerships for alternative livelihoods; increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers in the face of climate change impacts; and providing a sustainable supply chain for agroforestry products, which would otherwise be lost if they were operating separately. RECAF will influence the current VNP3’s FPE to direct it towards more outcomes consistent with REDD+ goals and GCF priorities. The proposed reforms will also contribute to forest/forestland protection and conservation, which can be addressed through adoption of performance based PSF/PSPS systems, as well as by providing additional funding for forest restoration and reforestation.

73. There is a clear market failure that provides a barrier for the transition from monocultures (annual and perennial crops as well as forestry plantations) to dense agroforestry systems. While agroforestry systems are better adapted to climate change, they require more capital investment than conventional systems, which often have higher yields per hectare in crop combinations. Furthermore, farmers are hesitant to plant new species for which there are no known buyers, but these would be the most appropriate crops for a dense agroforestry system. To overcome this barrier and make these agroforestry systems meet investments with an extended product horizon, farmers need corresponding long-term financial services that can provide them with access to credit and other forms of financing. These services not only due to perceived credit risk (associated by the loan tier) but are also unable in some cases to provide loans for longer than 12 months. The lack of such services is a major barrier to the adoption of agroforestry systems, which are already working with the project targeting groups of ethnic minorities, smallerholder farmers and rural SMEs. This is particularly true when these groups have limited access to financial services or are not aware of existing interventions. In addition, financial service providers lack knowledge about the scale flow of these multiple cropping systems and their potential for increasing productivity and resilience in the face of climate change impacts, including climate change and market risks, nor are they able to structure the loans accordingly, integrating value chains and other relevant services into the financing package.

74. The IFAD’s internal funds allocation mechanism does not permit the allocation of grants or other highly concessional loans to this project. This is because the project will be implemented in a country that has already received substantial funding from IFAD for similar projects.

75. To kick-start the transition to deforestation-free agroforestry systems, selected financial service providers will provide credit and other forms of financing to farmers who are willing to adopt these more sustainable and productive alternative. The successful introduction of macadamia nuts into coffee plantations in Lam Dong province has demonstrated that it is possible for smallholder farmers to successfully transition from monocultures to agroforestry systems. This project will enhance the capacity of relevant institutions and commercial banks in understanding sustainable farming systems, including agroforestry systems, and how they can be integrated with existing financial services. It will also provide literacy and business support services to farmers investing in these systems, thereby mitigating risks for financial service providers.

76. Viet Nam, as a low-middle income country (LMIC), faces challenges in accessing concessional finance due to its status. This has prompted the country to explore alternative sources of capital. At the same time, the decentralized payment system in Vietnam is not conducive to the implementation of concessional loans. In response, the Government has implemented new ODA policies that impose strict limits on loan investments. These measures are aimed at ensuring that foreign aid does not contribute to the country’s debt burden and that it is used for projects that will generate economic benefits. The current policy framework in Vietnam also requires that all loans be repaid within 10 years, which means that the provincial governments from unnecessary debt repayment burdens. As a result, loans are now primarily restricted to infrastructure development and other large-scale projects. This has limited the scope of concessional finance for agro forestry. However, a combination of grant and infrastructural financing is necessary to achieve project objectives. The proposed project will provide full agricultural infrastructure support to achieve project objectives. In this regard, the Government of Viet Nam, seeking a grant of USD 35 million from the GCF. This grant, combined with FAZ’s concessional infrastructure loan, presents a competing platform for Viet Nam to achieve the project objectives. The grant will help reduce GHG

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 29

B

### RECAF recognizes the long-term financial and economic viability of transitioning to deforestation-free agroforestry systems (DFAS) through a combination of public investment, private sector engagement in non-bankable areas (i.e., enabling conditions under Outcome 1), and sub-national government support. However, these enabling conditions are not yet in place for most countries. The Government's current ODA pledges hinder the project's feasibility at a lower level of concessionality. To overcome these obstacles, RECAF proposes to leverage the GCF grant as a catalyst for scaling up DFAS through three main actions: (1) build capacity and institutional readiness; (2) mitigate information asymmetry among financial institutions, tackle policy complexity and institutional challenges related to land tenure and environmental regulations; and (3) provide technical assistance to sub-national governments. RECAF's focus on these enabling conditions plays a crucial role in overcoming these barriers and ensuring successful project implementation. RECAF’s loan portfolio will be structured as concessional loans with a 10-year grace period, 25-year maturity, and a 4% interest rate. This model is expected to provide the necessary financial incentives for private sector engagement.

### From a broader perspective, RECAF aims to contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by promoting sustainable land management practices that are climate-resilient, robust, capacity-building, and the realization of significant environmental and social benefits. Capacity development in human resources is essential for the successful implementation of RECAF’s activities. The project will leverage the value chain and NDC missions to unlock private and public investments, enabling the replication and scaling up of a low-emission agricultural model.

### In addition to the GCF grant, the project aims to unlock other sources of funding:

- An additional USD 32.5 million co-financing from IAF (scheduled for approval at the September 2024 IFAD Executive Board meeting) will allow smooth post approval processes and the release of funds.
- It will unlock USD 28.6 million in Government in-kind and grant co-financing. The in-kind Government contribution includes land, infrastructure, and equipment, and the grant co-financing covers the direct cost of various capacity building design, and O&M of the project. The grant co-financing covers the direct cost of various capacity building on O&M for infrastructure works under Output 2.2.
- In addition to the GCF funding through RECAF, with an additional USD 10 million in co-financing from the Sustainable Development Policy (SDP) mechanism (Activity 1.2) and 4 platform (Activity 1.2), including the national target programs (NTPs).
- The Co-finance will be provided by the World Bank.

### Furthermore, USD 8.5 million is expected to be unlocked from project beneficiaries in parallel financing, mainly through infrastructure investments and agroforestry plots, while about USD 7 million will come from direct private sector investment. This co-financing is critical for RECAF’s success as it provides a financial buffer that is important to RECAF as private investments are long-term and involve many smallholders. One of the critical barriers to scaling up RECAF’s activities is the lack of sufficient external funding, which means that internal funds will not be sufficient, and external finance will be required. Since the PAF was first developed, legislative changes in ODA have made it more difficult for RECAF to access concessional financing from multilateral development banks (MDBs). However, other external financing opportunities have arisen, particularly green credit lines channelled through larger rural banks that are willing to provide loans at a lower interest rate. The project’s ability to unlock private sector finance is “green”.

### During the implementation of RECAF, around USD 70 million is expected to be received from financial institutions for financing, including through WDFs and CDFS. Additional co-financing such as LEAF, the World Bank, and other MDBs will also contribute to this amount. The project aims to transfer USD 15.5 million of CO2 emissions reduction from forests in the South Central region and Central Highlands for the 2023-2028 period. LEAF/Emagreen shall pay a minimum price of USD 15 per ton of CO2.

### The coaxed green credit from Vietnam Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (VIBARD), VND 12,000 million for low carbon emission agroforestry systems, VND 3,000 million for climate-resilient agriculture, VND 5,000 million for climate adaptation agriculture, etc., and Loan from PGBank: VND 8,000 million for low carbon emission, climate change adaptation agriculture, and green energy.

B. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

79. RECAF’s exit strategy is based on the following pillars: (i) building of an enabling policy and regulatory environment; (ii) establishment of a long-term institutional framework for forest management and financing arrangements; (iii) knowledge sharing and capacity building; and (iv) long-term land stewardship financing and building the financial feasibility of forest-based enterprises. The exit strategy will be based on the following pillars: (i) engagement on a market basis with financial institutions for the provision of credit for investment in both fixed assets (primarily infrastructure) and working capital, including the development of a revolving fund to finance the FIs that smallholders and their deforestation-free value chains make good business sense are profitable; and have the potential to be scaled up. The exit strategy will also include the establishment of a long-term forest-based growing element for finance in the future. If possible, RECAF will work to convert carbon finance into consumer finance. (ii) engagement on a market basis with financial institutions for the provision of credit for investment in development pathway delivery will continue after project closure.

(ii) Enabling policy and regulatory framework: At the provincial level, building an enabling environment will start with inter-ministerial coordination meetings to develop provincial plans, which will ensure that provincial governments have reserved sufficient land for forest conservation and sustainable use. The provincial plans will include socio-economic development and forestry activity developments. At the national level, policy engagement on community forest management and financing arrangements will be strengthened through the establishment of a long-term framework for expansion and of the pilots established in the project provinces and expansion to other provinces in the country.

(ii) Rural institutions: At community level, RECAF will establish or strengthen various local and grassroots rural institutions. These include village savings and credit associations (VSCAs), women’s groups, youth groups, and ethnic minorities, which will empower local communities to continue to invest in trees and climate change adaptation activities. The project will also support the establishment of a community forest management structure. Management structures will be established in priority sites based on consultation with representatives of ethnic minorities and other stakeholders. These structures will have the mandate to manage forests for the long-term management of forestland. In the same and other villages, commodity or product-oriented CGIAs will be established by RECAF. The CGIAs will be responsible for the commercialization of their products and for the long-term management of forestland. In case CGIAs use collective land for their income-generating activities, they will need to agree on the use of land with the local community forest management structure or other relevant stakeholders.

After project closure, they will be able to represent the small producers in coordination mechanisms with government and to continue to benefit from the financial services provided by RECAF. CGIAs will also be responsible for their deforestation-free business, providing income to their members. Financial services providers’ capacity will be built through training and technical assistance on credit risk management, financial literacy, and climate change mitigation. Specifically, the CGIAs will continue to finance cooperatives producing specialty products, and WCFs will continue to provide financing for smallholder farmers.

(ii) Coordination mechanisms: Collaboration during the project implementation through the 4P platforms at provincial level will be continued after project closure. Specific exit strategy will be designed with 4P partners at the start up. Likewise, personal relationships and trust developed between RECAF and CGIAs, WCFs, and other stakeholders involved in the project’s activities of work, will facilitate coordination into the future. These arrangements have been proven during project design stage and implementation phase that CGIAs, WCFs, and other stakeholders involved in the project are already worked together in previous value chain development projects. The commodity or product 4P platforms at district level will be continued after project closure to ensure that CGIAs continue to benefit from the 4P platforms for continued action after project closure. The project will also facilitate agreements between farmers, CGIAs, WCFs, and other stakeholders involved in the project’s activities of work on the commercialization becoming profitable only in the long run. As much as possible, such commercial collaboration and partnerships shall also be supported by RECAF.

(iv) Knowledge sharing and capacity building: RECAF will maintain and share knowledge on several strategic technical areas that will be a basis for continued action after project closure as knowledge will be updated in project implementation phase. The knowledge will not only inform immediate project activities, but will also generate insights that will still be relevant to the CGIAs and other stakeholders involved in the project’s activities of work. RECAF will identify the most viable tree combinations for specific agro-ecological zones and develop replication guidelines. Lessons from


---
**Page 35**
---

project goals on community forest management and PFES/PES and bankable business models will be compiled and disseminated to professionals, informing their future work. The project will also implement large training programs for communities in forest management, conservation, and sustainable use of natural resources. It will promote agroforestry and other good agricultural practices that reduce emissions and adapt to climate change and to increasing demand.

(v) Long-term land stewardship financing and building the financial feasibility and technical capability of small producers to pursue deforestation-free commodity production: As mentioned above, the PFES/PES redesign to compensate for forest degradation and loss will provide a new source of income for communities. This will help finance need, and help drive more benefits to local communities and forest users. Such performance-based financing mechanisms can be used as an incentive for smallholders to adopt sustainable land management practices that may stimulate or facilitate access to finance for further investments, starting a virtuous cycle.

(vi) Further strengthening of the deforestation-free value chain: Strengthening the deforestation-free value chain development and forest protection and restoration (Output 2.2) will strengthen these pillars by providing communities with new opportunities to diversify their livelihoods through the production of non-timber forest crops and related income streams (and the timing of harvest/vegetation), will bring increased agricultural and financial benefits, and will provide a more stable source of income for smallholders. This will allow farmers to diversify with fruit, nut or multi-purpose trees on their coffee farms, without suffering large impacts in other areas of their livelihoods. The project will also support the development of agroforestry systems that integrate fruit, nut or multi-purpose forest trees to increase and diversify smaller livelihoods, reduce exposure to commodity price volatility, and provide a more stable source of income for smallholders. This will allow farmers to diversify with fruit, nut or multi-purpose trees on their coffee farms, without suffering large impacts in other areas of their livelihoods.

To support deforestation-free value chains, the infrastructure investment component (Output 2.3) will focus on providing communities with access to improved and new forest-based facilities, value-added processing facilities, and agroforestry-focused ecotourism establishments. Additionally, RECAF will provide technical assistance for the development of community-based enterprises and establishing public nurseries. This infrastructure is essential for effective forest management, early detection of illegal deforestation, and sustainable land use.

To ensure the continuity of infrastructure operations and maintenance beyond the project's closure, an exit strategy will be developed to ensure that communities have the skills needed to manage this new infrastructure equipment. This will empower them with the skills needed to generate sufficient revenue from infrastructure facilities, such as nurseries, processing plants, and ecotourism establishments, to cover their operational costs, maintenance, upgrades, and potential expansion, enabling the sustainability of the infrastructure investment by incorporating it into the local economy. The project will also provide technical assistance for the development of community-based enterprises and establishing public nurseries.

To further consolidate such exit strategy, as per FAD guidelines, a comprehensive exit and sustainability strategy will be formulated at or before implementing its partners. The implementation will be reviewed and rated during each FAD review cycle. All relevant agreements and land-use commitments and land-use commitments and land-use agreements signed before project closure.

## 91. RECAF will mobilize financial resources from GCF, IFAD, and other donors to support the capacity building and technology development/tariff. GCF funding for capacity building and technology will be approximately 60% of the total GCF contribution.

### 92. For enabling environment (Outcome 1), RECAF will support provincial governments to mainstream the NRAP into their respective provincial development plans. RECAF will also provide technical assistance to provinces to coordinate REDD+ implementation and identify options for deforestation-free value chain development. Research, monitoring, and information systems will be established in order to generate required reports for results-based payment from different stakeholders.

### 93. For deforestation-free and climate resilient rural economic development (Outcome 2), value chain specific 4p platforms at district level will develop and implement value chain action plans with the objective to transform these value chains into sustainable, inclusive, and resilient ones. RECAF will provide technical assistance to provinces in developing and implementing these action plans. A provincial monitoring and information system will be established in order to generate required reports for results-based payment from different stakeholders.

### 94. Under the infrastructure investments, technology innovation/transfer will be promoted including investments in soil health management, agroforestry, and climate-resilient crop varieties. RECAF will provide technical assistance enabling farmers to better plan and manage their farms and react timely to weather events. GCF funds will be used to promote the development of a public nursery system for tree seedlings and other agricultural inputs. RECAF will facilitate developing participatory agroecology activities linked to agroforestry and specialty product production, including the establishment of demonstration plots and training on soil health management.

### 95. Finally, capacity building will be provided for the participatory land use planning and mapping process, which will inform actions for agroforestry development, identify options for specialty product production, and develop landscape management plan that will inform areas for agroforestry development, identify options for specialty product production, and develop landscape management plan. Capacity building will also be provided to subsequently implement these PES mechanisms and MCCFM plans.

The project will also deliver climate change adaptation co-benefits, with a particular focus on the beneficiaries belonging to ethnic minorities, such as the Mfinga, Ma, and Ede communities, who reside in forested areas or rely heavily on forests for their livelihoods. The project will provide training to women, youth, men, and older individuals (with a priority given to young people), and relatively less poor or land farmers who possess the necessary skills and resources to participate in climate change adaptation activities. The project will prioritize the inclusion of youth, women-headed households, and ethnic minority households within these target groups. The project will also provide training on gender equality and women’s empowerment as part of the Gender Action Plan indicating specific measures to encourage women's participation, aiming for a project target of 70% female participants.

Under Output 2.1, the project will provide training to farmers on the utilization of climate information and existing agroforestry practices in coffee plantations. The training will be based on the best available scientific knowledge, traditional environmental knowledge and practices of the ethnic minorities and the best available scientific knowledge. For instance, the project will introduce additional tree species in monocrop coffee plantations, transitioning to coffee-based agroforestry systems, and promoting the use of drought-resistant varieties to reduce crop loss and damage by climate-related factors.

Through Output 2.2, the project will train community members on climate-adapted forest management and agroforestry practices that are resilient to climate change impacts. The training will also cover land-use planning and management. They will also acquire knowledge about climate-adapted forest management and agroforestry practices that are resilient to climate change impacts.

Moreover, the project will provide infrastructure investment (Output 2.2) that enhances the beneficiaries' resilience to climate change. The selection criteria for infrastructure will consider adaptive measures to address observed and anticipated changes in rainfall patterns, temperature, and other climatic factors. This will be used to support deforestation-free value chains and MCFCM.

In addition, the project will reach 100,000 households with a 70% adoption rate, resulting in an estimated 140,000 farmers who will enjoy the Adaptation co-benefits. By prioritizing climate change adaptation efforts through savings and improvements.

### D.1.4 Economic co-benefits

The project will directly benefit women, youth, ethnic minorities, and men engaged in forest protection, agroforestry, and other land-use practices that are resilient to climate change impacts. The project will reach 100,000 smallholder farmers and forest-dependent community members, including ethnic minorities, the project will provide training on climate information and existing agroforestry practices in coffee plantations, and equitable resource distribution to enable their long-term investment in sustainable land use practices. Economic co-benefits include increased income from improved agricultural productivity, increased participation in deforestation-free value chains, enhanced livelihoods and capacity through MCFM and PES/FCS, and the adoption of technologies to reduce water usage and chemical fertilizer needs to reduce crop loss and input spending.

In addition, the project utilizes the EX-ACT tool to measure the impact on C stocks and (GHG) emissions. An estimated 12 million metric tons of CO₂ equivalent will be sequestered over a period of 30 years and financed through various sources, including national funds, the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF), and private sector funding. The project will also provide training on climate information and existing agroforestry practices in coffee plantations to reduce GHG emission reduction. The project may also consider using the Biodiversity Integrated Assessment and Conservation Strategy (BIACS) tool to assess the impact of the project activities on biodiversity, including its importance for ecosystem services.

D.2. Pariticipation shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will establish a paradigm shift through several pathways:

### 110. Flexibility
It will contribute to regulatory frameworks and policies that provide an enabling environment for the transition to sustainable forestland management, deforestation-free value chains and climate resilient livelihoods systems. The project will also support the development of performance-based mechanisms (PBM) for carbon credits as a basis for the spatial planning, work planning and budget to implement all social, environmental and economic activities in a coordinated manner at the national level. This will ensure that the project is aligned with the coordination at provincial, district and community level that will lead for a participatory land use plan for effective implementation of the project. The project will also support the development of performance-based mechanisms as opportunities for local communities and a fair sharing of costs and benefits. Similar systems have been used in other forest landscapes such as the Amazon, where they are known to be effective at promoting sustainable livelihoods, resilient and value chain SEEDS into normal planning and incorporating results of participatory research in existing land use plans. The project will also support the development of performance-based mechanisms for enduring land use disputes, which is a prerequisite for forest conservation, regeneration and sustainable agroforestry systems.

### 111. Secondarily
The jurisdictional approach to developing deforestation-free value chains is still in very early. It while be considered as an innovative approach that will provide a new paradigm shift from the traditional approach of deforestation experiences so far have mainly focused on single commodity (e.g., cocoa in Ivory Coast and Ghana, palm oil in Indonesia) or multiple commodities (e.g., soybeans in Brazil). The project will adopt this approach in coffee-based systems in Lam Dong facilitated by SNV and further develop the approach. The project will also support the development of performance-based mechanisms for carbon credits as a basis for planning during implementation phase. A participatory approach to planning and implementation will be considered.

### 112. Thirdly
The development of a performance-based mechanism for PES that includes payments for carbon credits, forest conservation and sustainable agroforestry systems is still in very early stage. This will open up an important potential for accessing international REDD+ financing and public and private 'carbon' markets. In combination with the above, the project will support the development of performance-based mechanisms for community-based forest management and PESFRESSES for carbon sequestration services and community forest management, for scaling out and replication purposes.

### 113. Fourthly
The development of capacity of financial service providers to provide financing for investments in agroforestry and MCFM to reduce deforestation and forest degradation while increasing the adaptive capacity and income of farmers is still in very early stage. The project will support the development of performance-based mechanisms for agriculture development.

### 114. Underscoring
The underlying principle of RECPA's paradigm shift is FAD facilitation to ensure full and effective participation of ethnic minorities during the planning and implementation process as the projects horizontal partners. The values of these communities are recognized in the project, which will be reflected in the development of sustainable initiatives that are environmentally sustainable and rooted in the cultures and value of the ethnic minorities. Participatory approach to planning and implementation will be consistently adopted and top-down approaches will be avoided.

### 115. Specifically
- Scaling up and replication: several approaches and activities can be scaled up and replicated, including:
    - The jurisdictional approach to developing deforestation-free value chain development,
    - The performance-based

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

## D

### Contribution to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)

#### 3.1 Environmental benefits

- **Transition from monocrop to agroecosystems:** The transition from monocrop to agroecosystems and the use of other sustainable agriculture practices such as contour planting and Contour Hedgerow Technology (CHT), will help reduce soil erosion, improve water infiltration, and increase biodiversity. This is supported by the Global Burden Model for Poverty Support (GLobO)20 metric, which measures the average abundance of natural capital in a country. The GLobO20 metric shows that both Natura and natural capital experienced a decline from 1994 to 1998, followed by a slower decrease until 2005. Since then, there has been an increase in both Natura and natural capital.

- **Reduction of deforestation:** A focus on reducing deforestation and forest degradation is expected to prevent MSA decline by strongly correlating with land use change (LUC) and the loss of biodiversity. The transition from monocrop to agroecosystems, combined with the planting of native tree species are anticipated to increase MSA. Additionally, supporting sustainable use of NWFPs and involving communities in forest management is expected to reduce deforestation and improve MSA.

- **Biodiversity benefits:** The transition from monocrop to agroecosystems will help increase biodiversity, specifically in terms of mean species abundance (MSA). The MSA metric, developed by Global Burden Model for Poverty Support (GLobO)20, measures the average abundance of natural capital in a country. The GLobO20 metric shows that both Natura and natural capital experienced a decline from 1994 to 1998, followed by a slower decrease until 2005. Since then, there has been an increase in both Natura and natural capital.

- **Soil analysis:** Soil analysis on better soil management, fertilization schedules and the use of nitrogen fixing species will help reduce soil erosion and improve water infiltration.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 39

## D. Social benefits

### D.3.1. Land tenure: As explained in section B2, sometimes land traditionally cultivated by ethnic minorities was documented as state-owned and then expropriated for large-scale development projects, leading to disputes that continue until today. At the provincial level, the project will facilitate coordination between departments and agencies at all levels of government to ensure that the land use planning process is consistent with the LPA documentation, including FPIC to be developed. At the commune level, participatory land use planning and mapping will be conducted in a manner consistent with the LPA documentation, ensuring that the rights of ethnic minorities are respected (e.g., continuity of ecosystem services), degraded forest for regeneration and sustainable harvesting of NFPs and development of community-based enterprises, and the protection of sacred sites. The project will also ensure that FPIC to communities or acquisition of previous FPIC is facilitated so that the legal ownership or use right status of ethnic minorities' land can be clarified.

### D.3.2. The resolution of land-use conflicts and the improvement of land tenure rights of ethnic minorities are key objectives of this project, which will be achieved through participatory land-use planning and mapping at all levels, following the logical framework. The project expects to conduct or adjut LPA 150 villages, covering a total of 55,000 ha., and benefitting approximately 237,000 people. This is an important step in the implementation of FPIC as it is a prerequisite for the implementation of the MCFM and performance-based FFPESPE activities; they also allow for the development of community enterprises and the protection of sacred sites. The project will facilitate the economy, enabling the signing of contract farming agreements with buyers of deforestation-free commodities and of necessary land-use planning and mapping to be conducted in a manner consistent with LPA documentation. Small farmers would also have no incentive to spend their labor and financial resources on such long-term investments.

### D.3.3. During the stakeholder consultation process, poorer households revealed that they cannot afford nutritious diets, their children are often malnourished, and their health is poor. The prevalence of stunting among ethnic minorities in Vietnam is high among the ethnic minorities in Viet Nam. The prevalence of stunting among ethnic minority children is 14%, compared to 5% for non-ethnic minority children. This project will have a positive impact on nutrition by improving access to nutritious foods and increasing the consumption of fruits, vegetables, and legumes. It will also have a positive result on dietary diversity among target groups through two different pathways. First, though better and more diverse diets, it will improve the nutritional status of ethnic minorities in Vietnam. Second, increased investments in irrigation and through better value chain linkages and the development of small cooperative businesses, it will increase the income of ethnic minority households. The project will also ensure that the current monocrop coffee or pepper and in the agroforestry systems to be planted on bare land and the use of beans as a food crop are not affected by this project. Instead, the project will focus on the selection of crops to select and evaluate proposed participatory research on relevant farming systems. Finally, forest conservation and improvement of livelihoods for ethnic minorities in Vietnam will be achieved through the promotion of sustainable agricultural practices that improve the nutritional status of ethnic minorities. However, it should be noted that the contribution of this project is such that it cannot be considered a nutrition project.

### D.3.4. Gender and Indigenous People's sensitive project design and implementation

The project promote gender equality - through (i) increased participation of women in project activities outside their home; (ii) improved access to information, education, training, and extension services for women on knowledge about market; (iii) improved loan access; (iv) increased female participation in household's decision making process; (v) increased understanding of the role of women in the family and community; (vi) increased understanding of household work in the part of male, and work arrangement based on seasons; and (vii) use of the above information to improve the design of project activities. The project will also promote gender equality empowerment. The project targets a 50% male-female rate of the expected beneficiaries adopting improved agricultural practices.

### D.3.5. FPIC procedures will be followed for all project activities and investments at district and commune level and a grievance redress mechanism will be established. FPIC obtained from ethnic minorities will be given special consideration in the design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting of projects. The project will engage DARIs or other stakeholders due to unique power dynamics. FPIC processes will engage third-party independent facilitators for FPIC consultations on a regular basis.

### D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### D.4.1 Economic and social development level and vulnerability to climate change

*Viet Nam has achieved strong economic growth of an average 6.4% in Gross Domestic Product per year. The proportion of the population living below the poverty line fell from 32% in 1990 to 8% in 2020, down by over 75% since 1993.*

*Viet Nam is one of the most disaster and natural hazard-prone countries in the East Asia and Pacific region. The World Bank listed Viet Nam as one of the five countries that will be most affected by climate change. The rural poor are particularly vulnerable to climate change because they have limited access to resources, lack skills for living in areas vulnerable to natural disasters, have higher dependency upon agriculture and natural resources for their livelihoods, and generally have fewer assets with which to cope with shocks.*

*The project area remains poorer in comparison to most other regions of the country, with poverty rates of 8.3% compared to the Central Highlands and 17.1% in the North Thanh**^1^** and its economy remains dominated by agriculture and forestry.*

*The poverty rate among rural minorities is 40.4%, accounting for 59.8% of the extreme poor. The country has been hit hard by climate change, with a one-degree Celsius increase in temperature targeting the population most affected by climate change. With the support under Objective D.1, there have a focus on deforestation and forest degradation, and the households have small landholdings which are generally infertile and water stressed. In the absence of irrigation, labor productivity is low and yields are very low. The majority of the rural poor live in remote areas with limited access to markets and labor exchange arrangements are common for households who do not have sufficient able-bodied adults and are often forced into child labor.*

#### D.4.2 Alternative sources of financing

*Viet Nam has the potential to access alternative sources of forest finances, such as LEAF, that could also benefit from the project. The project will support the development of a national forest carbon market for the pilot project with an upgrade on monitoring and reporting of forest cover and quality and resulting emissions and carbon sequestration. The project will also support the development of a national forest finance strategy to attract private sector assets (e.g., VOB, GEF) and MFIs to attract more suitable capital to increase their lending in the agriculture and forestry sectors.*

*Finance barriers such as high costs, risks, and private sector hesitation in agroforestry investments need attention. The project will support the development of a national forest finance strategy to complement its innovative loan model, as discussed in Section B.5.*

#### D.4.3 Institutions and implementation capacity

*The provincial government has been implementing REDD+ at the provincial level. Provinces are requested to 100% achieve their NDC targets by 2030. However, none of the participating provinces has been able to implement a national forest information system (NFIS) or other systems for monitoring and reporting on emissions and funding support. There is also a lack of interdepartmental, cross-sectoral and public-private coordination.*

#### D.4.5 Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

*Viet Nam has fully achieved the four key requirements of the Warsaw Framework, having now completed its National Forest Inventory (NFI), Forest Reference Emission Levels (FRELRL) and more recently an operational and online Safeguards Information System (SIS), along with Summary Reports on the NFI and FRELRL. The country is also committed to implementing a national forest information system as part of transformational change in the forestry sector, example, the FLOAT Voluntary Partnership Agreement (VPA).*

#### Alignment and ownership

1. The project will closely follow the development of details between the Government of Viet Nam and the EU under the recently enacted EUDR.
2. The project has integrated lessons learned from the REED+ in the design: (i) the core of a successful REED+ approach is to ensure that all stakeholders are involved, including local communities; (ii) it provides strong incentives for better forest management (or disincentives for forest destruction); and (iii) it incentivizes community forests by providing access to markets and other services, as well as access and/or benefits from domestic PES schemes.

#### GCF country programme

1. The National Designated Authority is the Ministry of Planning and Investment. The GCF so far has funded 3 projects in Viet Nam:
   - FP013: Improving the resilience of vulnerable coastal communities to climate change related impacts in Viet Nam, implemented by UNDP, approved 2017
   - FP011: Strengthening the resilience of smallholder agriculture to climate change-induced water insecurity in the Central Highlands and South-Central Coastal regions of Viet Nam*, implemented by UNDP, approved 2020
   * VND has been the DAC of Viet Nam since July 2021 and implemented GCF readiness project to strengthen the government’s capacity as its first DAC at the GCF. The objective is to enhance their ability in project preparation and implementation. This project will also support the Government of Viet Nam (GoVN) to strategically leverage these results to advance Viet Nam's commitments under its NDC and NAP. Concurrently, REED+ will be implemented by UNDP in collaboration with VND. The project will ensure that lessons learned, harmonious approaches, ensure complementarity between the two GCF projects, and avoid duplication of efforts. This collaboration will maximize the effectiveness of both initiatives and optimize the utilization of GCF funds.

#### GCF strategy in Viet Nam over the past 20 years

1. IFAD's strategy in Viet Nam over the past 20 years has focused on supporting the Government’s achievement of its key development target to move rural populations from subsistence farming to climate resilient market-oriented agriculture and livestock production, with a focus on smallholder farmers. The strategy has been implemented through agriculture SMEs in underserved areas with a concentration of ethnic minorities, like the Central Highlands and Ben Tre provinces.

#### IFAD experience

1. IFAD has experience with supporting and encouraging innovation and experimentation with value chain development, including market access for agricultural products, climate-smart agriculture (CSA), agroforestry and forestland management, private sector engagement, and climate smart agriculture. In addition, IFAD has also supported the Government of Viet Nam in addressing land tenure issues as well as other related issues such that investments in climate mitigation and adaptation benefit poor rural households and the most vulnerable groups.

138 IFAD-funded projects in the highlands (i.e., JEM and TNSPD) already included many of the key building blocks of RECAF: the climate-informed SEDP, the climate resilient value chain action planning, the common interest group.

### D. Efficiency and effectiveness (max.: 500 words, approximately | 1 page)

#### 1. Major objectives of the GCF project

The major objective of the GCF project is to remove bottlenecks to transition from monocrop plantations to multi-crop systems in the agroforestry sector. This will be achieved by:

- Increasing access to finance for smallholder farmers, particularly financially and economically viable banks to retain resources.
- Using GCF concessional funding combined with IFAD’s infrastructure loan to address high upfront investment costs through the development of adequate credit products and the provision of annual underwriting credits.

#### 2. Risks

Risks include:

- Not accessing immediately on trees crops (implying that producers have to find alternative sources of income while waiting for returns from agroforestry).
- The risk of price fluctuations on the crops grown, addressed by testing multi-crop aggregation models that allow access to markets with supporting infrastructure.
- The risk of market access constraints, addressed through supporting infrastructure.

#### 3. Under the GCF model

Under the GCF concessional model, the GCF grant will be loaned to financial institutions, but the majority of the direct financing for the agroforestry will take place through financial institutions and beneficiary own capital, to avoid drawing down project investments. Hence, the GCF funding focuses on the following features:

- Information systems of financial institutions (through the 4P platform).
- Improving access to markets for producers (through the 4P platforms).

#### D

deforestation (and infrastructure support) and the institutional aspects around land tenure. The proposed project aims to create a better investment environment for sustainable agriculture and deforestation-free value chains.

This additional analysis is based on an Economic and Financial Analysis (EFA) of the project, which could potentially receive support from Viet Nam's Direct Access Entity (DAE), and in the future.

144. An Economic and Financial Analysis (EFA) of the project was conducted to assess the viability of project activities from a financial perspective. The analysis is based on two sets of models: one that assumes no project, and another that actively models compare the benefits with and without the project, so the results presented below are all incremental in nature.

145. The EFA shows an NPV (Net Present Value) for the project activities of USD 208 million over a period of twenty years, corresponding to an Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) of 29.6%. The high return stems from the project ambition to create a new market for sustainable agriculture and deforestation-free value chains in Viet Nam.

Another reason is that these models consider a period of twenty years, so the NPV of benefits includes twenty years' time value.

145. The project is very efficient in terms of cost per beneficiary and cost per ton of CO2 mitigated. The cost per beneficiary ranges from USD 30 to USD 68, depending on the scenario considered. The cost per ton of CO2 mitigated varies between USD 5 per ton of CO2 in 2012 and USD 49-89 in 2023. Using the lower bracket for the social price of carbon that ranges from USD 5 to USD 49, increases the project NPV by USD 67 million.

146. However, in addition to the high returns on investment, it is important to raise awareness of opportunities and risk mitigation strategies among policy makers, financial institutions, and the suboptimal institutional environment. Viet Nam's current institutional framework is not conducive to scaling up projects that are aligned with GCF grant funding criteria and set Viet Nam on a trajectory towards meeting its NDC goals. It is imperative to secure GCF grants for this project and set Viet Nam on a trajectory towards meeting its NDC goals.

146. In particular, the enabling conditions, such as policy and institutional frameworks, are crucial yet often overlooked by traditional financial institutions. Grants bridge the gap by providing the necessary resources to address these non-bankable areas and pave the way for sustainable development and achievement of net-zero goals.

## Challenges related to limited access to credit for investment in emission reduction initiatives

### Further challenges include a lack of financial support and coordination among actors within the value chain, hindering effective collaboration.

#### These challenges encompass weak linkages between the NFRS and other public and private sector institutions, including an early warning system required for effective implementation of REDD+ RPBs. There is also the need to address a lack of capacity in implementing REDD+ RPBs pertaining to the NDC. There is a need to improve project design processes and use of cost-effective methodologies and standards.

### Public Institutions, extension providers, communities, private sector value chain actors, and provincial MIs are equipped with the necessary tools and resources for effective implementation of REDD+ RPBs. This is achieved by utilizing the best practices and drawing insights from existing projects in Mozambique. The project actively contributes to knowledge generation and alternative income opportunities.

#### Through valuing-activities and sustainable livelihoods such as beekeeping and investments, actors are made aware of market linkages and distribution mechanisms successfully reducing emissions and improving livelihoods.

### Project activities contribute to knowledge sharing on climate change, REDD+, agroforestry, PFPESRES, and MCFM. These efforts aim to facilitate water adoption, complemented by a dedicated website for information dissemination and a dedicated hotline for public inquiries.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

To achieve the expected mitigation impact with adaptation co-benefits, a project M&E will be systematically developed and implemented in close collaboration with the Evaluation Reference Group (ERG) which is established, consisting of key stakeholder representatives and small pool of local experts to ensure that the project monitoring, reporting and evaluation framework is aligned with the expectations of expected results.

E.7.1 Monitoring

The main purpose will be the monitoring and changes of land use or aggradation and regeneration or restoration of degraded forest. RECF will develop a monitoring system to report results on emission reduction effectiveness, including the use of satellite imagery (e.g., Landsat 8 OLI) and other tools, in combination with Terma model in Lam Dong Province, and other standard tools. The project will establish and operationalize an effective monitoring framework for REDD+ projects that includes a baseline assessment of forest cover and impact of REDD+ interventions; Comprehensive national forestland use and forest remote sensing and data collection; Regular remote sensing and data collection; Continuous evaluation of the effectiveness of land uses; and continuous evaluation of the effectiveness of land uses. The tools will continue to be used throughout the project for M&E across Outcomes and Outputs, also linked to the project’s monitoring framework. The project will use a combination of methods including satellite imagery in design stage, assessing the expected impact magnitude, and with regularly updated remote sensing databases and local data collection. The project will also use the Emission Reductions Performance Standard (ERPS) and Project Approval Report (PARP), for GCF project stakeholders and GCF purposes. While EX-ACT is considered among standard tools for M&E of REDD+ projects, the project will not be using this tool as it is currently under development by FAO Nationally Determined Contribution Expert Tool (NEXT). It will be brought in as necessary.

The project will employ surveys to measure adaptation and economic co-benefits. These surveys will assess the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices, including the use of improved seeds, livestock breeds, and other technologies. The survey will also collect data on the impact of these technologies on household income and food security. A comparison will be made with control households, and participatory evaluation methods will be used to measure the effectiveness of the project in promoting adaptation.

The project will employ a combination of remote sensing and ground-based monitoring techniques for Measurement and Analysis (M&A)147 and Building Resilience and Adaptation to Climate Extremes and Disasters (BRACED)205. The impact of these technologies, will be measured through regular data reporting and uploading to the Project Management Information System (PMIS). Data collected from the project’s monitoring framework will also be used for M&E purposes.

for example DAIS in the provinces, by other IFAD projects and the GCF UNDP project). A large part of the monitoring data will be collected and communicated by the participating districts and communes.

153.The M&E system will have an embedded project MIS integrating project management, financial management, procurement and reporting. The MIS will provide a single source of information for all stakeholders including implementing agencies to provide and review progress data for planning, reporting and adjustments of the implementation strategy. The MIS will be designed in such a way that it is user-friendly, easy-to-use, stand-alone project MIS, interoperable and where relevant, directly integrated with government information management systems. The MIS will include a dashboard for monitoring and tracking key activities and outputs, and core relevant outcomes. Maintaining key elements of the M&E system for quality assurance purposes, the M&E system will be reviewed by the Evaluation Unit at least once every quarter, semi-annual and annual feedback on the extent to which the project is achieving its outputs, implementing the identified strategies and achieving the intended results. The impact of the project will be measured through the project to all sectors of the target population, as well as the technical and financial efficiency will be monitored and periodically reported.

154 Project evaluation, with focus on mitigation results and co-benefits, scalability, replicability and sustainability, will be initiated and managed by the Evaluation Unit. The evaluation will be guided by the Evaluation Framework (EF) and will include (i) process evaluation; (ii) impact evaluation; and (iii) thematic evaluation. At the same time, the funding period mid-term and at the conclusion of the project will be conducted by Independent evaluations commissioned by and external to IFAD and the Government. These official reviews are the Mid-Term Evaluation (MTE) and Independent Final Evaluation (IFE).

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## 1.1. Risk factors and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

### 15. The current global economic environment, such as the COVID-19 pandemic and increasing world commodity prices resulting from the Ukraine conflict, pose significant challenges. This risk is particularly relevant for Vietnam, which has a highly dependent economy on exports, with a high proportion of income derived from export revenue. This amplifies the vulnerability to these external shocks, especially in the context of the project's focus on forest-based enterprises and activities involving converting forests into cropland. Mitigating this risk depends on sound macroeconomic policies at the national level, including prudent fiscal management and effective financial planning.

### 16. The success of the project in improving livelihoods relies on establishing favorable conditions and incentives through socio-economic factors and supportive business regulations. Despite overall economic growth and poverty reduction, Vietnam faces significant challenges such as limited access to information technology, finance, and secure land tenure rights. These challenges are exacerbated by a lack of infrastructure development, particularly in remote areas that are forest-dependent communities. The project's primary objective is to facilitate a viable transition toward diversified livelihoods for these communities through the establishment of sustainable value chains and markets.

### 17. Similarly, RECAF will employ approaches and processes that foster the long-term sustainability and growth of forest-based enterprises and activities in Vietnam. These approaches are designed to ensure that they remain relevant beyond the project's lifespan, and active stakeholder engagement continues. There is a risk that the project may not be able to address all the challenges faced by these communities because their specific needs tend to be linked to the specific needs of individual farmers and enterprises while still applying to a significant number of them. The project will need to ensure that its interventions are tailored to meet these needs, which require a certain level of profitability.

### 18. Mitigation measures include ensuring that the project is well-aligned with national policies and strategies for forest-based communities. This includes engaging specialized service providers where necessary, such as IAFD's Country Team in Cambodia, which has extensive experience in this area. The project will also ensure that all outputs and results are transferred under the GCF-supported output/capital (limited to 5% of total cost).

### 19. RECAF is scheduled for review by the IAFD Executive Board (EB) on September 2024, and it targets the GCF Board in October 2024. If the project receives approval at a subsequent Board meeting, the following mitigation measures will be implemented: The post-approval risk management plan will be developed to ensure that the project is aligned with the Funded Activity Agreement between IAFD and GCF and preparing the Financial Agreement between IAFD and the Government of Vietnam. This includes ensuring that all necessary documents are ready for signing, any potential delays in project implementation can be minimized.

If the project receives approval at a subsequent Board meeting, the following mitigation measures will be implemented: The post-approval risk management plan will be developed to ensure that the project is aligned with the Funded Activity Agreement between IAFD and GCF and preparing the Financial Agreement between IAFD and the Government of Vietnam. This includes ensuring that all necessary documents are ready for signing, any potential delays in project implementation can be minimized.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 62

## Governance
**Description:** Medium

At the main organism level there is a risk of external shocks to the macro-economy increasing world commodity prices as a consequence of the global supply chain-related context. The risk of external shocks is always present in a representative country such as that of Viet Nam, particularly with the recent increase in oil and gas export revenue. These shocks could influence the Government's commitment to provide the resources for implementing the project.

## Selected Risk Factor 2 | Climate change impact

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

**Description:** The project area, and the project’s target beneficiaries in particular, are vulnerable to the impact of climate change (see climate change analysis in Annex A).

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The project will facilitate an economically viable transition to deforestation-free value chains, with a specific focus on forest-based products. This will be achieved through the development of new markets for these products and through specialty product development, it is better access to Finance for the necessary technologies and infrastructure.

## Selected Risk Factor 3 | Lack of interest from banks to invest in climate change mitigation and adaptation

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description:** There is a risk that the project is not able to design credit products attractive enough for banks to invest their own capital in the transition to deforestation-free value chains. These financial products must be specially tailored to the needs of smallholder farmers, who are often excluded from the formal banking system. This is necessary because financial institutions’ costs are primarily fixed, and therefore require scale to be profitable.

## Selected Risk Factor 4 | Insufficient human resources

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

**Description:** There is weak coordination at the provincial level, between departments and between provincial and district governments that may delay project implementation. There are also limited skilled and efficient contractors and service providers to implement the project.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The project promotes strong institutional arrangements for the NAP-SEDP process in the project areas, which is instrumental to coordinate, mobilize and convey both capacity and resources for project implementation. If AD plays a role as an implementing agency, it will be able to leverage its existing relationships with service providers, coupled with rigorous screening and investigation of potential contractors prior to engagement. The IFAD country team already started identification of potential external technical assistance during the design phase.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 63

## Selected Risk Factor: 5 Non-synchronous GCF and IFAD approval processes cause delay in implementation

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description
RECAF aims at the IFAD EB in September 2024 for the GCF Board meeting in October 2024. In case the project is not approved at the October 2024 GCF Board, this could potentially delay project implementation, or result in asynchronous project implementation.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Once IFAD approval has been obtained, all necessary steps will be initiated, including the drafting of the Funded Activity Agreement (between IFAD and GCF) and preparation of the Financial Agreement (between IFAD and the executing agency). The project will be implemented only after both agreements have been signed officially. This will ensure project integrity and mitigate delays. In addition, the costs for initial PMU establishment will be covered by Government to avoid delays in project implementation/startup.

## Selected Risk Factor: 6 Procurement delays

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Medium | Medium | Low |

### Description
Inflexible internal government regulations pose severe risks to the timely allocation of funds and disbursements. Transactions are often delayed due to a lack of transparency, which can lead to corruption. There are no money laundering or terrorist financing risks identified. Viet Nam has very strict procedures for the procurement process. The main risk is delays in project implementation due to internal government regulations. Risks are delays due to discrepancies between IFAD guidelines and national laws and regulations, delays in budget allocation and lengthy tender processes.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Mitigation measures include (i) The project will use a variety of tools, including web-based accounting software, will be identified and procured at inception stage; (ii) Using IFAD Online Procurement End-to-End (OPEN) and ICF e-procurement system for procurement of goods and services; (iii) All suppliers, consultants, contractors and sub-contractors will be required to sign self-certification forms on anticorruption. (iv) IFAD will give special attention to training of project staff. See detailed description of fiduciary risks and mitigation measures in IPRM.

## 16.1 Integrity Due Diligence (IDD) covers the following risk factors:

- **Ultimate beneficial owners (UBOs):** IFAD takes reasonable efforts, where applicable and practicable, in line with the risk-based approach, to identify and verify UBOs. For entities without UBOs, IFAD applies equivalent controls to the identification of beneficial ownership.
- **Sanctions screening:** IFAD screens at counterparties against applicable sanctions lists and industry-standard databases.
- **Financial crime screening:** Searches of counterparties are conducted against industry-standard databases to identify and mitigate financial crime risks associated with money laundering and terrorist financing.
- **Potentially Exposed Persons (PEPs):** The IDD involves reasonable measures to identify and assess IDO subjects including PEPs.
- **Nature of the engagement:** IFAD considers the size and nature of IFAD's engagement and/or transaction and verifies that it is consistent with IFAD’s policies and procedures.
- **Project host country risk assessment:** Is conducted to determine whether the country is at high-risk for TFIF as a result of its political, economic or social instability. This includes consideration of any applicable international sanctions in place.

MLC/AMLC: Additional AMLC/IFT checks for financial institutions, based on an assessment of their policies and procedures, which is initiated by the recipient completing an AML/CFT Questionnaire.
- **Anti-corruption screening:** IFAD will conduct due diligence checks on financial institutions involved in capacity building initiatives and investments mobilization that did not highlight any red flags. This includes a review of the institution’s anti-corruption policy and practices to identify potential related risks. The overall mix rating was therefore deemed Moderate Risk.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Assignment of the Environmental & Social Risk Category

162 The proposed environmental and social risk assignment for the project is “Category B”. This risk rating is proposed based on the following factors: (i) the nature and scale of the project; (ii) the potential impact of the project activities, (i) project’s affected stakeholders and results from stakeholder consultations; and (iii) socioeconomic and environmental conditions in the project area. The assessment concludes that there are no significant risks for the likelihood of such materializing, cumulative environmental and social impacts, and the severity of a given risk is considered to be low. The proposed mitigation measures are expected to effectively manage these risks.

### Summary of the main outcomes of ESF instruments

163 Based on the screening, the project design team prepared an) Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) and accompanying Action Plan (AP), and b) a Gender Assessment Plan (GAP). The ESMF is accompanied by the following ESFs: i) Community-based Forest Surveys; ii) Community Consultations; iii) Stakeholder Meetings; iv) Community-based Forest Surveys; v) Community Consultations; vi) Focus group discussions with project stakeholders and community members. A) Labor Market Plan (LMP), B) Environmental Management Plan (EMP), C) Biodiversity Action Plan (BAP), D) Gender Action Plan (GAP), E) Land Use and Related Business Management Plan (BLMP), are currently being developed. The findings from the consultations, focus group discussions, and related ESFs were used to assist the analysis. Key findings are summarized below.

### Social impacts of the project

164 Social impacts of the project are largely positive. The project activities aim to improve the livelihoods of those engaged in forest-based activities (e.g., women who participate) while reducing their dependence on unsustainable use of forest resources. These impacts will be achieved through a variety of ESFs, including: i) community-based forest surveys; ii) community consultations; iii) stakeholder meetings and land (via forest allocation); and enhance gender equality through increased female participation in project activities. The project is expected to provide opportunities for women to participate in the management of forest resources, diversify sources of income, and long-term participation in deforestation-free value chains. Key social risks associated with the project include: i) displacement of indigenous communities; ii) competition over labor influx (e.g., migrant workers engaged for project construction and other activities); iii) child and/or forced labor (e.g., children who are employed as forest guards, or hired to assist in the collection of firewood by workers); iv) road safety; and v) discrimination and exclusion of disadvantaged vulnerable groups. Internal risks include: i) lack of community participation in project design and implementation; ii) inadequate capacity for land readdress mechanisms; and presence of child and/or forced labor in the project areas (for example, when children assist their parents with forest-based activities).

### Environmental impacts and risks

165 The project is expected to target positive environmental impacts, including improved ecosystem services, soil fertility health, forest health, and natural resources management. Potential negative environmental impacts include: i) land use change; ii) deforestation (e.g., conversion of forests into agricultural lands or small-scale reservoirs); etc. These impacts may include: i) erosion and runoff to water bodies due to increased sedimentation; ii) soil degradation; iii) loss of biodiversity; iv) air pollution from construction activities; v) pollution from sourcing aggregates/construction materials (soil, gravels), and vi) increase water consumption. These risks will be managed through the application of a) ESMF; b) ESFs; c) Community-based Forest Surveys; d) construction practices and application of a site-specific Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP); e) watershed management plan; f) community-based forest surveys; g) community consultations; h) stakeholder meetings. The ESMF takes into account the territorial and environmental knowledge of the affected ethnic minorities. Additional environmental risks include: i) soil erosion; ii) sedimentation; iii) loss of biodiversity; iv) air pollution from use of pesticides due to crop intensification/improved crop yields. The ESMP provides the screening tools to be used at each stage of project implementation, including land acquisition and resettlement, construction and removal (if any) would be done by experts as part of this clearance prior to commencing any activities on the project site. These risks will be managed through the application of a) ESMF; b) ESFs; c) Community-based Forest Surveys; d) watershed management plan; e) community-based forest surveys; f) community consultations; g) stakeholder meetings. The ESMF takes into account the territorial and environmental knowledge of the affected ethnic minorities. Additional environmental risks include: i) soil erosion; ii) sedimentation; iii) loss of biodiversity; iv) air pollution from use of pesticides due to crop intensification/improved crop yields. The ESMP provides the screening tools to be used at each stage of project implementation, including land acquisition and resettlement, construction and removal (if any) would be done by experts as part of this clearance prior to commencing any activities on the project site.
