### Project Outcomes: potential impacts on Indigenous Peoples and measures to address those impacts

The project will implement a participatory approach in consultation with Indigenous Peoples, taking into account environmental and social risks and impacts identified under the initial assessment in the ESFM (which helps determine the scope of further analysis). The project will also consider the findings from the stakeholder engagement plan and the proposed mitigation measures for effective ES management during project implementation. The IPP has been developed to ensure that Indigenous Peoples are fully informed about the project, their rights and interests, and their role in project development and implementation. A community-driven development approach will be adopted to ensure the ownership and commitment of Indigenous communities to the project. This will include capacity building for Indigenous communities on increased self-reliance and community empowerment. The project will enhance existing community-driven development initiatives by providing technical assistance and resources to support the conservation of natural ecosystems and socioeconomic and cultural systems are interdependent. The IPP sets forth steps process for stakeholder engagement, including a summary of key findings from the initial assessment, and provides guidance on consultation that will be free to, free and informed consent of the target ethnic minority communities for proposed project activities.

### SEP: Summary of multi-stakeholder consultations undertaken during project design & planning for implementation

#### 10. A Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) was prepared during project design and provides guidance on how to conduct stakeholder engagement throughout the life cycle of the project, including during project design and implementation. The project/fragments affected, out of which 353 were male and 361 were female. Outreach involved a blend of household visits, community meetings, focus group discussions, and individual interviews. This extensive engagement helped inform project design – particularly on how to better encourage participation of ethnic minorities in the project (including through traditional ways) - and thereby determine preferred methods of communication (including methods of grievance redress).

#### Engagement and information disclosure during implementation

The SEP will continue to be updated as needed, but will continue to depend on the target communities and is designed to be, therefore, sensitive to ethnic minority community needs and preferences (as well as differentiated approaches) in terms of how they are engaged. The project team will continue to engage with the affected ethnic minorities and Koth in the target project communes. FPC consultations will be facilitated into the annual Indigenous Peoples’ Forum (IPF) meetings, which are held at least once a year for all communities involved in project activities. Gender and youth targets are also mainstreamed into the annual IPFs to ensure that targets are achieved. The results of the stakeholder engagement process will be documented and shared with the IPs instruments; once approved, will be disclosed at least 30 days prior to the GCF board meeting date. The documentation will include a summary of key findings from the initial assessment, and provides guidance on consultation that will be disclosed in Vietnamese in communes where potential affected peoples reside. The documents will also be disseminated through other means (e.g., community meetings).

#### FPC-specific (FPC/ethnic minority community) consultations

The project team has engaged with the FPCs of the target ethnic minorities and Koth present in the project area. FPC-specific consultations aimed to: (i) provide the concerned ethnic minority communities with information about project’s design, implementation, and monitoring and evaluation; and (ii) solicit their preferences based on their current traditional practices, including preferred approaches to avoiding or mitigating adverse impacts of the project activities. The FPCs were informed through a combination of the following: presentations, interviews, and household surveys (in this case, the IPFM-specific questions were part of the broader household survey). The results of these consultations will be documented in the FPC consultation report for the project design phase (June – November 2021 and May–July 2022 respectively) to the potential target ethnic minorities and Koth. These documents will include a summary of key findings from the initial assessment, and provide meaningful feedback for incorporation into project activities. All project information has been presented in a manner that is accessible to all stakeholders, including those with limited literacy or who do not speak Vietnamese languages. Sufficient time has been arranged – between the early stages of project design and design completion – to allow for adequate consultation with the affected ethnic minorities and Koth on the environmental and social risks and impacts, and provide feedback throughout project cycle. During project implementation, before activities are implemented in a given commune, the project team will consult with the FPCs of that commune so that the project will take full advantage of ethnic minority’s traditional culture, governance systems and natural

### 170. **Migration screening and mitigation**

The ESM details the screening procedures and tentative risks by project Outcome/Outcome/activity, along with the steps to be taken in case of a positive risk finding. The ESF also includes a detailed document that outlines the process for conducting these consultations during implementation. Both documents detail the project's grievance redress mechanism.

### 171. **The project has designed a GRM**

To provide complaints with redress procedures that are accessible, easy to understand, and in local languages. The ESF provides information on how to lodge complaints, including the forms, channels, steps, and processing/timeframes for each step of the resolution process (e.g., submission of the complaint, review by the project, decision of the resolution date, etc.). The GRM accounts for local practices of ethnic minorities in relation to grievance mechanisms. The ESF provides guidance on how to ensure that the project's grievance redress mechanism is consistent with international standards and/or Article 27.1, likewise, support PMUs and IP and practices for improved soil fertility. Two sub-sections provide details on the project's grievance redress mechanism and the ES instruments implementation of mitigation measures, and monitoring/reporting throughout the project cycle.

### Project-specific grievance redress mechanism

The project has designed a GRM to provide complaints with redress procedures that are accessible, easy to understand, and in local languages. The ESF provides information on how to lodge complaints, including the forms, channels, steps, and processing/timeframes for each step of the resolution process (e.g., submission of the complaint, review by the project, decision of the resolution date, etc.). The GRM accounts for local practices of ethnic minorities in relation to grievance mechanisms. The ESF provides guidance on how to ensure that the project's grievance redress mechanism is consistent with international standards and/or Article 27.1, likewise, support PMUs and IP and practices for improved soil fertility.

### Capacity of the Existing Entities to implement the ESM and ESF arrangements for compliance monitoring

The project provinces have prior experience with implementing projects financed by IAF, the World Bank, Asian Development Bank, etc., and have established a number of grievance redress mechanisms. These entities have specific performance indicators and targets; provincial PMUs will be trained during the first year of project implementation to effectively implement the triggered ESS standards during the project cycle.

### Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words)

The gender assessment was carried out through extensive collection and analysis of secondary data (national, regional, and local) and primary data. The sample size for the household survey is approximately 654 people, of which 80% are ethnic minority women and close to 50% are females. All consulted people live in 21 districts of 5 project provinces.

### Key findings

The gender assessment suggests that the ethnic minorities in the project area are very poor and as such vulnerable to the potential impact of climate change, particularly to their current agricultural production which makes up 77% of total income. The average monthly cash income for women is approximately $100, while it is lower than that of men ($240) and average productive land access among female-headed households is less than half (35%) compared with male-headed households (86%). There are also significant differences in monthly average cash income between males and females. In terms of shared land titles, it is noted that across all types of land use types covered under the household survey (including Perennial Land, Annual Cropland),

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 67

## Production Forest: Protection Forest, Agri-land for Animal Husbandry and Residential land), only 30.7% (n=192) have both wives and husbands with names on their LURCs (See Section 3.3.2).

### In terms of division of labor and decision making, gender stereotypes still exist due to social norms in which women are perceived as more responsible for household chores while men spend most of the time away from home for paid work and social activities outside their home. Almost all of the financial decision-making rests with female members of households. This is because women are seen as more responsible for managing family finances, especially by men as they are perceived to be more knowledgeable about cultivation practices, innovation, labor, and market due to their long-term experience in agriculture. Women also have limited access to credit facilities and other services (i) identifying business opportunities, (ii) protecting their business from being harmed, and (iii) bargaining for better prices. This is because women are seen as less responsible for managing family finances especially by men living in nearby villages or neighboring provinces, as they have to ask for permission of their husbands (See Section 3.3.5).

### Recommendations: Based on the results of gender assessment, a Gender Action Plan and IIP were prepared. The following actions will be implemented to promote women's participation in activities that benefit them at all household and community level. To promote female participation in project activities (which devolve from the current labor division and social norms), the following key actions will be implemented:

- Ensure at least 40% of female participants in all project planning activities – be intended to benefit local people (community members) and not just women.
- Reduce women's burden from traditional household chores by:
    - Explicitly including women as beneficiaries of projects that save time and labor and introduce drug-free reduction mechanisms;
    - Encouraging the use of small-scale practices that involve mechanization to free-up intense and heavy labor. This can be shared in groups of people that typically share labor.
- Provide opportunities for women to attend training programs enabling women to take trainings outside their home.
- Promote the establishment of mentorship training (e.g., technologies, leadership training, financial literacy, business development, etc) to develop value chains that women are more comfortable/fit for niche products such as beekeeping, sericulture, weaving, natural dyes (which can be introduced/included in agro-forestry interventions).
- Collectively identify and promote the role of women who have already demonstrated their leadership skills and champions.
- Promote gender mainstreaming throughout the whole process of governing-led FLA including joint ownership of forestland to promote decision making on the part of women.

### The above actions aim to promote equal participation of women from project's target groups, which will enhance their capacity for better management of natural resources and foster the process of economic empowerment for female members. Further details are provided in the full gender assessment and project-level gender-action plan in Annex V.

## Financial Management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Financial Management

#### Staffing
All five provinces and MAID will use their respective accounting and finance staff to manage the financial aspects of the project. All provinces have identified staff who will work on RECAAP, most of whom are experienced in IFI-funded operations. IFAD will provide additional hands-on training and support before and during implementation as well as technical assistance for the provinces to ensure adequate staffing for PM.

#### Budgeting
The tight and inflexible international government regulations pose severe risks to the timely allocation of funds and disbursements. Therefore, the project will have dedicated staff at the PM with the required skills to address all budget-related issues. The PM will also be responsible for ensuring that all provinces submit their annual budget allocations for each year. In addition, the AWBP for each year will be submitted 60 days before the start of each fiscal year. This is in line with the IFAD policy on the submission of Annual Work Budget Plans (AWBPs), Outcomes, Outputs, and activities (GCF budgeting requirement). IFAD funds will only finance capital expenditures. GCF and Government co-financing funds will also finance recurrent expenditures and soft investments such as

## Training and Consultancies
Budget execution will be monitored quarterly utilizing the accounting software of the project.

### Funds Flow and Disbursements
All procurements and MARD will maintain DAS in both for both IFAD Loan and GCF grants. The State Treasury will flow funds to DAS for Project Accounts based on annualized approved budgets for each province and MARD. VET's main Treasury system is reliable for maintaining DAS accounts, however, the project will use a separate accounting software for its own records (IFAD). All procurements and MARD will have procedures and systems to ensure that accurate IFARs are sent to IFAD within the required time frame. The project will also provide an annual report on the status of the project for the next two quarters.

### Internal Control Environment
All provincial governments and central ministries are required to follow stringent financial regulations of the Government. Therefore, the internal control environment is reliable though inflexible rules cause some delays in disbursement. All procurements and MARD will have procedures and systems to ensure that accurate IFARs are sent to IFAD within the required time frame.

### Accounting and Financial Reporting
All procurements will produce and customise accounting software for the project needs. The software will need to maintain accounting records on double entry bookkeeping principles. The system will be able to generate financial statements, including Balance Sheet, Profit & Loss Statement, Outcomes, Activities, outputs, IFAD, and government-reporting reports. In addition to quarterly IFARs, procurements and MARD will provide an annual report on the status of the project for the next two quarters.

### Project Implementation
The project will be implemented in phases. The first phase is expected to last 12 months from the date at which the PMF and PIM will be sent to IFAD. The PMF and PIM will include a report template to be used by the project team for the preparation of the PMF and PIM. The project team will also prepare a combined GCF account statement. IFAD will work with the project team to ensure that IFAD and GCF required information is included in the PMF and PIM.

### External Audit
Private sector auditors will audit annually project financial statements. The project will submit audit reports to IFAD within five months of the end of each period. Tolls of the annual project audit will include an assessment of the project’s compliance with GCF requirements, including but not limited to: (i) whether the project is in compliance with GCF requirements; In addition, the GCF has the right to conduct ad-hoc reviews on the use of its funds and may request additional information from the project team.

### Procurement
Procurement in Viet Nam is governed by the Law on Public Procurement No. 20/2013/QH13, Decree 24/2014/NĐ-CP on Instructions for Implementation of The Law on Public Procurement. The procurement framework of all public sector entities is based on this law and its instructions. The current public procurement system is applicable and mandatory to all procurement activities by public bodies at central and local levels for goods, works and services.

### The e-procurement System
The e-procurement system in the country is well functioning supporting the procurement process, including advertisement of procurement plan, invitation for bids, bidding submissions, counting of bids, bid opening advertisements, awarding of contracts, contract signing, etc. The project team has been trained on how to use and endorse use of the system to procure NCBs contracts of goods and works under the two banks systems.

### Project Procurement Risk
The overall residual risk for project procurement is moderate. The main project procurement risks include: (i) minor corruption in the bidding process; (ii) non-compliance with the IFAD/UNDP guidelines on terms and conditions and thresholds for applying direct contracting and single source selection, procurement method (C03); (iii) there are no clear procedures to ensure that all procurements are conducted through the e-procurement system based on the approved AWPS supporting planned activities; (iv) limited English skills of project staff including procurement team; and (v) procurement process challenges (planning, bidding, contract award and contract implementation) are often delayed.

### Major Mitigation Measures
Several mitigation measures for project procurement risks are: (i) using IFAD’s Online Procurement End-to-End System (EPES) for all procurements of goods/services; (ii) procuring good/service packages under RECA where applicable; (iii) requesting bidders and contractors to sign a non-disclosure agreement to prevent abuse as part of bids/proposals and contract documents; (iv) conducting prior and ex-post reviews to strengthen enforcement of the dolement system; (v) carrying out coaching, on-the-job trainings, and refresher trainings.

### Applicable regulations and procurement arrangements: 

Procurement under RECAF shall be carried out in accordance with IFAD’s General Conditions for Financial Arrangements (GCFAs) and the updated public procurement regulations as long they are consistent with IFAD Project Procurement Guidelines. In cases where there is a conflict between the two, the GCFAs will prevail.

### IFAD Procurement Guidelines will take precedence and govern:

The IFAD Procurement Guidelines will take precedence and govern all aspects of project implementation in accordance with the terms of the RECAF Agreement. The Provincial Forestry and Environment Department (PFE) (hereinafter referred to as PFE), the District Forestry and Environment Office (DFO) (hereinafter referred to as DFO), the District Chief (DC) (hereinafter referred to as DC), the Deputy District Chief (DDC) (hereinafter referred to as DDC), the Project Manager (PM) (hereinafter referred to as PM), and the project implementing partners (DAMO and sub-departments under DAMO, WUD, District People’s Committees and other relevant local authorities) are responsible for project procurement activities.

### IFPDs (Under 2.3):

1. **IFPDs Under 2.3:**

   - The PFPDF is an independent legal entity established under the Forestry Act No. 40/2561 B.E., which was amended by the Royal Decree No. 38/2561 B.E.

To ensure that procurement activities are carried out in accordance with IFAD project procurement guidelines and the project implementation manual. Measures that form part of this framework for transparency include:

G.4 Disclosure of funding proposal

No confidential information. The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF as no information is being provided in confidence.

With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity will provide a redacted copy of the funding proposal to the GCF for internal use only and will ensure that:

- Full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the procurement manual;
- Redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP250

## Proposal name: Achieving emission reduction in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast of Viet Nam to support National REDD+ Action Programme goals (RECAF)

### Accredited entity: International Fund for Agricultural Development

### Country: Viet Nam

### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Catalyses a shift in the enabling environment for long-term deforestation-free value chain infrastructure development, including by (i) the use of GCF funding focusing on non-carbon generating activities and (ii) developing a more diversified portfolio of projects with multi-faceted profiles of agroforestry investments. | GCF support is in the form of grants for a project with the potential for revenue generation from carbon credits by (i) the use of GCF funding focusing on non-carbon generating activities and (ii) developing a more diversified portfolio of projects with multi-faceted profiles of agroforestry investments. |
| Shifts focus from plantations to multi-crop agroforestry systems, which can provide natural forests as a result of community involvement and livelihood diversification, reducing the risk of deforestation. | In order to avoid the risk of double-counting, revenue generation by the project will not be set as carbon credits for the duration of the project. |
| Mobilizes substantial financing from beneficiaries and local financial institutions, aligning with GCF’s objective of leveraging producer partnerships while helping demonstrate a business case for value chains and agroforestry-oriented financial products. |  |

2. Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE) and, if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II of document GCF/J.40/02.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background


---
**Page 84**
---

1. Vietnam’s rapidly growing economy is placing increasing pressure on its natural resources. This is particularly apparent in the issue of deforestation of forests, one of Viet Nam's most valuable assets with over 10.1 million hectares of natural forests and another 4.7 million hectares of forest land under management. The country has a strong interest in finding ways to balance the conservation of forests with sustainability challenges of forests and economic development in rural areas.
2. Without appropriate interventions to sustainably enhance forest management and agricultural productivity, pressures on the country’s forests are likely to intensify, reducing the capacity of forests to provide ecosystem services and contributing to climate change. In the critical focus area of Viet Nam's nationally determined contributions (NDCs), with an associated carbon budget of 105 million metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO2-eq) by 2030 and conditional commitment of 82.5 megatonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO2-eq) by 2030, the country has committed to reducing emissions from land use change and forestry activities by an additional 14.1 Mt CO2-eq by the same year.
3. The project proposes a multi-pronged strategy to achieve this vision, by helping unlock the potential of communities and private agroforestry actors in partnership with the public and financial sectors, to invest in forest conservation and the development of deforestation-free value chains (e.g., coffee, tea, rubber, cashew nuts). The project will focus on a variety of specialty products (honey, medicinal plants, mushrooms, dyes, silk, etc.). It will do so by:
(a) Bolstering the regulatory framework and forest monitoring and protection infrastructure, along with productive infrastructure for deforestation-free value chains;
(b) Filling gaps in financial services to support agroforestry activities, unlocking additional financing streams for forest conservation;
(c) Linking REDD+ finance to directly support producers through payment for forestry ecosystem services (PES), while also providing technical and financial assistance to rewarding communities and private actors for supporting mitigation goals for Viet Nam; and
(d) Addressing knowledge and financial barriers for communities and producers through technical capacity development on deforestation-free value chains (including training and knowledge sharing, as well as access to finance). The project will provide support for local financial institutions to develop financial products tailored to the long returns of agroforestry.
4. The project focuses on five provinces in Central Viet Nam which have experienced a significant loss of natural forests and forest degradation (362,532 hectares), representing 10 per cent of the total area of natural forests and forest land under management in ethnic minority groups while ensuring a positive economic return on investment.
5. The total project funding amount is USD 96.80 million, which ICF Funding is requested to provide as follows: USD 72.43 million for project implementation purposes; USD 12.46 million for the establishment of an independent third-party monitoring and verification infrastructure. The Government of Viet Nam will provide USD 28.64 million in grant and in-kind resources.
6. The project’s environmental and social safeguards risk is categorized as G.
7. The project will be executed by the five Provincial People's Committees, and the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development in the case of output 1.2.

### Component-by-component analysis


---
**Page 85**

# Project Description

The detailed description of project activities is contained in annex 15.

## Component 1: Vist Nam - REDD+ design phase (2016-2018)

### Output 1.1: REEDO phase 1 (design)

This output aims to ensure that the design phase fulfills all required functions, including afforestation:

* Upgrade the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS) to meet the requirements of REDD+ Transactions/TDREDD Environmental Excellence Standard (ART/REES) standard applied in Vietnam.
* Develop a detailed action plan for implementing the REEDO Action Plan through regional investment plans and dialogues on private sector resource mobilization, payment for forest ecosystem services, and community forest management.

### Output 1.2: Protocol policies, legal and institutional frameworks, and stakeholder coordination

This output will ensure that Vietnam has a protocol in place to foster four platforms at the provincial level, and develop locally-appropriate mitigation actions linked to deforestation-free value chains and community forestry systems.

## Component 2: Vist Nam - REDD+ phase 2 (implementation)

### Output 2.1: Deforestation-free value chains developed by 40%, increased access to finance

The action plans created by 4R at the district level:

* Developing the capacity of farmers, community interest groups and small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in implementing deforestation-free practices; and supporting financial providers to develop agroforestry-tailored financial products.

### Output 2.2: Deforestation-free value chains and forest restoration infrastructure upgraded and established

This output will be financed by IFC funds, and includes:

* Small-scale investments in the development of agroforestry systems, fertilizer production facilities, post-harvest facilities with value-added processing, and (if feasible) investments in small-scale irrigation schemes as well as public nurseries to support afforestation and reforestation.

### Output 2.3: Collaborative forest management capacity enhanced through performance-based financing

This output will be financed by IFC funds, a first-in-country program for ecosystem services focused on enhancing the capacity of local communities and private sector agroforestry cooperatives and a long-term planning financial, and (if supported) for multi-stakeholder community-level collaborative forest management (MCCFM), with particular land-use planning and implementation of MCCFM pilots.

## Project Management Costs

* Total cost: USD 2.5 million
* GCF cost: USD 1.48 million

This cost will support the management, coordination, and implementation of the project activities in compliance with the contractual obligations that are included or referred to in the legal agreements between the parties involved. The total project management costs must be less than 5 per cent of the grant portion of requested funding from GCF. The overall amount exceeds the reference figure of 5 per cent established in the GCF Policy on Fees for accredited

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The proposal envisions direct emission reductions of 6.68 Mt CO₂ eq over 12 years as a result of reduced forest degradation and sequestration from agriculture and afforestation in a total of 50,000 ha (including 47,390 ha of degraded land under community-based forest management), estimated following the relevant EX-ACT methodology. This is a significant direct contribution to the country’s NDC target of 18 Mt CO₂ eq., or roughly 34.1 Mt CO₂ eq. conditional, with a scalable model as argued below.

In addition to direct emission reductions, the project is likely to have a substantial impact on forest governance and community-based forest management in the target provinces, as deforestation pressures are reduced as a result of forest protection policies and reforms, including the establishment of protected areas, community-managed forests, and community-based forest management and performance-based payments for forest ecosystem services.

Significant co-benefits are also envisaged to a rugged landscape prone to floods, as healthier forests help mitigate against floods, landslides, and erosion. The project will train forest communities in the use of agroforestry systems and provide advisory services including pest information, resilient crops and agroforestry practices, and financial literacy training for women and youth, especially Indigenous Peoples, against the impacts of climate (estimated: 140,000 beneficiaries).

In order to avoid risk of double-counting, emission reductions generated by the project will not be sold as carbon credits over the duration of the project.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift Scale: High

The project takes a multi-pronged approach to addressing key regulatory, technical and financial challenges in sustainably forest land management, deforestation-free value chains and climate resilient livelihoods based on agroforestry systems.

From a regulatory and institutional perspective, the project plans to enhance coordination between the Ministry of Environment and Forestry and Provincial People’s Committees which have a high degree of agency on land use and economic development matters. The project will also support the implementation of REDD+ in Vietnam, while addressing remaining gaps in the REDD+ framework. Vietnam is relatively advanced in terms of REDD+ implementation; project activities will build on the foundations provided by the National Forest Inventory, National Forest Monitoring System, Safeguard Information System and registry, and fill gaps to meet national forest monitoring systems, Safeguard Information System and registry, and fill gaps to meet

# Sustainable development potential

## Needs of the recipient

### Scale: Medium

28. Vietnam is a country with strong economic growth, with GDP growing at a pace above 6 per cent annually over the past decade. In contrast to other regions in the country, with poverty rates of 13 per cent in the Central Highlands and 9 per cent in the North, increasing to 40 per cent among ethnic minorities targeted by the project.

28. Loan finance provides official development assistance (ODA) funding for the project, which will substantially enable conditions for achieving higher levels of complementarity to complement infrastructure loan. Grant financing will support addressing the enabling environment governance, coordination and institutional capacity building. The project is expected to address some of the challenges that agroforestry investments face, characterized by long return periods and high risks, particularly at its inception stage.

## Country ownership

### Scale: High

28. The project is a key priority for the Government of Viet Nam, in terms of achieving its NDCs by developing one of the strategic pilots to reduce deforestation and emissions from land-use change. The national designated authority has been closely involved in driving the


---
**Page 88**
---

### Efficiency and effectiveness

The project has a relatively high mitigation cost-effectiveness at USD 15.4 (C/CO2 eq or USD 37.8 per ton CO2 eq) when GCF co-financing is considered. In addition, it provides significant economic and environmental co-benefits. This is reflected in its strong economic internal rate of return of 29% per year and a high social rate of return of 15%.

The GEF contribution of USD 35 million will be in the form of grants, with a co-financing ratio of 1:1.75 with IFAD (USD 32.4 million in loans) and the Government of Viet Nam (USD 28.6 million). The project is expected to leverage additional financial resources from other sources. The use of financial instruments places a greater burden on GCF in terms of the concessionality of the financing, but this is mitigated by the fact that the project will be implemented through a trust fund, taking into account the limitations posed by Viet Nam's official development assistance policy and IFAD’s own rationale. GCF funding will thus largely focus on financing technical assistance, developing the institutional capacity of the project management unit, and providing grants for early adopters – including a back-loaded financial return and relatively higher risk profile of investments.

The project is expected to leverage additional private investment from both financial institutions and beneficiaries’ own capital, leveraging an additional private investment between USD 13 and 14 million, which is a positive factor with regard to the project’s ability to sustain and scale up investments.


### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

Project brief. The project's activities are organized to support two desired outcomes of upgrading the country's REDD+ design pillars, integration into provincial policies and institutions, and promoting sustainable land use in agriculture, forestry, and other land use. Given that the project promotes forest conservation and encourages farmers to adopt sustainable agricultural practices, it is expected to have a positive impact on soil quality with regards improved soil quality and biodiversity in the target areas, as well as water conservation.

Environmental and social (ES) risk category and safeguard instrument. The project will be categorized under ES Risk Category 3 (medium). This is within the AEF's accreditation level. The infrastructure to be supported will be small-scale such as post-harvest processing facilities, irrigation systems, organic fertilizer production facility, forest protection and management activities, and other related activities including community-based conservation facilities, micro irrigation systems, organic fertilizer production facility, forest protection and management activities, and other related activities including community-based conservation facilities, among others. The risks of adverse social impacts due to changes in land classification and land-use regulations will be low due to the built-in provisions for consultations and the full and effective participation of local communities. The project will also include Category 4 (high risk) activities. The AEF has prepared an environmental and social management plan, which is a safeguard instrument that will be applied at the appropriate instrument for the project since the project will have several site-specific activities or subprojects, including small-scale infrastructure, post-harvest and processing facilities.


---
**Page 89**
---

# Compliance with the GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) standards.

The discussion of ESS compliance is divided into two parts: ES1: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts; and ES4: Community Health, Safety and Security (CHSS). The project involves a broad spectrum of activities that may have environmental or social risks. This includes the potential exposure of residents to construction site and traffic accident hazards.

## ES1: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts

The prepared ESMF provides a brief background of the project area, identifies the potentially affected communities, and describes the project activities. It outlines the procedure and requirements for managing the risks and impacts of subprojects. Management measures are proposed to address these risks and impacts.

### ES1.1: Labour and Working Conditions

The ESMP provides brief background information about current labour and working conditions legislations in Vietnam which is found to be inadequate with respect to child labour, forced labour, and bonded labour. The ESMP has addressed these issues related to children, adults, and forced labour. Among the measures proposed are preparation and implementation of a Labour management plan during the first year of project implementation; inclusion of the requirement for contractors to identify and report on any cases of child labour, adult forced labour, or bonded labour; inclusion in the procurement documents of the requirement for contractors to identify and report on any cases of child labour, adult forced labour, or bonded labour; requirement for contractors to conduct OHS training to raise awareness and promote application of OHS practices among workers; and where necessary, contractors will be required to engage qualified OHSS staff to oversee OHS issues. Additionally, the ESMP proposes that a risk assessment should be conducted at the beginning of related works and site-specific EHS assessments will cover potential labour issues, including risks of child or forced labour for suppliers and in the value chains relevant to the RECAP project.

The ESMP also includes a reference to the existing Labour Code and the social security redress mechanism (GK).

### ES1.2: Water Efficiency and Pollution Prevention

The project is expected to have only minor impacts on air and water quality, most of which are temporary, occurring only during the construction of small-scale infrastructure in the form of erosion and sedimentation generation. The ESMP proposes that standard mitigation measures have been identified and are included in the environmental code of practice for the project area. These include the use of appropriate application techniques to reduce pesticide use due to the need to intensify production or increased yield from fields. The risk of increased pesticide use is mitigated by the adoption of good production/good agricultural practices and utilizing integrated pest management.

### ES1.3: Community Health, Safety and Security (CHSS)

The project involves a broad spectrum of activities that may have environmental or social risks. This includes the potential exposure of residents to construction site and traffic accident hazards. Standard mitigation measures are proposed for these risks which include extension of occupational health and safety measures, including provision of signage, fences, and other barriers to prevent access to areas where hazardous materials are stored.

The project also involves the use of small reservoirs as part of a CHSS hazard. This hazard from small reservoirs will be mitigated through proper engineering design. Proposed reservoirs will undergo EAS assessments which will input into the engineering design (ESD) process and will be included in the ESMP for review. The risk of unexploded ordnances (UXOs) as one of the inherent CHSS risks in the project, though


---
**Page 90**
---

considered to be low since construction activities will reportedly not occur in previously untouched land. Therefore, the ESMP indicates that UXO risk assessments will be conducted for all subproject sites where UXOs are considered a potential hazard. If necessary, UXO clearance and remediation measures will be implemented as permitted by UXO clearance is complete. Interventions involving agricultural production, and the exclusion of areas from agricultural use, will be subject to an environmental impact assessment. Exposure to pesticides will be mitigated under the project which will include training on good agricultural practices and integrated pest management, as well as in instances where pesticide use is unavoidable, the application of best available technology (BAT) will be required. As regards potential for conflict in relation to anticipated small-scale labour influx and the use of local labour, it should be noted that the number of workers involved with each subproject is likely to be low. Workers of infrastructure scheme contractors will also be required to abide with a code of conduct.

### ESMP: Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement
The infrastructure component of the project may involve land or right-of-way acquisition. However, it is expected that the majority of land acquisitions will be for existing infrastructure assets already listed in the exclusion list. This includes: infrastructure schemes that require physical resettlement of more than 10 households; any subproject that physically displaces more than five (5) percent (by value) of assets of an individual household; any subproject that physically displaces any ethnic minority group or households considered to be vulnerable under the ESMP (e.g. those with limited access to resources); any subproject that restricts resource access that could not be mitigated; and/or (ii) adverse impacts on the livelihoods of households that are of any ethnic minority group, or households that are considered to be vulnerable under the ESMP. The project will seek to support and/or implement involuntary resettlement of local people and village consultation; and new infrastructure schemes will only be implemented if there is no other option. If any, all shall be voluntary, following the ILO Standard 17 which follows the Voluntary Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement (VLAR) principle. The Voluntary Land Donation principle. According to the ESMP, if the screening highlights the need for involuntary resettlement or land acquisition, a Voluntary Land Donation form must be prepared and consulted upon, as well as approved and disclosed prior to subproject implementation.

### ESMP: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources
The ESMP has identified and addressed the risks of potential encroachment of project activities on protected areas. The project will seek to avoid or minimize the disruption of ecological flows with the development of small reservoirs. Encroachment on protected forest areas will be avoided or minimized as the project will focus on reducing deforestation of areas surrounding the proposed reservoirs and other infrastructure schemes, including those in highly protected forest areas. The possible disruption to ecological flows (e.g. migratory fish and water use) can be mitigated by ensuring that appropriate mitigation measures are put in place such as migratory fish assessment and provision of fish ladders where needed, as well as consultation and cooperation with relevant authorities. A detailed environmental screening will be carried out when infrastructure schemes are identified, an EIS screening will be carried out on the proposed reservoirs and other infrastructure schemes (e.g., construction activities of the project (e.g. construction of canals and farm roads) will be carried out outside any protected areas, and in accordance with the relevant laws and regulations of Vietnam.

### GCF: Indigenous Peoples
In order to ensure that the project does not have a negative impact on indigenous peoples living within or adjacent to the proposed activities in the project area, the AE has prepared a comprehensive Indigenous Peoples Plan, and ensured the maintenance of their rights and interests. The project will respect the free, prior informed consent (FPIC) of indigenous peoples. There is a clear 40 per cent project beneficiary target for Indigenous Peoples, including for women. These targets are outlined both in the annual work programme and budget. The AE standardizes the FPIC process to ensure that it is consistent with the FPIC process used by prior or informed consent (FPIC) of the affected Indigenous Peoples. During project development,

### Implementation arrangements

The project will have a national-level project management unit (PMU), which is currently being established in GCF. There will also be provincial or sub-PMUs at each of the five provinces which will manage the public infrastructure components and provide technical services on service provision down to the community level.

The national-level PMU is MRWD and will be responsible for ensuring effective application of the ESMP for all activities. Its responsibilities include providing guidance, training, and capacity building; monitoring and evaluating the project; and national ESF capacity-building consultants - individual or firm - to provide ESF training and technical assistance as required.

The provincial-level PMUs are responsible for the implementation management of the sub-PMU projects, including preparation of project documents and finalize ESF documents; securing IAFD clearance of ESF documents and approval of the government's ESF Implementation Plan (EIP); ensuring that all ESF documents, Project Documents Plans, and Gender Action Plans ensure that the final subject design has incorporated measures to mitigate potential negative impacts during construction and operations.

### Social Engagement Mechanism (SEM)

During project preparation, the project has conducted consultations with potential beneficiaries (e.g., farmers, communities, local government units) through a series of meetings with key stakeholders and other stakeholders in a form of formal household surveys, key informant interviews and focus group discussions. Stakeholder feedback in the form of recommendations related to gender, social inclusion, and environmental sustainability have been incorporated into the project design and approaches.

Further engagements with stakeholders have been planned, including community engagement workshops, which will be held at least once per year for each provincial PMU; training focus group discussions to discuss ESF requirements and subproject development; and meetings with local government units, civil society organizations, and communities at subject-projeto site, to discuss subproject implementation status, GEF and ESF project documents, and other relevant issues. The PMUs will also categorize the project's stakeholders and outline the objectives and strategies for engaging with them.

The ESMP describes the project GEM procedures on the units responsible for resolving complaints related to GBV and SEAR. The provincial-level PMU will also have access to the project GEM. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the HGF safeguard policy, and other relevant policies, the PMUs will not take any action at my stage, including before a claim has been made.

### Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAR) Safeguarding

The GEF Independent Evaluation Unit (IEU) is part of the project's GEM. As all of the project's GEM is considered compliant with the SEAM provisions of the GEF Revised Environmental and Social Policy, The ESMP has been developed in accordance with the GEF SEAM guidelines. The ESMP described the overall mitigation measures, such as mainstreaming GBV and SEAR provisions in all documents and procedures, including the project's GEM, and ensuring that these are consistent with the national guideline for GBV prevention. Specific SEAM risks and safeguard plans will be conducted to ensure that the project is compliant with the GEF SEAM guidelines.

The ESMP outlines a pathway for the establishment of a project-level GEM and outlines a district pathway that explicitly focuses on responding to GBV and SEAR-related complaints. This pathway prioritizes the confidentiality and privacy of the aggrieved parties, including those who are not directly involved in the project or its implementing partners. The AE is recommended to incorporate the information of the GEF Independent

### 4.2 Gender policy

The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore meets the requirements of the GCF Updated Gender Policy. The gender assessment, which was based on both primary (n=65) and secondary sources, is rich in information about the project area and the target group. Most of the data is qualitative (i.e., older than 10 years old), and some references are to areas that are outside the scope of the intervention.

The gender action plan (GAP) follows the structure of the project's overall logical framework, which is an excellent approach. Both quantitative and qualitative indicators are used to monitor progress in the GAP. The AE has a gender action plan for each component of the project. The GCF requires that the AE ensure compliance with the commitments made in the plan and overall gender mainstreaming outcomes of the project. The GAP activities have costing attached to each activity, which is included as part of the total cost of the project and the overall budget of the project. With regard to gender indicators, the AE is encouraged to flesh these out in the early stages of the project implementation, and clearly state in the GAP table for proper accountability.

The project document is clear on discrimination and targeting of ethnic minorities. The project will ensure that women are included as beneficiaries (e.g., through increased participation of women in project activities); by attending project training to improve their current knowledge and skills; by increasing access to credit, land tenure, and other basic services; (iv) increased female participation in household decision-making; (v) reduced household vulnerability to climate change; (vi) improved access to education for girls; and (vii) full use of the advantage of a matrilineal system to increase female involvement in income-generating activities for economic empowerment. The AE also commits to include issues of gender-based violence which will be mainstreamed throughout the project.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The proposed mitigation project for Viet Nam aims to support the implementation of the National Forest Programme (NFP) in the provinces of Quang Binh and Thua Thien Hue, with Central Coast regions of the country. The REDD+ framework promotes activities that reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, sustainable forest management, conservation, restoration of forests and the enhancement of forest carbon stock.

Project funding will provide a GCF grant of USD 35 million, an ADB loan of USD 280 million, and a World Bank loan of USD 190 million. The co-financing ratio is 1:0.17 and accounts for 64 percent of total financing. Furthermore, the project will also benefit from private sector involvement through public-private partnerships and co-investment from the private sector in related infrastructure and agroforestry.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (low risk)

The International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) is an international AE with a mission to reduce poverty by empowering poor rural people, particularly women. IFAD has USD 23 billion in grants and low-interest rate loans to fund livelihoods in fragile and developing


---
**Page 93**
---

### Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

In the proposed project aims to upgrade the host country’s policies and institutional frameworks for the development of a sustainable deforestation-free value chains and performance-based finance. Potential risk to the project's success includes volatility of commodity prices, which could lead to increased costs or reduced revenue from performance-based finance. Higher global commodity prices would raise the opportunity cost of not converting forest land into agricultural use, potentially leading to increased pressure for deforestation-free production methods. Another factor that will affect project success is the ability to attract sufficient financing from microfinance institutions and rural banks to support a deforestation-free value chain. The project will need to address the financial constraints faced by smallholder farmers in meeting their credit needs of individual farmers and find alternatives to traditional financing to support agricultural activities.

### Project viability and concession

Under the component for emission reduction, the proposed project will strengthen forest management through performance-based financing and will develop deforestation-free value chains, among other activities. These activities and related initiatives are well within the expertise of the host country’s institutions and can be expected to be implemented in a manner that is manageable. The proposed GCF grant will represent 36 per cent of total project size. The level of co-financing required for this component is estimated at USD 10 million, which could be catalyzed by the AE and host country. Furthermore, the proposed project is expected to attract an additional USD 25 million in parallel financing and co-investment in infrastructure and agroforestry. The project is estimated to generate a significant amount of emissions reduction over its 12-year life.

### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of financial commitment will be material.

### Compliance risks (medium risk)

Based on available information for this funding proposal, the Secretariat has determined a compliance rating risk of "medium." It is robust Anti-Money Laundering and Counter-Financing of Terrorism (AML/CFT) measures are in place at both levels of executing entities (EEs) and Vietnam, ensuring compliance with Financial Action Task Force (FATF) standards. Extended checks found no significant risks related to money laundering or terrorism financing. The economic nature of financial crime necessitates continuous monitoring and mitigation.

### 4.4 Fiduciary

The grant from GCF to IFAD, and grants from IFAD to the six EEs will be drafted in accordance with annexes 1 and 4 subject to disbursement requests from IFAD to GCF (as per disbursement request forms) and disbursement requests from the six EEs to IFAD (as per disbursement requests), based on the use of 70 percent of the amount already disbursed). Fund flow for the project will be managed by IFAD in accordance with the guidelines based on disbursement requests and financial reports considering 70 percent of the amounts already disbursed.

All provinces and the MARD will maintain Designated Accounts (IBA) in USD for both the GCF loan and GCF grant, and for project accounts in Vietnam dong at the State Treasury. Fund will be transferred from the IBA to the project account in the State Treasury within a province and for the MARD. The treasury system of Viet Nam is reliable for maintaining IBAs and projects and for generating reports on disbursement requests (DRs) and financial reports (FRs). All provinces and the MARD will have procedures and systems to ensure that accurate IBAs are sent to IFAD within the agreed deadlines. All withdrawal application will be accompanied by supporting documents.

All provinces will procure and customize accounting software for the project needs. The software must comply with the Vietnam Accounting Standards and inventory management principles. The accounting system will be customized to generate GCF (expenditures split by expenditure category, outcomes, outputs, and activities), IFAD, and government-required reports. In addition to the above, all provinces will submit monthly financial statements and quarterly financial reports using the GCF template and guidelines within 30 days from the end of each period.

Private sector auditors will audit project financial statements annually. The project will submit audit reports to IFAD within five months of the end of each period. The terms of

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

- As a mitigation initiative, the project aims to reduce 60 Mt CO2 eq over 12 years of project lifetime (2018–2030). The project has been designed with a high confidence in achieving mitigation impact potential in the agriculture, forestry and other land use sector with a clear intervention logic and well-conducted baseline and scenario analysis.
- The theory of change is based on the assumption that the project will have an impact as well as mitigation impacts, but the advice from the Secretariat to the AF on this point has changed over the past year. It was therefore decided not to include adaptation in the theory of change at this time since this would not serve any practical purpose at this point. Instead, adaptations co-benefits are identified along with mitigation outcomes, with relevant indicators in the logical framework.
- The project is designed as a high-cost-effective intervention that will have an impact on land use and the analysis of land-use change scenarios is detailed and credible, giving us confidence that this will be a high-impact, low-risk intervention with results that will enhance our overall cost-effectiveness in delivery.

On the basis of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU), both have expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, any claims or disputes against the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

The decision to approve a project or activity for funding by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Submission by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval confirming that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals needed by it and any other entity involved in the project or activity;

(b) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval, or the date the Accredited Entity receives the Board decision confirming that it has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and

(c) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP250

## Proposal name: Achieving emission reduction in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast of Viet Nam to support National REDD+ Action Programme goals (RECAF)

### Accredited entity: International Fund for Agricultural Development

### Country: Viet Nam

### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

1. This funding proposal* for a medium-sized, public sector mitigation project in central Vietnam is submitted by the Government of Viet Nam through the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) as the accredited entity (AE). The executing entity (EE) is the Government of Viet Nam, acting through the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development. The target provinces are Dak Lak, Dak Nong, Gia Lai, Lam Dong and Ninh Thuan provinces.

2. The project aims to support the implementation of National REDD+ Action Plan (NRAP), as a key element of Viet Nam's nationally determined contribution (NDC) 2022 to the Paris Agreement. NRAP is aligned with Viet Nam’s national climate change strategy, namely, sustainable management of forests, and the conservation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks in order to achieve emission reduction from deforestation and forest degradation in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast of Viet Nam to support National REDD+ Action Programme goals (RECAF). It has two outcomes, designed to reinforce each other.

3. Funding proposal package for "Achieving emission reduction in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast of Viet Nam to support National REDD+ Action Programme goals (RECAF)", received from IFAD on 6 September 2024.


---
**Page 98**
---

### Outcome 1: Viet Nam’s REDD+ design policies are fully upgraded and integrated into provincial policies and institutional frameworks with improved public-private-producer partnerships (4Ps) to operationalize REDD+. RECA will support REDD+ phase I activities at national level, by updating and enhancing existing national REDD+ policies and mechanisms to meet specific requirements of the new REDD+ Action Plan (MAP), including: (i) strengthening forest governance; (ii) protecting existing natural forests, especially in areas susceptible to deforestation and forest degradation; (iii) ensuring that benefits from REDD+ activities are shared equitably among all groups of forest-dependent communities; and (iv) establishing sustainable, deforestation-free value chains and infrastructure in agricultural communities linked to deforestation processes (feasibility studies will be conducted for the establishment of a National Forest Monitoring System [NFMS] which will be implemented through public-private-producer partnerships [4Ps] platform to be created and integrated into existing institutional frameworks). The NFMS will be linked with Viet Nam’s National REDD+ Action Plan (MAP), which will be implemented through public-private-producer partnerships (4Ps) platform to be created and integrated into existing institutional frameworks. The NFMS will also be linked with the national forest value chains, and linked to Viet Nam’s Payments for Forest Environmental Services (PFES) with enhanced carbon credits.

Activity 1.2: Mobilize resources for NFMS implementation and inclusive investment planning and integration into existing institutional frameworks

### Impact potential Scale: Medium

The proposed project aspires to have a mitigation impact of 6,684,33H tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO₂ eq.) through the activities of Outcome 2 in the five target provinces, over 1.25 million hectares.

- **Mitigation impact potential:** set out in annexes Z2a (describing the methodology) and annex Z2b (providing the technical details of the calculation using the GCF AF-ExC tool). This is to be achieved entirely through enhancing carbon stocks, through carbon sequestration (e.g., agroforestry, perennial agriculture, sustainable forest management, and forest restoration. The mitigation impact potential is calculated based on the project’s estimated activities to prevent or slow down deforestation or forest degradation.

#### Focus on carbon enhancement
The Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) notes that the project proposal uses GCF AF-ExC and much of the language of the funding proposal, including the key outcomes, refer to "achieving emission reduction." This is misleading because the project’s activities are focused on enhancing carbon stocks. It is otherwise referred to as carbon removals or carbon sequestration, which is a completely different pathway to mitigation impact. This should be clarified in the documentation before project approval.

The funding proposal suggests that the project may have an indirect positive impact on reducing deforestation and forest degradation through the activities of Outcome 2. However, this is not reflected in the project’s estimated mitigation impact calculation.


---
**Page 100**
---

### Paragraph 101 of the funding proposal explains the focus on carbon removals (and not emissions reductions), stating that the attribution of the reduction of deforestation and forest degradation to REDD+ is a complex issue. The project will therefore focus on carbon sequestration in a period which has already witnessed more stringent policies (e.g., logging ban in 2016) and increased enforcement, as well as an increase in the number of protected areas. This analysis refers to the period 2016 to 2018. Data for business-as-usual scenario would thus refer to a period prior to 2016. The project will also focus on the potential of Vietnam to fill a significant niche in the mitigation efforts of Viet Nam in support of the REDD+ Action Plan and the NDC, which is to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (EDG) and degraded lands, taking carbon out of the atmosphere (rather than preventing it getting there).

#### LEAF Coalition and RECAF: The RECAF project is clearly aligned with wider national efforts to address climate change. It will be implemented in close collaboration with the Accelerating Emissions Forest Finance (LEAF) Coalition, and the results-based payments (REBPs) under this initiative are expected to cover 100% of the project costs, amounting to USD 51 million. It is noted that the RECAF work is in the North Central Coast of Viet Nam and has no geographical overlap with the proposed RECAF project.

#### The RECAF project will focus on two main areas: (i) reducing emissions from deforestation in the South Central region and Central Highlands during the 2022–2026 period. LEAF/Emergent will pay for this service with a minimum price of USD 10/tonne of CO₂, resulting in an estimated payment of USD 5 million per year; (ii) increasing carbon sequestration is 4.26 million hectares (h), including 3.24 million ha of natural forest and 1.02 million ha of plantations.

#### The intention is that the LEAF interventions focus on reducing emissions, while the RECAF project focuses on carbon enhancement, thus pursuing distinct but complementary strategies to address climate change in Viet Nam. The funding proposal states that “given that RECAF is focused on REDD+ activities in the same provinces as targeted under LEAF, there will be a need for careful consideration of potential synergies and possible complementarity.” An agreement between MARD and IIFAD has been prepared in which MARD would commit to supporting the implementation of the RECAF project through its existing LEAF jurisdictions and RECAF. (See footnote 20 on page 238 of the funding proposal.)

#### In the question-and-answer session with the AE, the TAP asked about the risk of double counting of carbon removals in Viet Nam, which could be happening if they were being funded twice – by GCF and by buyers on the public or private carbon markets. In response, the AE clarified that the project will not be considered as double counting, given its alignment with the activity period between 2012 and 2026. Results-based payments are to be reinvested in forest conservation activities in Viet Nam. The TAP also asked about the potential of RECAF to increase carbon removals beyond the project’s target. In response, RECAF covers different ‘activities’ – agroforestry, perennial agriculture and improved forest/land management. By not including avoided deforestation… this allows for a clear separation between the two projects in terms of their respective contributions towards carbon removals in these landscapes.

#### The TAP also asked if Viet Nam has left the option open of selling 76 per cent of the RECAF project’s carbon removals, as part of the project’s exit strategy. “RECAF will assist the Government of Viet Nam in reinvesting these benefits into further REDD+ outcomes, aligning with the national REDD+ Action Plan and NDC,” the AE responded. This means that 76 per cent of the carbon removals will have been ‘paid for’ by GCF, with the remaining 76 per cent of the


---
**Page 101**
---

carbon credits are free to be sold on voluntary carbon markets or through other mechanisms. This approach appears consistent with accepted practice internationally, and is motivated by the AI as follows:

* The total carbon emissions resulting from the implementation of the project’s activities has been estimated at (684,338 t CO2 eq over 12 years). These ERs are resulting from the USD 76 million invested in component 2 (REDD+ phase 2), including USD 18.5 million from GCF [2% of the total investment]. The estimated emissions reductions that can be attributed to GCF, representing 1,603,200 t CO2 eq.

* However, and given that the BPs are part of the exit strategy of the project, and an incentive for the communities to continue with forest conservation from deforestation in the first place, it is expected that the remaining ERs (i.e. 5 Mt CO2) are generated thanks to investments from the different components of the project. The USD 10 million invested by GCF, World Bank, and IFAD will be used by the government of Vanuatu to seek REDD+ BP or other type of carbon market certification for these remaining ERs. This approach is consistent with accepted practice internationally.

* Presentation of mitigation potential: The IAP/ERF document proposes project has a significant mitigation potential, with 6,680 Mt CO2 eq (equivalent) almost half of the conditional land use change and forestry (LULUCF) emission reductions by 2030 that are set out in Viet Nam’s NDC. The methodology used to estimate this mitigation potential is robust, and the assumptions behind the emission factors are meticulously recorded in the calculator spreadsheet.

* However, the use of the calculator sub-module 5.1 on “Forest management and degradation” for capturing the mitigation impact of community forest management and forest restoration activities is not consistent with the project baseline situation. The baseline situation is without the project. If the baseline situation has that forest degradation has stopped, it is not logical to expect that any further reduction in emissions will be secured without the project. If the baseline situation involves some degree of ongoing forest degradation, then the project interventions are necessary to halt and reverse that degradation. In order to determine whether this is the case or not, it is important to look back at whether, and to what extent, the project is proving successful in slowing and eventually halting forest degradation. This issue has been included in the suggested actions outlined in the last section of this assessment.

* In addition, there are a number of weaknesses in the presentation of the funding proposal that could lead to confusion about how to join the project with its mitigation potential and the project interventions towards Outcome 2, particularly the agroforestry intervention. The funding proposal does not provide any information on whether it is able to report with any certainty on the actual mitigation impact achieved. These issues are outlined below, and the weaknesses in the funding proposal could be strengthened through addressing them before signature of the funded activity agreement for the project.

* The project theory of change has a missing step in the results chains, suggesting that investments in infrastructure, capacity-building, market access, finance etc. lead directly to carbon sequestration. This is not supported by any evidence and it would not be able for this sequestration to occur. This analysis is captured in the IAP/ERF Figure 2 below.

# No detailed spatial targets are included in the narrative for the various interventions on the ground. The narrative is designed to communicate how increased carbon sequestration. There is a need to have more specific spatial targets foregrounded so that they can inform spatial planning and decision-making on planting and harvesting, through to reporting and verification (MRV) of results. This will allow progress towards these goals can be tracked, reported and compared with these indicative targets, in order to assess if project activities are having the desired impact.

* Shift from monocrop coffee tree to 30% crop shaded coffee
* Shift from cocoa trees to intercropping with cashew and/or cassia spice trees;
* Shift from monocrop cashew trees to cashew intercropped with lemongrass plants;
* Shift from bare land or degraded annual cropland to diverse agroforestry systems, such as mixed plantations of coffee, cocoa, cashew, lemongrass, guava, durian and avocado (to maximize yield and sequestration);
* Shift from monocrop rubber trees to intercropping with perennial harvesting, focused on pulpwood production, to mixed plantations with nine-year rotational harvesting for logwood production;
* Shift from degraded bamboo-timber forests with unplanned harvesting to well managed plantations with agreed fellling rates; and
* Shift from degraded or abandoned secondary forest to well informed and equipped community forest management, including enrichment planting.

## No monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) system for the project interventions included in annex 11 (monitoring plan) or annexes 22 (mitigation estimates). The RECAF project is designed to be implemented on degraded systems. In order to track the results of the RECAF project and understand how these can be attributed to the GCF/JAF/DAF investment government, there is a need for area site visits, and verification of the extent of land under low-emission practices. This will allow the project implementation period (and ideally by MARD and provinces) for the full 12-year project cycle. The RECAF project narrative should be amended to incorporate 4 on the numbers of hectares "brought under low-emission practice", but this needs to be broken down into a detailed matrix for meaningful monitoring, with project


---
**Page 103**
---

(personnel directly engaged in the process). The indicator wording could also be adjusted to reflect the enhancement of carbon stocks instead of the lowering of emissions.

(a) No deforestation/degradation monitoring system is in place in the agroforestry landscapes to monitor and report on deforestation for agrobiodiversity/personal agricultural use. This is a significant gap, as it means that deforestation could be seen as a direct perverse result of project activities, in response to new market opportunities created by the proposed agroforestry system.

Even if the assumption is made that deforestation and degradation are not ongoing, because of the lack of monitoring there will be no way to know whether or not corrective actions can be taken if necessary. This kind of cross-checking is particularly important for projects in which there are multiple stakeholders who are claiming to be "deforestation-free," building on other donor-funded pilots.

(b) The narrative element of annex 22 setting out the anticipated carbon removals, while correct, does not provide a summary table of the figures that have been entered into the spreadsheet. There is a need for a much more detailed summary table than the one provided in annex 22. This will allow the project team to see at a glance how the outputs are derived. As one example, the figures in the management module (in sub-module 5.1, Forest management and degradation) can only be understood by adding rows from tables of the three modules for each of the two key outputs (Outputs 2.1 and 2.3). This is essential for the project team to understand how the outputs are derived and to check that the new planting and harvesting regimes which they will need to plan spatially with communities are consistent.

(c) The agroforestry extension strategy is described only briefly in the funding proposal and Project Implementation Manual (annex 21) and no breakdown is provided in the budget for this activity. It would be helpful if there was a more detailed description of Output 2.1 (agroforestry), with similarly little detail on extension in Output 2.3 (conservation agriculture). The project team will need to decide how they can ensure quarter more carbon will be incentivised in the ways described in the project, but also come up with an approach for ensuring that farmers who are not involved in the project with producers on their traditional knowledge, needs, preferences, perception, and concerns, if significant behavioural change is expected to result (for example less frequent harvesting of trees). The project team will need a clear strategy for the delivery of training, setting out the methodology and approach to shifting behaviour. This should be linked directly to the extension strategy to ensure that the desired shifts on the ground, and thus fulfilling the mitigation impact potential.

No plan is in place for climate-adaptive approaches to agroforestry and forestry management. The annexes provide a brief description of the project team which states that annex 2 now includes downloaded assessments providing insights on temperature and rainfall projections for the region. However, there are no plans or strategies set out with Assessment Report, with differential impacts for drought and flood in the project landscapes. There is also no plan to use the climate-adaptive approach (Climate Zone modelling tool) in estimating the potential impacts of climate change on the proposed agroforestry system. This could be a significant risk as the proposed agroforestry approach which could compromise not only the sustainability of the agroforestry systems, but also the overall mitigation impact of the project.
