# 1.3 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium

The proposed project aims to bring about paradigm shift through several features:

- Building the enabling environment for REDD+ results-based payments; scaling up forest land allocation and tenure security; supporting a jurisdictional approach to developing deforestation-free value chains; sharing and learning among stakeholders.

## 21. Building the enabling environment for REDD+

The project will enable a paradigm shift through the development of policies and practices that will provide an enabling environment for the transition to sustainable forestland management, forest restoration, and agroforestry systems. The project will also contribute to the wider national and provincial systems for claiming results-based payments - for both reduced emissions from deforestation and degradation (REDD+) and avoided deforestation.

## 22. Scaling up forest land allocation: Activities to improve tenure rights of local farming communities are intended to address a barrier to long-term investments in sustainable farming systems on forest lands, which will be critical to the success of the project. The project will make a contribution to solving enduring disputes and improving security of tenure over areas that have been identified as suitable for agroforestry.

## 23. Potential for knowledge-sharing and learning: Activity A.1.2 has been designed to facilitate knowledge sharing and learning among project stakeholders across the project area, and with the wider public. In addition, the 4P platforms at provincial and district levels will provide a forum for dialogue on forest land allocation and tenure security; sharing and learning among stakeholders.

## 24. Potential for knowledge-sharing and learning: Activity A.1.2 has been designed to facilitate knowledge sharing and learning among project stakeholders across the project area, and with the wider public. In addition, the 4P platforms at provincial and district levels will provide a forum for dialogue on forest land allocation and tenure security; sharing and learning among stakeholders.

In addition to the regulatory framework, increased capacity of forest protection authorities will enable them in better address forest land-use


---
**Page 105**
---

conflicts of digital encroachment, increased knowledge on viable agroforestry systems, and increased capacity of financial service providers will provide an enabling environment for financing long-term investments in climate resilient farming systems and deforestation-free value chains. The private sector is expected to play a key role in this transition. Private sector investment will be considered as the basis for co-creating initiatives that are environmentally sustainable and rooted in local communities.

### 20. Private sector engagement: A critical aspect of scaling up is the engagement of domestic financial institutions and investors in value chains. Section C.1 of the funding proposal anticipates that the private sector will provide a significant share of financing through direct or indirect private sector co-investment in activities initiated by the IFP partners and SMEs. Investment in production systems, such as agroforestry, is expected to be the main driver for scaling up. Section C.1 will engage commercial banks to build their capacity to develop products, partnerships and applications of financing technology that support such investments by producers. It will also support the development of a credit risk assessment tool to create creditworthy development funds, to access new capital and make microfinance loans to its users.

The AE stated that they had "conducted multiple meetings with potential commercial bank partners, and they are now in discussions with them." The AE further noted that the private sector is more engaged even so more now as there is increasing State Bank of Vietnam (SBV) pressure to make “green” loans, and SBV has been encouraging banks to engage in agroforestry.

### 21. Sustainability of payment for forest ecosystem services: The area where success and scale-up seem least certain is that of the development of a performance-based mechanism for payments for environmental services (PES). The PFS model being proposed as an alternative, a mechanism would be at a trial in Viet Nam. According to the funding proposal, it would “provide real and tangible benefits to local communities” by providing them with income from open up potential for accessing international REDD+ financing and public and private carbon markets (financial proposal paragraph 112), by providing an alternative to other equitable benefit sharing. The proposed PFS model is based on the existing Provincial Forest Protection Development Funds, and aspires to “create an enabling environment for scaling-up of forest protection and restoration activities through a performance-based, voluntary and data-informed process, and can be more easily linked to Viet Nam’s National Strategy for REDD+.” It is envisaged that the PFS model will be set up to be set up through policy work in Output 1.1, and the mechanism itself in Output 2.1, to facilitate scaling-up.

The AE acknowledges the extensive research, thought and consultation that has gone into the planned intervention to develop a results-based PFS mechanism (as reflected in the feasibility study). The AE notes that the PFS model is different from the current model, which “basically contracts community labour for patrolling forests” and “does not provide any financial incentives or support for forest management decisions” (funding proposal paragraph 112), as “not linked to forest protection or regeneration performance.” However, the AE notes that this is a resource-limited project and welcomes. The likely sustainability of the free standing PFS model, to be established through GCF capitalization grants totaling USD 1.27 million (budget line R27), can be questioned, however, as it is not linked to any other funding source or revenue stream in the medium term. For a sustainable paradigm-shifting outcome of the RECAP project, the PFS would need have been integrated into the broader six-year program and its budget.

# Sustainable development potential

## Scale: Medium to high

The proposed project is aligned with the United Nations 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, including contributions towards the country's efforts to meet Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 1: End poverty in all its forms everywhere; SDG 2: End hunger, achieve food security and improved nutrition and promote sustainable agriculture; SDG 3: Ensure healthy lives and promote well-being for all at all ages; SDG 5: Achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls; and SDG 13: Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts.

### Environmental benefits

The project will promote the transition of monocrop perennial cash crops to agroforestry systems and the use of other sustainable agriculture practices, such as crop rotation, intercropping, and mixed farming. This will help reduce soil erosion and land degradation. Transitioning on small-scale farmers and use of nitrogen-fixing species should reduce chemical fertilizer inputs by 50% or more in the long term.

### Health benefits

The project is expected to have a positive role on dietary diversity among target households through the introduction of new crops, such as fruit trees, nuts, herbs and medicinal plants into current monocrop coffee or pepper and in agroforestry systems. The inclusion of these species will increase the number of food items consumed by families, which will increase the nutritional value of the farm products.

### Gender and social inclusion benefits

The project includes gender equality through: (i) increasing women's access to land and other resources on their home; (ii) involving women in project training to improve current farm knowledge, financial skills, and learn new knowledge about agroforestry systems; (iii) providing women with opportunities for leadership participation in household decision-making, thanks to improved farm skills; (iv) reducing women's workload by increasing the efficiency of farm operations through better tools and work arrangements based on seasons; and (v) increasing female involvement in income generation activities for economic empowerment. The Gender Action Plan incorporates specific

# Adaptation co-benefits: The project summary (paragraphs 5–7) and section B.2 states that role of women in forest-dependent communities is critical for building resilience of forest-dependent communities. Although the feasibility study (annex 2) describes climate change impacts in the five provinces, the funding proposal narrative fails to identify the specific adaptation co-benefits that will be achieved by the project interventions. The project interventions will reduce vulnerability or exposure to these hazards. Paragraphs 5–7 mention that the project will focus on watershed management, erosion control, soil fertility management, and the supply of non-wood forest products (NWFP) in order to build resilience against climate change and other extreme weather conditions. The funding proposal outlines a framework for developing adaptation interventions that are based on participatory assessments with communities to monitor and evaluate the effectiveness of the co-benefit adaptation measures, ensuring they meet international best practices and are sustainable under changing climatic conditions.” (Funding proposal paragraph 52.3) It is unclear where this framework can be found.

It is noted that farmer training under Output 2.1 will include training on the utilization of climate information services, including weather forecasts and early warning systems for forest fire information; and also that the proposed project will complement and coordinate with the work done through the ongoing GEF-funded project (FP125) titled “Strengthening the resilience of smallholder farmers in the Red River Delta, North Central Coast Region, Northwest Highlands and South Central Coastal regions of Viet Nam 2020-2026” implemented by United Nations Development Programme (UNDP). The funding proposal states that this project is a sister project, undertaking similar investments in irrigated agriculture and agroforestry, but motivated through different mechanisms.

### Land tenure co-benefits: The second key co-benefit of the project relates to land tenure reform. The project involves piloting the forest land allocation (FLA) of encroached and/or degraded forests in five provinces (Lai Chau, Dien Bien, Lao Cai, Ha Giang, and Quang Binh) with communities forestry and agroforestry. Security of tenure is expected to be improved through FLA to 150 large community forest land areas (CFLAs), including those managed by ethnic minorities), with improvements to the enabling environment for scaling up this process. As provided in the funding proposal, a pilot will be conducted involving provincial forestry boards and communities to address land tenure issues. Guidelines for FLA processes, including FPFL, will be developed, including processes to document customary claims, define criteria by which allocation of forest land is made, and ensure that the rights of poor and disadvantaged households and communities are adequately represented.

The project will also involve the development of 100 km² of protected areas for protection, degraded forest for regeneration and sustainable harvesting of forest products, as well as delineated areas on which agroforestry systems can be developed. This will

also continues to the empowerment of ethnic minority communities and facilitate their integration in the formal economy, including the signing of contract farming agreements with buyers of deforestation-free commodities and of securing formal loans for investments in agriculture and other sectors.

While these important interventions are welcomed – both for the 150 pilot communities and the wider enabling environment – the AE notes that Barrier 3 (funding proposal paragraph 25) remains a significant challenge to scaling up forest landscape restoration projects. In response to questions from the TFAP on how progress towards forest land allocations will be monitored, the AE noted that this is not yet clear in the project document and that if it does not unfold successfully, the AE notes that “as for how the land allocations will be monitored and reported, this needs to be clarified.” The AE also highlighted the importance of transparency and clear documentation at community, district and provincial levels and beyond, to ensure open and inclusive land allocation.10

### 1.5 Needs of the recipient Scale: Medium to high

As highlighted in the funding proposal, Viet Nam’s updated NDC of 2022 contains an unconditional emission reduction contribution in the land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF) sector of 34.8 Mt CO₂ eq by 2030. The conditional LULUCF target is 51.6 Mt CO₂ eq. In addition to these targets, Viet Nam has committed to a further 79.5 Mt CO₂ eq under the LULUCF are an additional 141.8 Mt CO₂ eq by 2030. Based on the initial submission, RECAF seeks to contribute almost half (64.8 Mt CO₂ eq) of the conditional LULUCF emission reductions by 2030. This is equivalent to 57% of Viet Nam’s unconditional target and 19% of its conditional targets.

The provinces of Dak Lak, Dak Nong, Kon Tum and Lam Dong, and the province of Ninh Thuan in the South Central region are the focus areas for RECAF.

43. The AE argues that GCF investment will be a critical component to domestic financing allocated for REDD+ and to support implementation of key policy reforms and targeted investments in forest landscape restoration projects. The AE also notes that there is a need for institutionalizing much needed inter-sectoral and multi-stakeholder approaches to reform forestry management, including the development of a national forest product strategy and market products, and to lay out the fundamentals of an enabling environment implementing the action plan.

45. GCF grant financing will be deployed in the poorest, forested areas of the country where institutions are weakest; local ethnic minority communities lack access to public and private funds; and there is limited capacity for project implementation. The AE notes that support other smallholders in poverty-reducing agricultural and forestry supply chains and their adaptation to climate change through the use of agroforestry systems, as well as providing grant finance from IFAD, focused on providing the necessary infrastructure, through provincial governments, to farming communities.

46. As a landlocked country, Viet Nam has achieved strong economic growth of an average 6.4 per cent in gross domestic product (GDP) over the last decade, and the debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to decline from 50% in 2019 to around 38% by 2030. However, it assigns the responsibility of servicing external debt obligations to provinces, with strict limits on loan investments. To address these challenges, the Government of Viet Nam is seeking a grant of US$47 million from GCF to support the development and implementation of a climate-oriented land, presents a strong platform for Viet Nam to achieve the project objectives. The AE will continue to monitor progress in this regard.

### 1.6 Country ownership

#### Alignment and ownership:
The proposed project aims to implement the National REDD+ Action Plan (NAP) 2017 in the Central Highlands provinces of Dak Lak, Dang Giai, Gia Lai and Lam Dong; and the province of Ninh Thuan in the South Central Coast. The project is aligned with the national REDD+ strategy and the National Strategy for Sustainable Forest Management. It aims to reduce deforestation and forest degradation by strengthening community forest governance, a jurisdictional approach to deforestation-free supply chain management of major commodities, development of agroforestry, and enhancing the resilience of the target beneficiaries.

#### The project is also well aligned with the government development target to move rural populations out of poverty. The current 2019–2025 Country Strategic Opportunity Programme focuses on smallholders and small and medium enterprises in agriculture, in underserved areas with a concentration of ethnic minorities.

The project has been designed through a lengthy and participatory process, following a proposal by the Government of Vietnam to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) via the World Bank – international access entity and co-financer of GCF. After formal endorsement of the revised concept note, each province formed a project development board, collected baseline data and prepared a provincial action plan. The communities in the target sites were extensively consulted; they participated in the two IFAD design missions, during which extensive discussions were held with provincial governments and local stakeholders. The letters of consent from the provincial governments are attached in annex 1, indicating their willingness to enter into a partnership agreement with IFAD. It is important to note that the communities will not take a loan, but will finance the same level of infrastructure development itself.

#### Civil society engagement:
Although local and international civil society organizations of varying sizes were consulted during the project design phase, extensive consultations held with these organizations in developing the project, with consultation focused on governance issues. The target sites are located in 21 districts across five provinces; 353 male and 301 female were consulted at community level in 21 districts across the five provinces, of which 353 were male and 301 were female. Outreach involved a blend of household surveys, key informant interviews, focus group discussions, and participatory mapping exercises. The consultations focused particularly on how to better encourage participation of ethnic minorities, women and youth (in age-appropriate ways) in the project design process and in addressing grievances and grievance redress. During implementation, stakeholder engagement will be sensitive to ethnic


---
**Page 110**
---

**minority community needs and preferences, as well as differentiated to the needs of vulnerable persons in the project area.**

52. Free, prior, and informed consent (FPIC) consultations were conducted with ethnic minority groups and Kinh people present in the project areas, providing information on the proposed project activities and their potential impacts on the environment and communities, including preferred approaches to avoiding, minimizing and mitigating social and environmental risks associated with the proposed project activities. As part of the FPIC consultations, key project information was disclosed at an early stage so that affected communities could provide feedback on the project design and its impact on them. This is particularly important in relation to the potential target ethnic minority and Kinh communities who were expected to benefit from, and may be impacted by, the proposed project activities. Once the environmental and social safeguards are finalized, FPIC consultations through provincial project management units will be conducted to incorporate improved project information and solicit meaningful feedback on the revised project areas and activities.

17. Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

- Of the project’s total proposed budget of USD 76.08 billion, 34 per cent (USD 25.9 billion) is proposed to be a GCF grant; and 66 per cent (USD 40.18 billion) is made up of co-financing to the project, closely woven into the project logic. The co-financing is composed of USD 20.64 million from the World Bank, USD 357.9 million from the Asian Development Bank, and USD 38.5 million from the PFGs as well as a grant for infrastructure investment in the province of Dak Nong. Major co-finance of USD 1 billion is provided by the Government of Vietnam (GoV) to support the PFGs to undertake infrastructure investments in target communities, in support of agropastoral, peri-urban culture, and forest fire management and forest restoration.

### IFAD loans and their linkages

- IFAD loans are linked directly with the project’s logic in two areas: deforestation-free agroforestry value chains and community forest management. For agroforestry value chains, the loans will finance investments in the following areas (a total of 12,000 ha): small reservoirs for water storage and supply for crop cultivation; irrigation canals and piped water systems (upper and lower catchments); and irrigation stations for crop cultivation. Other investments will include small-scale rural roads (upgraded from earth roads) connecting producers collecting points/warehouses; micro-watershed management; agroforestry value chain strengthening; and ecosystem infrastructures and facilities. Investments in support of community forest management will finance the following areas: firewood collection stations; seedling nurseries; fire watch towers; water points for forest fire fighting; forest protection stations; forest park development; and other infrastructure and facilities (see annex 21). The final list of eligible loan types in annex 21 does not include the post-harvest agri-processing infrastructure, which is critical to the value chain strengthening logic. The AE is encouraged to make sure it is on the list.

- Given the centrality of the IFAD loans to the project’s logic and also its efficiency and effectiveness, it is essential that the loans be linked to the actual producer needs on the ground for agroforestry value chains and community forest management. This will be done in accordance with the project implementation manual (annex 21) of clear steps for planning and selection of infrastructure, which indicate that infrastructure provision will be demand-driven, as well as the table of investment priorities (annex 20). The priority areas for investments will be identified and prioritized through the process of value chain action planning in connection with the AE’s work plan.

# Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP believes that the project "Achieving emission reduction in the Central Highlands of Vietnam" is aligned with the Government of Viet Nam to support National REDD+ Action Programme goals (RECAP®) and has the potential to achieve significant reductions in carbon stocks in the target provinces.

The ITAP also notes that the AE should adjust considering the project title to more accurately reflect the chosen project strategy, which aims to achieve carbon enhancement, rather than emission reductions. This will avoid confusion and show clearly the distinct focus of this investment on GCF’s FTA and the Government of Viet Nam in contradiction to other initiatives.

The ITAP recommends that the funding proposal be submitted with the following condition precedent to the execution of the funded activity agreement:

(a) An updated theory of change diagram reflecting the on-the-ground results which are currently missing from the results chains, and are essential in explaining how mitigation impacts are achieved;

(b) A detailed set of spatial targets per Project province for implementing the planning and harvesting patterns used in calculating the intended carbon enhancement;

(c) A measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) plan, whereby the achievement of the mitigation impact is measured against a baseline and reported to GCF, as well as being fed into provincial and national MRV systems;


---
**Page 113**
---

**(a)** a strategy and plan for annual monitoring, through the annual performance report, of the rate of deforestation and forest degradation in agroforestry target districts, together with a narrative explaining any positive or negative links between emerging trends and the Project’s objectives.

**(b)** an additional key, accompanying annex 22 to the funding proposal, explaining precisely the linkages between the hectares over which specific planting and harvesting patterns are executed, and the corresponding carbon stocks in the soil, biomass, and above-ground vegetation; and

**(c)** a strategy for delivery of agroforestry and forest management extension and training, setting out the methodology and approach to shifting production and harvesting practices, in a manner which is respectful of local and traditional practices, but is also designed to achieve specific behavioural change for enhancement of carbon stocks.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP250)

## Proposal name:
Achieving emission reduction in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast of Viet Nam to support National REDD+ Action Programme

## Accredited entity: International Fund for Agricultural Development

## Country: Viet Nam

## Project/programme size: Medium

### Impact potential
Thank you for the positive feedback. (TAP mentioned [point 5] the need to change the title of the project to align it with the content. This important issue is extensively discussed in the “Overall Remarks” section.)

In addition, TAP recommends including in the supplementary report: (i) an updated Theory of Change, (ii) a description of planned climate-smart interventions, (iii) an MRV plan, (iv) an annual deforestation and forest degradation monitoring system. IFAD welcomes these recommendations and looks forward to further discussion on how best to integrate them into the project design.

### Paradigm shift potential
RECAF has considered a short-term investment in the PFES mechanism as a short-to medium-term measure for intermediate farmers during the transition to more sustainable practices which is by essence a transitory phase, with expectations that the new production practices will result in enhanced revenues. Some countries outside Vietnam, such as Cameroon for instance, have already adopted this approach. The PFES model allows for gradual transitioning gradually toward more sustainable practices, combining different types of mechanisms including financial incentives and capacity building. This is particularly relevant during the transition period, to the compliance with environmental requirements.

However, we acknowledge that the PFES mechanism may not be suitable in all contexts as it typically requires sustained funding to achieve lasting, transformative impacts, as short-term models are more likely to result in a return to business-as-usual practices. Thus, an approach that leads to a flexible-benefit-sharing approach could make short-term investment more viable, but long-term replenishment of the PFES remains at the same time the preferred solution for lasting success.

### Sustainable development potential
Thank you for the positive comments.

**Page 115**
---

The co-benefit adaptation indicator will be developed during project implementation following the methodology for climate resilience impacts outlined in the Funding Proposal section B.2. The DoP under the MARD will be responsible for defining and monitoring these indicators, as part of the MRV plan once the project is underway.

### Seeds of the recipient

Well noted, thank you.

### Country ownership

Well noted, thank you.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

In paragraph 42, ITAP requested clarification on why the final list of eligible loan types in Annex 21 excludes post-harvest agricultural facilities. The reason is that under the new ODA management system (OAS) regulations (Circular No. 15), HAD loans can only be used for infrastructure projects making both private and public infrastructure ineligible, including post-harvest agricultural processing and storage facilities as well as tourism and ecotourism infrastructure aimed at generating income for local communities and promoting sustainable development in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast of Vietnam. These types of projects were excluded from the investment list, in line with the policy's focus on public infrastructure and alternative development projects.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

The request of ITAP to change the title of the project from "Achieving emission reductions in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast of Viet Nam" to "Viet Nam National REDD+ Action Programme goals (RECAP)" to "Achieving carbon enhancement in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast of Viet Nam to support National REDD+ Action Programme goals (RECAP)" is noted. The ITAP also requests that the project title be changed to reflect the national and regional agreements already signed, as well as financial and legal binding commitments made by Vietnam with respect to these agreements. At this point, ITAP does not consider that changing the title is possible. For this reason, we should gently deny the request.

We would like to thank ITAP for recommending the approval of RECAP to the GCF Board.

RECAP will provide a GCF’s supplementary report including:

i) an updated theory of change diagram;

ii) a detailed set of spatial targets per Project province; for implementing the carbon sequestration activities;

iii) a measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) plan;

iv) a strategy and an annual deforestation monitoring plan.

vi) a strategy for delivery of agroforestry and forest management extension and training.

# Executive Summary

## Background
A gender assessment had been conducted during June–November 2021 and May-July 2022 amid resurgence of COVID-19 in Vietnam by mid-2021. The assessment aims to understand, to the extent possible, the socioeconomic status, culture, and livelihoods of project's target groups which include men and women, ethnic minorities, workers, and farmers. The assessment was carried out through extensive collection and analysis of secondary data (national, regional, provincial level) and primary data (project level). Primary data were collected through consultation with about 654 people, of which 90% are female and the remaining 10% are male. All consulted people live in 21 districts of 3 project provinces in the Central Vietnam. These districts are identified (through data analysis under the project) as hotspots for deforestation.

## Purpose
The gender assessment aims to understand how gender relations, roles, and responsibilities affect men and women's access to resources, thereby proposing actions through a gender action plan to promote gender equality and empowerment for women among project’s target groups. Since most of project beneficiaries are ethnic minorities, the Gender Action Plan and Indigenous People Plan will be implemented in collaboration with the provincial government and other stakeholders, particularly by each of the five crop project provinces. The gender minimization under the project will be planned and carried out in a manner that contributes to achieving the expected targets over the project life. This is based on the assumption that the project’s target groups are not only affected by climate change but also by the social assessment and gender assessment that have been done under the project. Consultation with potential beneficiaries (known as affected people) and other stakeholders (interested groups) takes the forms of household survey, focus group discussion, and key informant interviews.

## Key findings
The results show that the socioeconomic status of target groups in this project are very poor and as such vulnerable to the potential impact of climate change, particularly to their current agricultural production which makes up 77% for animal husbandry, 26% for human husbandry, and 65% (average) for crop production. The average annual income per household is VND10-30 million ($480–$1,460) and average productive land access among female headed households is lower than that of male headed household. However, for youth respondents, there is no statistically significant difference in monthly average income between men and women (Section 3.2). The gender gap exists only when comparing across all types of land asked under the household survey (including Personal Land, Annual Cropland, Production Forest, Protection Forest, Agri.-Agric. for Animal Husbandry and Residential land), only 58% (n=182) have both husband and wife’s names on their LURs (See Section 3.3.2).

In terms of labor division, the decision to work outside the home is due to social norms by which female typically spend most of their daily time for unpaid domestic works whereas male spend most of their time for paid work and social activities outside their home. Almost most of the decision in financial matters are made by men (Section 3.2). The gender gap exists only when comparing agricultural production appears to be mainly taken by mostly by males as they are more knowledgeable about cultivation practices, innovation, labor, and market due to being more socialized (See Section 3.3.3 and 3.3.4). Women are less likely to have access to credit facilities from banks or other financial institutions; b) protecting their business from being harmed, and charging for better prices. They are also less likely to be able to visit friends, relatives in nearby area (villages, neighbouring province) as they have to ask for permission of their husband (See Section 3.3.5).


---
**Page 124**
---

# Recommendations

Based on the results of the gender assessment it is highly recommended (through the Gender Action Plan and Indigenous People Plan) that female members be included during all project planning and implementation activities that benefit them at both household and community level. For ethnic groups that are of matrilineal societies, this system will be used as an advantage under the project to promote the participation of EM women in decision making process and increase their representation in leadership positions. To ensure effective inclusion of female participation in project activities (which deviate from the current labour division and social norms), the following key steps are recommended:

- Ensure at least 40% of female participating in all project planning activities – be it intended to benefit women or men at both household level, including female headed households,
- Reduce women's burden from traditional household chores by:
    - Exploring and promoting activities that help women save time and labour including dry/dry reduction mechanisms.
    - Exploring the potential of small-scale practices that involve mechanisation to free up intensive and heavy labour. This can be shared in groups of people that typically share similar tasks.
    - Encouraging male to share more domestic works (towards equal sharing) to enable female to take trainings outside their home.
- Provide funding and capacity building trainings, e.g., technologies, leadership training, financial literacy, etc. to women who are interested in value addition activities that make them more comfortable/ skilled for niche products such as bee keeping, sericulture, weaving, natural dyes (which can be introduced/ included in agro-forestry interventions).
- Gender sensitive selection of project staff and partners including facilitators and champions;
- Promote female participation throughout the whole process of government lead forest land allocation (FLA), including joint ownership of forest land to promote decision making on the part of women.

The above key recommendations aim to promote equal participation of female from project's target groups, which would enhance female participation in project planning and implementation activities and foster the process of economic empowerment for female members.

# INTRODUCTION

## L1 Project Goal

The project is entitled "Achieving emission reduction in the Central Highlands and South Central Coast of Viet Nam to support National REDD+ Action Programme goals" (RECAF). The RECAF project's goal is to reduce GHG emissions from AFOLU sector, contributing to the climate-resilient sustainable development of forest-dependent communities in the target provinces. To achieve this goal, the project will implement a set of interventions.

### Goal Statement

II Viet Nam’s REDD+ design pillars and NRBP are fully upgraded and integrated into provincial socio-economic development processes with effective public-private-producer partnerships (PPPs) and financial and legal incentives to operationalize deforestation-free value chains for REDD+. The project will also support the implementation of a national forest management framework across the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast and beyond to establish a pathway towards low-emissions sustainable development, BECAUSE the necessary policy and institutional frameworks, including equity, and financial means will have been placed to reduce GHG emissions from the AFOLU sector while contributing to the climate-resilient sustainable development of forest-dependent communities.

The project focuses on developing deforestation-free value chains, fostering alternative livelihood options, and improving governance in the target provinces. It aims at reducing the vulnerability of forest-dependent communities as described further below. The Theory of Change diagram (Figure below) visually depicts these approaches and illustrates the anticipated outcomes of the project's interventions.

### Project target groups

It is estimated that RECAF will reach 420,000 direct beneficiary households who are mainly smallholders and vulnerable individuals, and a total of 1,000,000 people who will indirectly benefit from the project. Specifically, the project's target groups include: (i) rural poor households with land and laborers; (ii) unskilled employed rural poor; (iii) rural poor lacking land for sustainable production; (iv) women-headed households; (v) youth; (vi) people displaced by conflict; and (vii) youth to promote agroforestry/commercial agricultural production. Vulnerable households, such as the poor, near poor and/or women-headed households, youth and others identified through local SEEDP and RAP mechanisms will be prioritized among the target groups. RECAF will also prioritize reforestation activities in vulnerable areas identified by Disaster Risk Management planning/zoning; and ethnic minority households will be prioritized among the target groups. RECAF will propose specific measures to ensure women's participation in relevant activities, including minimum participation rates (at least 40%).

In addition to above target groups, the project will also include small and medium scale enterprises/agri-businesses (SMEs), and producer organizations (cooperatives, Common Interest Groups (CIGs)), savings and credit groups, and so forth, in project supported non-deforestation value chains.

**Page 129**
---

# I.4 Project area

The project will cover five provinces, including Gia Lai, Dak Lak, Dak Nong, and Lam Dong in Central Highlands, Ninh Thuan in the South-Central Coast (See Figure below). Within five project provinces, districts selected for project intervention have a total of 920,660 hectares of natural forest, of which about 85% become protected areas. Thus, no further protection is needed anywhere, and no project impact can be expected there. Thus, it is estimated that project activities will contribute to the protection of about 500,000 hectares of natural forest.

# I.5 Project's potential impacts

## 1.5.1 Positive impacts

Impacts will, overall, be positive, including (but not limited to) the following key benefits:

At community level:
- Knowledge


---
**Page 130**

* Access to improved sustainable farming knowledge (climate smart agricultural practices, different crop rotations, good agricultural practices, GAP Standards) that are gender-sensitive and lead to women's empowerment.

* Bio-inoculant and LEISA-based practices introduced for improvement of soil health and productivity.

* Improved capacity building (knowledge management, leadership, and also to enhance women’s confidence for embarking on business enterprises)

### Soil and Landscape
* Improved soil health, diversified farming and landscapes, thereby increasing productivity of crops and livestock and promoting integration of environmental management practices.
* Local communities will have deeper knowledge and understanding of local soil types/micro-habitats and hence can contribute to effective interventions.

### Infrastructure
* Access to infrastructure and facilities to improve agricultural production efficiency and promote adoption of deforestation-free supply chain and help in reducing drudgery.

#### Credit Loan
* Access to loans for smallholders those who are willing to adopt climate-smart farming practices.

### Income generation and business development opportunities
* Job opportunities (on-farm and off-farm) for both men and women.
* Job and skill development opportunities for youth (particularly for those who are disadvantaged), including promotion of agricultural SMEs, on-the-job training and apprenticeship programme.

### Networking
* Become part of forestry and farmer groups for mutual support/learning.
* Become part business partner with private sector.
* Become part of saving/credit group for mutual support/business partnership (including MFI) women development fund, currently in Dak Nong and Ninh Thuan, to be replicated).

### Gender equality
* Improved participation of men and women in project planning, implementation, M&E.

#### At provincial and national level:
* Mainstream REDD+ into SDUP


---
**Page 131**
---

### Adverse impacts and risks

Will be minor, localized, limited, manageable and reversible,

- Potential minor temporary environmental impacts due to minor upgrading of small-scaled irrigation canal, drainage, road, power line, access road, production facility, infrastructure and equipment for water supply system, fire protection stations, fire watch tower, pumps, and other public infrastructure foreseen under the project.

Can be avoided or minimized through proper design and construction measures,

- Can be avoided through a) consultation and b) exploring ways to engage EM peoples into project design and implementation, and c) participatory monitoring during project implementation.

#### Social and Environment Risks

- Improper storing, handling, and application of pesticides and other chemical inputs
- Possible increased use of chemical fertilizers and pesticides due to crop intensification which may raise risk of environmental and human health deterioration.
- Compromised food security and nutrition for EM peoples
- Contraction of diseases (e.g. HIV/AIDS, sexually transmitted diseases, other communicable diseases... COVID19, due to labor influx)
- Conflicts with local communities
- Gender based violence (e.g. verbal and physical abuse, sexual harassment, violence against children...)
- Unequal access to project benefits/costs if eligibility for selection of beneficiaries do not exist, or exist but not well applied
- Possible social exclusion (because of vulnerability, land tenure issues)
- Agroecological degradation due to commercial value chains
- Declined emphasis on food crops, resulting in reduced dietary diversity with serious implications for nutritional security and human health, particularly of pregnant women, mothers, mothers and children due to increased shift towards commercially important cash crops and agroforestry,
- Inflation
- Traffic accidents

# METHODS

## Purpose of gender assessment

The purpose of this Gender Assessment (GA) is to understand the status-quo of key gender-related issues at country, particularly those at project levels, and on the basis of that proposes gender-responsive actions that will be realized under the proposed project – through mainstreaming gender issues into project design and to promote gender equality through project activities during project implementation.

The gender assessment and gender action plan aim to: 1) support climate change interventions and innovations through a holistic gender mainstreaming approach; 2) Promote gender equality through climate change mitigation and adaptation interventions whereas minimizing social, gender-related and climate-induced vulnerabilities of women and men in the context of Vietnam. The project is based on current inherent social, economic and environmental vulnerabilities and exclusions as well as potential impacts induced by the climate change in Central Vietnam. Above gender actions are done through FPS gender mainstreaming process that has already started during project design stage and will be maintained and enhanced during project implementation.

## 2.2 Methods

The gender assessment starts out by, first, exploring and analysing the gender roles and norms in Vietnam that shape how men and women undertake roles in their daily income generating activities and other relevant activities at household and community levels. A particular focus is placed on the project’s target group (women and men) and the specific gender-related issues and challenges they face present in the project area. Second, the gender assessment was conducted on the basis of the social assessment of the project's potential risks and impacts. The social and gender assessment aligns with the logical framework approach to ensure that the project is designed with a gender equality lens and social inclusion plan in particular. While the social assessment contributes to project design through avoiding/minimizing adverse impacts and risks, and enhance project development effectiveness, the gender analysis provides an understanding of how to mainstream gender issues into project activities, thereby promoting gender equality through project investments.

### Data Collection Tool

A set of questionnaires were prepared by the project design team for the purpose of Focus Group Discussions (FGD), Key Informant Interview (KIE), and Household Survey (HS). The questions were translated into Vietnamese language by local government staff. Staff selected to provide conduct these surveys were trained through Zoom and were provided sufficient time to practice through follow-up online sessions to ensure they well understand the structure and purpose of each questions and learn how to ask such questions in a way that is culturally appropriate to local people, particularly people from ethnic minority groups.

### Sample Size and Sampling

For the household survey, a sample of 353 households was taken. The percent of male interviewees is 58.7% and that for female is 41.9%. To ensure representativeness from 21 project districts, a typical


---
**Page 133**
---

commune in each district was selected for the survey (including FGD, KII and HS). A stratified random sampling was adopted in each select commune to enhance the representativeness for potential project beneficiaries.

Table 1 - Number of research participants

| No. | Type of consultation | Male | Female | Total number of people participating |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Household Survey | 205 | 387 | 592 |
| 2 | Focus Group Discussion | 70 | 107 | 177 |
| 3 | Key Informant Interview | 55 | 46 | 95 |
| Total | 330 | 295 | 628 |

In addition to primary data collected through household survey, focus group discussion, and key informant interview, secondary data such as research papers, media reports, statistical books, technical reports, community development report, etc. were collected for a better description and analysis of the socioeconomic context of the affected households, and their community at large.

Data Collection

Focus Group Discussions (FGD): FGDs were conducted in small groups (8 persons per group). FGD aimed to collect information on the current status of deforestation, the challenges, constraints and experiences to discuss pre-defined topics. This information was collected to support the gender analysis and gender assessment baseline, and the preparation of the required social and environmental baseline data. All FGDs were conducted in local languages with trained local governmental staff. The group facilitators introduced topics for discussion and helped groups to participate in a lively and natural discussion amongst themselves. FGD aimed to obtain the insight of local people on their own community and its environment, as well as to identify the inconsistencies and variations that exists in their community with regards to their beliefs, knowledge, attitude and practices with regards to their agricultural and forestry productions and their associated livelihoods. The information collected was used for the preparation of the baseline data for each household.

Key Informant Interview (KII): KII aims to collect information about select pressing issues and problem in the target community – through individuals who are locally known as experts in the topics under question, and who have a broad social network with individuals and parties who play certain roles within their communities. The KII is used for collecting information on a commodity that are likely be supported by the project to promote deforestation free value chain.

Household Survey: Household surveys were administered to invited representatives of local households using questionnaire uploaded through personal mobile devices of local government staff when possible.

Languages: All FGD were conducted with the language assistance of local governmental staff who are from the same ethnic minority groups. This person was full time with each of the FGD to ensure participants from ethnic groups are comfortable using their own native languages and both languages in order that communication for the purpose of the focus group discussion.


---
**Page 134**
---

# COVID-19 Prevention in Dak Dong and Ninh Thuan Provinces

The above surveys were conducted amid the surge of COVID-19 (June-July 2021). Thus, national and provincial measures for COVID-19 prevention were entirely observed by local government and local governmental staff who were directly involved in the surveys. For Gia Lai and Dak Lak provinces, surveys were carried during June and July 2022.

## Data Analysis

Mixed method was applied for the gender assessment to enhance the reliability and the validity of the study findings.

### Focus Group Discussions (FGD)

Qualitative data obtained from focus group discussion were analyzed using content analysis.

#### Household Survey

Household survey data was collected through questionnaire prepared by project design team and designed into KoboToolbox platform. KoboToolbox was selected because it is a free, open-source software for collecting and managing data of this survey, and it is free. Data collected on field were submitted to KoboToolbox platform after each day of household interview. Data were cleaned using Microsoft Excel and were analyzed using the statistical package for social sciences (SPSS) version 25 and Statistical Package for the Social Sciences (SPSS). Descriptive statistics was conducted to obtain frequency for all responses collected through the online survey.

#### Key Informant Interview (KII)

Data collected from KII were reviewed and analyzed to support the meaningfulness of data collected from FGDs, particularly for the topic related to Contract Farming.


# 3. GENDER ASSESSMENT

## 3.1 Country Overview

### 3.1.1 National Policies Related to Gender Equality & Climate Change

Gender equality has been mandated in the latest Constitutions of Vietnam (2013). It is furthered by the Law on Gender Equality No.73/2006/QH11, which was approved in 2007. Its aim is to address gender equality, particularly women's rights and women's empowerment through legal frameworks to allow consistent mainstreaming of gender aspects into all relevant laws. In addition, the National Strategy on Gender Equality 2021-2030, the Action Plans on Gender Equality 2013-15, 2016-20, and other legislation defining the responsibilities for the implementation of the Law.

Various Decrees and Directives were promulgated in relation to gender equality. To facilitate the implementation process, some laws have been amended or new ones need to be incorporated into existing realities for mainstreaming gender aspect to promote gender equality in applicable laws. Some prominent laws that promote gender equality include: Law on Marriage and Family (2014), Law on Domestic Violence Prevention and Control (2007), and Labor Code (2019).


---
**Page 135**
---

In addition to legislation frameworks, national strategy on gender equality have been unfolded. The latest national strategy for gender equality (2016-2020) was built upon the success and lessons learned from previous (2005-2015). The strategy aims to ensure the gains of gender equality through creating favorable conditions to promote equal participation of men and women in various fields of social life, covering six key targets including political, economic and labor, gender based violence, health care, education, and family planning. In order to implement this national strategy on gender equality, national action plan on gender equality have been prepared to guide the implementation of the national strategy. In terms of state management, at provincial level, special people committee, prepare specific gender equality implementation plans in areas such as agriculture, industry, education...to promote more specific gender mainstreaming effort in activities of their own sector.

In addition, National Target Program also make an effort to mainstream gender into their respective programs, such as National Target Program on New Rural (2012-2025), on Sustainable Poverty Reduction (2013-2020), on Comprehensive Labor Market Development (2016-2020), Pollution Resolution and Environmental Improvement (2012-2015) and for the period from 2016-2020 for radical resolution for public sensibly polluting establishments. At provincial level, provincial people committee also published Action Plan on Gender Equality to realize National Strategy on Gender Equality.

At international level, Vietnam has ratified various conventions related to promoting gender equality. Some of them include Forced Labor Convention, Equal Remuneration Convention, Discrimination (Employment and Occupation) Convention.


Climate Change/Natural Disaster

The Government of Viet Nam has a strong legal framework in place to respond to the potential impact of climate change. Resolution 24-NQ-CTW dated 3 June 2015 issued by the Communist Party is a high-level directive on climate change. The Resolution strengthens the roles of relevant agencies in responsible for: 1) climate change adaptation, 2) natural disaster prevention and control, resource management; and 3) environmental protection. It focuses, in particular, on climate change adaptation and serves as the foundation for planning directions and policies for socio-economic development, ensuring sustainable development and poverty reduction. The Resolution also provides guidance on the draft National Strategy on Climate Change for the period from now up to 2060.

The Law on Natural Disaster Prevention and Control (2013) states: gender equity is one of seven core principles of disaster prevention and control. The document provides guidelines of how natural disaster prevention and control should be carried out in a manner that ensures equal participation of relevant agencies in natural disaster prevention and control. It prescribes activities of natural disaster prevention and control, the rights and obligations of agencies, organizations, households and individuals participating in natural disaster prevention and control, and state management and available resources to ensure achievement of this effort. Although there is no specific guidance on


---
**Page 136**
---

gender mainstreaming in the Law and Decrees under this law, there has been Guidance that has been issued by IARD and General Women’s Union on Gender Mainstreaming in Community Based Natural Disaster Prevention and Control (for communities).

National Green Growth Strategy for 2021-2030: vision toward 2050 (No. 1658/QĐ-TTg), dated October 1, 2021. Its overall target is to "accomplish green growth, thereby contributing to the restructuring of the economy in conjunction with renewing the growth model, in order to achieve economic development that is sustainable and inclusive; contribute to the realization of a good life for all people; and contribute to a good and carbon neutral economy; contribute to the realization of the goal of reducing global warming". The target is achieved through the following six guiding views:

1. Green growth contributes to the restructuring of the economy in conjunction with renewing the growth model, thereby contributing to the resilience and the ability to withstand external shocks, as well as the actualization of the Strategy for socio-economic development in the 2021-2030 period, the system of national master plans together with the development strategies of provinces and cities.
2. Green growth is an important method to carry out our sustainable development and directly contributes to the reductions in greenhouse gas emissions as a step towards a carbon neutral economy.
3. Green growth places the people at the center and helps reduce human vulnerability to climate change; encourages each person to maintain a lifestyle that is responsible to the community and the environment, while contributing to the creation of a harmonious coexistence living culture while also establishing a civilized and modern society that is in harmony with nature and the environment.
4. Green growth must be based on modern institutions and governance, advanced science and technology along with high quality human resources, and it has to suit the international context as well as the conditions within our country.
5. The green economy is characterized by the use of advanced technologies, digital transformation along with smart and sustainable infrastructure; creating momentum so that private investments play an increasingly important role in the green economy.
6. Green growth must be based on a spirit of innovation and cooperation to foster people, the business community along with related organs and organizations; it is promoted through a spirit of innovation and the aspirations to develop a prosperous and sustainable country.

3.1.2 Status Quo and Results of National Policy Implementation

Despite strong commitment to establishing a legal framework, with Law on Gender Equality as a pivotal law that has been enacted various sections, gender is still not integrated systematically into the laws and policies related to agriculture, food and nutrition security and related indicators are not sex-disaggregated (FAO 2019).

3.1.2 Gender Stereotypes

At global level, in 2021, Vietnam was ranked 87th out of 156 countries) on Global Gender Gap Index. The country ranks 4th lowest (from 73rd in 2020) on Economic Participation and Opportunity; but 9th (from 93rd in 2020) on Educational Attainment, 152nd (from 131st in 2020) on Health and Survival and 121st on Political Empowerment (WEF Global Gender Gap Report 2021). At the regional level


---
**Page 137**
---

(East Asia and the Pacific), Vietnam was ranked 9th overall on the Gender Gap Index. At country level, though improvement has been made at policy and action levels over the past decades, gender gaps still exist for a variety of various reasons. Gender stereotypes and social norms are among the key reasons (UNDP 2018).

Social norms act as a powerful mechanism that regulates conflicts in groups, even in cases where there is no central authority (Axend 1983). Whether people observe a norm or not would depend critically on two factors: the degree to which they perceive themselves as being evaluated by others for conforming to social norms that are conditional on both types of expectations being present (Beechert 2006). In Vietnam, the social norms, which form part of common behaviour, are that men are leaders, decision makers and breadwinners in their family. Women are expected to take care of household tasks, and decision making within their family. Also, patriarchal attitudes and deep-rooted gender stereotypes regarding responsibilities and roles of women and men in the family and in society result in subordination of women (SDG 2015). For example, girls are often discouraged from pursuing education or career work in the family. Similarly, girls are often involved in doing housework than boys do. Because of the women's responsibilities in chore, women aged 25 and older tend to choose jobs close to home to enable them to balance their job and caring role (SDG 2015).

It his the peak in 2018 at 74.3 million people, and then decreased to 65.9 million during COVID 19 (WR, ILO 2021). ## 3.1.5 Labor Division in Production

In agriculture and forestry, women engage in the most of the production activities, including sowing, crop care, to harvesting. They also manage income-expenses and are responsible for selling products. On the other hand, men are mainly involved in land preparation and the use of pesticide, which is generally considered physically heavy (ISDS 2015).

The labor division, which is rooted from social norms and the roles of men and women in relation to their physical health, affect how they access necessary resources for production/income generation and related activities. These include access to land, technology, inputs, credit, markets, education, food and nutrition, including access to resources for agricultural production such as land, technology, training, finance and markets, and agricultural extension services albeit their high partition in agriculture. In addition, women are more likely than men to be involved in the division of labor within the household. Women also have limited access to credit due to kinship-based practices of inheritance. The most vulnerable women were found to be the ones from patriarchal groups and those living in rural and mountainous areas.

It is noted while agriculture remain constant (100%) over the period from 2008-2018, forestry slightly decreased over the same period whereas fisheries slight increased.

### 3.1.7 Political Participation

According to WEF (2021), albeit progress made, Viet Nam still need to improve gender parity in politics since only 11.3% of the Political Empowerment sub index gap has been closed, further regressing since 2020 (-1 percentage point). Although 26.7% of parliamentarians are women, to date there are still no women ministers, and there has never been a woman head of state.

### 3.1.8 Gender-sensitive Assessment

Matriarchy or patriarchy in ethnic minority groups:

Communal organization and kinship are an important aspects that characterize key cultural traits of other groups in Vietnam. Matriarchy and patriarchy—other matriarchy or patriarchy—are traditional fundamental that affect the marriage customs and practices among ethnic groups in Vietnam. It also affects how men and women access to assets, exercise power and decision making over the use of such assets, as well as inheritance, marriages, and other things. As such behaviors of members in each ethnic group, particularly in the area of asset ownership, inheritance, marriages, among other things.

**Practices of matriarchy**

For some ethnic groups in Vietnam, matriarchal is historically characterized by a society in which women hold power and control over the resources. They are the ones who proposed a marriage. The Ede, Kho, M’Nong, Cham, Raglay, for example, are well known for the matriarchal, and these marriage practices are common among them. For example, women from Kho and Cham are the ones who propose to marry. When a husband dies, his wife will stay with her mother’s family as well as other aspects. If there are divorces, men leave the family with nothing. When the husband die, they will be buried in their mother's hometowns. In terms of inheritance, heritage is also passed down from mother to daughter. For example, if a woman dies and leaves behind a child, her assets such as tools and equipment are turned back to her mother or to his sisters if his mother has passed away. This social arrangement is typically derived from the fact that


---
**Page 141**
---

Gender equality from the perspective of ethnic minorities

From the above, one may ponder that men are somehow just laborers in the family in matriliney societies, and women are more likely to be responsible for household assets, inheritance, etc. However, within these societies, this may not be considered a misconception.

Ethnic minority communities do not maintain the concept of gender equality as the one defined in the development field. For EM people, husband and wife assigned their work based on their physical ability and social status. In other words, they do not consider "gender equality" for them would be “being together,” “caring for each other,” “working together,” “sharing responsibilities” between husband and wife. This is what they perceived as an ideal family (Pham 2012:7).

It is true that in the forest or rural settings, men are commonly involved in undertaking heavy works such as cutting down trees, hunting, fishing, farming, building/repair houses. Thus, domestic works, albeit with more devotion of time, is considered light duties inside the house. Women, as such, consider it necessary for them to do these works to maintain a “harmonious” family (Pham 2012:6).

Water collection and treatment remain a cumbersome activity especially for ethnic minority households (UNWomen 2016).

Job opportunities for EM women

Currently, national policies are contributing to creating better opportunities for some groups of female workers, such as in labour-intensive exports. However, these policies seem to fail in granting sufficient job opportunities for ethnic minorities and women with low levels of formal education in general, and from disadvantaged rural regions in particular. These issues seem to be more serious for the case of women of ethnic minorities. It is also noted that unpaid domestic work and care at the major data gaps in Viet Nam, especially with regards to time use and informal employment is also higher among ethnic minority women (UN Women 2016).

UN Women (2016) recommends that particular attention should be given to the plight of ethnic minority women and girls, who continue to lag behind both ethnic minority men, and Kinh and Chinese women in accessing economic opportunities, and facilitate women’s access to employment outside jobs in sectors traditionally seen as female.

Land use titles

In Vietnam, it was presented in the Land Law 2013 that where the land use rights, or land use rights and the house ownership, and other assets attached to land is the joint property of both husband and wife. full names of both husband and wife must be written in the certificate of land use rights and house ownership when there are two people (Pham 2012:6). In this case, only


---
**Page 142**
---

one person is written on such certificates. If required, such land use right certificate can be updated to indicate the names of both husband and wife if the land is joint property of both husband and wife.

Even though there has been an increase in the number of female-only and joint holders of land use title for non-residential and residential land since early 2000s, the number of formal entitlements over non-residential land by male was lowest among ethnic minority groups that practice patrilocal succession (UNDP 2013).

### Agricultural extension

Women have fewer opportunities to access training and field workshops. Men are overrepresented in technical trainings involving new livelihood strategies (e.g., shrimp farming, orchard plantation), now machinery and cultivation techniques and non-traditional crops that are seen as commercially promising (UN Women, 2016). Women’s limited access to rural advisory and agricultural extension services, combined with their lack of education and literacy, results in lower levels of education, increased time constraints and limited mobility, among other constraints (FAO 2019). Furthermore, although women are more involved in agriculture, they are also less likely to work on smaller farms and to cultivate subsistence crops. By contrast, women farmers remain confined to more traditional types of training such as small-scale animal husbandry (FAO 2019).

### 3.1.9 Gender Based Violence

- **Domestic Violence**

At national level, domestic violence in Vietnam has been a critical social problem for more than the past decade. In 2015, the National Study on Domestic Violence against Women in Viet Nam, conducted by GSO with technical support from the WHO and MOH, found that 58 percent of ever-married women report they have suffered physical, sexual, and emotional violence from their husbands at least one of these types in their lives; 27 percent of women had experienced any of these types of violence within the last year. The study also found that 10 percent of women reported their husband’s violence to a family member or friend (other than themselves) while 35 percent reported their husband’s violence to a health worker or police officer.

In 2019, the above survey was repeated. It was found that 61.1% of respondents in Viet Nam who had ever been married or married/partnered reported that she had experienced physical violence from her husband/partner during her lifetime (lifetime violence), and 46.5% experienced physical violence from her husband/partner within the last year (last 12 months). The percentage of women reporting lifetime violence was experienced by 13.3 % of women in Viet Nam in their lifetime, and for 57.5% of women this occurred within the last 12 months. Common reasons for physical violence include “family problem” (50.8%), “anger” (49.6%) and “jealousy” (49.3%). Among those who experienced physical violence from a husband/partner, only 7% of women reported that they had told someone about it; 21% sought help from friends or family members; 50% did not seek any help at all.

It was noteworthy in the 2019 survey than, with regards to emotional violence, more women reported their experience of emotional violence by a husband/partner compared with any other


---
**Page 143**
---

form of violence: 47.0% have experienced it at least once in their lifetime and 10.9% within the last 12 months. 27.3% of women experienced one or more forms of extreme behaviour (a form of psychological abuse) during their lifetime, while 6.2% did so in the last 12 months. When controlling behaviour is combined with physical, sexual and emotional abuse, the violence rate rise to 60.2 per cent during lifetime and 27.8 per cent in the last 12 months. 20.0% of women experienced economic abuse by a husband/partner during their lifetime and 11.5% in the last 12 months.

The national survey also found that gender-based perceptions on violence significant contribute to men's and women's acceptance of violence. Cultural beliefs about masculinity and femininity also influence people’s attitudes towards violence against women. For example, in rural areas almost one third of women in rural areas are more likely to agree that men were the decision-makers and household head than those in urban area. 51.8 % believe that a man is justified in beating his wife if she were unfaithful. Women who have experienced violence during their lifetime or within the last year, or of violence were also more likely to justify and excuse perpetration of violence by a husband/partner compared with women who have not experienced violence. These facts were also more strongly supported by people living in rural areas compared to urban areas and among women of lower education.

* **Child Abuse**

In terms of child abuse, almost one in four women with children under 15 years old reported that their children have been physically abused by the partner. Violence against children also has a strong relationship with violence against women. Women who experienced physical or sexual violence from their abusive acts are typically not incidents but often are repeated behavior, with physical and sexual violence against women usually starting early in a woman's relationship when sexual and emotional violence appear to be more common. The presence of children in the home, especially if they are young, women participating in the national survey, affects them even more than physical or sexual violence. It was reported that women who experienced physical and/or sexual violence by a husband/partner were more likely to report uncommon behavioural issues of their children such as frequent nightmares, unusually quiet or withdrawn.

In terms of economic and social disadvantage, Violence against women often disrupts women's ability to work resulting in a risk of loss of employment, a decrease in income, and increased isolation. It was found that women who experienced violence were more likely than those who did not experience it to have even more limited access if they are living with violence. This is even more so for those who do not work or rely on husband as breadwinner, particularly most vulnerable women who are poor, or poor, and having a disability, early in life of ethnic minority. If they are not empowered to mobilize resources as a preventative measure, they are more disproportionately vulnerable to events such as natural disaster and climate change, in the long run.

* **Sexual Harassment**

At national level, there is no official and systematic data on sexual harassment. Sexual harassment was also challenging to record since it may take the forms of physical, verbal or non-verbal harassment. In a survey conducted by the International Labour Organisation (ILO) in Vietnam, in 2015, up to 17 per cent of mid-career workers interviewed (n=150) said either they or someone they knew in their

### 3.10 Impacts of Climate Change

Viet Nam is sixth most affected country in the world by climate change. Each year, intense and unpredictable weather events cause fatalities and extensive damage to infrastructures, and impact on the livelihoods of already disadvantaged communities in urban and rural areas in which children and women are particularly at risk (UNICEF 2021 website, retrieved July 9, 2021). The level of impacts of climate change is not gender neutral. Women and girls face greater risks due to their lower socio-economic status, socio-cultural norms, access to resources, poverty as well as gender and there has been growing research evidence that demonstrate that the effects of climate are not gender-neutral. In fact, the effects of climate change are more severe for women (WB 2021).

In terms of numbers at global level, the World Food Programme (WFP) estimates that the risks of hunger and child malnutrition on a global scale will increase by 20% to 2050 if adaptation action strategy are not in place (WFP 2015). The two primary drivers for malnutrition is the lack of fruit and vegetables in diets, and the loss of income from agriculture. Women are more likely than men to be food insecure as they are less able to access resources and communities are least able to afford water storage, irrigation infrastructure, and farming technologies which assist in their adaptation strategy (WB 2021).

Stakeholder consultations revealed that due to extreme events (heavy rainfall, prolonged dry periods) poor farmers often lose their seeds. In many cases these crops are often damaged or die, necessitating a second sowing. Farmers, particularly the poor, do not have seeds saved and face difficulty in sourcing seeds for a second sowing. Seed Banks or mechanisms for sourcing seeds in such situations is critical as failure to sow can lead to food hunger or starvation for the household unless means for income generation are available through wage labour or other options. This is not always possible in rural communes particularly in EM.

### 3.11 Impacts of COVID-19

**Impact on Labour Force**


---
**Page 145**
---

The three waves of the Covid-19 pandemic in Vietnam in 2019 and 2020 had resulted in unemployment for millions of workers in Vietnam. In 2020, for instance, 3.1 million Vietnamese workers aged 15 or above lost their jobs or faced their working hours reduced (ESO 2020). Despite recovery, by the first quarter of 2021, there are still 9.1 million people aged 15 and over across the country who are adversely affected by the Covid-19 pandemic. Of 9.3 million workers, 540,000 people lost their jobs or faced reduced working hours in the first quarter of 2021 due to business operations, 3.1 million workers having worked with reduced working hours or required to work on a reduced schedule or take shifts. 6.5 million workers reported having experienced lower income. It is expected that the fourth wave of Covid-19 in Vietnam (fourth wave) will continue to hinder the recovery effort of both workers and governments.

In terms of sectors, workers in the service sector were the most severely affected (20.4%), followed by industry and construction sector (16.5%), and agriculture, forestry, and fisheries (7.3%). In Central Highland provinces, where the pandemic spread faster than other regions, and among those least affected group, the impact on services (which are among the top 5 major sources of income in Central Highland) may cause a cascading effect onto those whose major income are from agriculture – from consumption to production. The number of workers who lost their jobs or faced reduced working hours due to Covid-19 pandemic in Vietnam has not only slowed down the labour market recovery (achieved in the second half of 2020) but also left many workers, especially women, unemployed and become informally employed. The number of people who were informally employed as a result of the pandemic was dropped to 49.9 million (99% thousand lower from the previous quarter) and was mainly in the rural areas and among men. It is noted that there are still 3.5 million workers engaged in subsistence work in the agricultural sector, which accounts for up to make up nearly two-thirds of this group, primarily those aged 50 and over (accounting for 59.4%).

It was also noted when migrant poor workers lose jobs in urban area, they tend to return home in rural area to ease the cost of living in cities (e.g. house renting, food costs and other services). Accordingly, many of them are unable to find work or earn enough money from their jobs to accept unstable jobs as they do not have many job-opportunities compared to men. They have no choice but to accept less stable jobs as long as they can ensure earnings for themselves and their families.
