• **Impacts on GHV**
COVID-19 left hidden impacts disproportionately on women. Data from Vietnam Central Women’s Union indicated that during the period of social distancing, the number of calls to their hotlines increased by approximately 30 percent compared with the same period in the previous year and by nearly 47% compared to the same period of the previous year. In economic terms, growing empirical evidence from Vietnam and other countries suggested evidence of induced impact of COVID-19 on women for instance:
• Women’s burden across all spheres were not only heavier but also more dangerous.
• They bear greater burdens of unpaid care work which limits their economic participation and suffer increased incidences of gender-based violence during crises.
• They will endure a worsening of their burdens until COVID-19 is well under control, and for a long time after.

Women expect their labor income to fall by 50 percent more than men. Women are 24 percent more likely to lose their job permanently than men. Women tend to reduce their current consumption and increase savings. Women are also more vulnerable in terms of economic hardship as they are generally in more precarious employment than men to begin with.

- **FEMALE HEADED HOUSEHOLDS**

At country level, the percentage of female headed households is 21.80%. In Central Highland, it is 23.7 which is among the two lowest regions. However, it appears that the rate of female headed households has increased over the past ten years – from 18.6% in 2008 to 23.7% in 2018.

**Figure II - Female Headed Households**

| Year | Male | Female |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2008 | 18.6 | 25.8 |
| 2010 | 24.3 | 25.2 |
| 2012 | 27.9 | 21.1 |
| 2014 | 29.8 | 20.8 |
| 2016 | 29.7 | 23.7 |

**Data source: GSO 2018**

Also, it is noted that female headed households could result because of death of the husband, but if a childless woman marries and her husband dies before she does, or if a woman with the husband migrated out may still be counted as male headed, but in practice, it is female headed for all practical purposes. Census surveys thus overlook these "invisible" female headed households and they are included in the category of other housing, invisible female headed households.

# 3.3 Project’s Target Groups

## 3.3.1 Demographic Information

A total of 353 households participated in the survey; 41.9% of survey participants (n=148) are female and the remainder (58.7%, n=205) are male. Survey participants represented five provinces including Dak Nong, Dak Lak, Gia Lai, Ninh Thuan, Lam Dong (See Figure 15 below). Of the total 1,235 people surveyed, 49% were female and 51% were male. The average age of survey respondents was 36 years old.

Of the 353 total persons who answered the household interview, 218 (61.8%) are household head, of which 28.1% (n=77) are female-headed households. Most of household heads (71.7%, n=235) were born in 1940 or earlier and only 10.5% (n=36) were born after 1980 (See Figure 16 below). The average household size is 4 person (min=2, max=9, SD=1.2). The average age of survey respondents is 38 years old (min=17, max=85, SD=11.5). The average age of all household members reported by 353 survey respondents was 36 years old.

Three largest ethnic groups participating in the survey include Ba Na (18.7%, n=66), Rade (17.8%, n=63), Mnong (16.4%, n=58); followed by Khmer (11.3%, n=40), [Paa]2 (10.2%, n=36), Ede (9.1%, n=32), Nang (4.2%, n=15, etc. In terms of religion, more than half of the survey participants has no religion (57.8%, n=204). The second largest group is Buddhism (25.6%, n=92) followed by Christians in Vietnam including Protestantism (24.9%, n=88), Catholicism (0.9%, n=3); Buddhism (1.4%, n=5); and others (1.4%, n=5).

## 3.3.2 Access to Land Resource, Income and Youth

### Access to Productive land

The average land area of female-headed households (n=77) is lower than that of male-headed households (n=286), at 0.560 ha and 0.429 ha respectively. However, there is no significant difference in average land area between these two groups*. In terms of Land Use Right Certificate (LURC), for Perennial Land, 43% of respondents (n=35) indicate both husband and wife have shared land title; 15% (n=12) has husband only on LURC and 3% (n=7) has only wife on LURC. The same pattern applies for Annual Land where both husband and wife have their names on LURC (46%).

* Mann-Whitney U test, p<0.05

1*(119)=1,388, n=205; Cohen’s d=0.171


---
**Page 153**
---

**Monthly cash income**

The monthly cash income earned by all household members from the sample (n=580) is $3,192[571]NID. Monthly cash income earned is higher than female: 3,776[429] NID vs 2,529[315] NID.

**Youth**

For youth (16-30 years of age), there are 235 youth members (male=124; male+female total=235). One member reported from 353 households in the survey. The monthly cash income of 88 youth members is $3,438[806] (male: $3,956[608], female: $2,096[108]). However, income differences between male and female are not statistically significant*. This suggests that youth does not only make more income than older group but also do not have income difference between male and female. The monthly cash income of 45 youth households is $3,776[289] NID.

The average land area of youth households is lower than that of non-youth households (0.78[0.06] and 10.50[0.56], respectively). There is also a significant difference in average land area owned by households headed by youth and households headed by non-youth*. The average number of outstanding loans this year among youth households have (n=109) is lower than that of non-youth households (248) and is 1.65 and 1.31, respectively. There are significant differences in average number of outstanding loans between these two groups*.

**3.3 Labour Division**

Participants from the survey seems to follow the division of labor that is based on the traditional gender roles: female members are likely involved in domestic works, particularly activities that are light physically and are related to finance, such as keeping money, spending more food and children education. On the contrary, male members are in charge for heavy works, including activities that require physical strength or involve risk, such as farming, fishing, hunting, etc., during community meetings. The works that male and female members share least include works that are typically done by only male or only female. For example, minor repair in home and pesticide spraying are done by mainly male while cooking, washing dishes, laundry, family diet, keeping money, etc., are done by female member include household chores (cooking, dish washing, laundry, family diet, keeping money). Other works that are less shared and therefore done mainly by male including works that involve going out of home such as buying agricultural input, attending meetings, trainings.

3.3.4 Decision Making

With regards to decision making as to domestic works and agricultural works, although most works are shared, female member do as much as twice male members with regarding to work items that female do mainly and solely (1,019 vs 540 responses).

Figure 16 – Decision Making (domestic works and agricultural works)

| Item | Female Members | Male Members |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Child Care | 382 | 7089 |
| Cooking | 5334 | 1012 |
| Cleaning | 540 | - |

However, when it comes to internal/home issue, male members appears to be less decisive. In fact, female make sole decision four times higher than male (315 vs 81 responses). This may be explained by the fact that most of survey respondents are from ethnic minority groups that practice matrilineal in which the mother or a female elder has authority over the family group.

However, when it comes to internal/home issue, male members appears to be less decisive. This may be explained by the fact that most of survey participants are from ethnic minority groups that practice matrilocy in which the mother or a female elder has authority over the family group

### 3.3.5 Agency

Improving women's agency, particularly their ability to define goals and act on them, is crucial for advancing gender equality and the empowerment of women (Meadall et al. 2017). In this analysis, ten questions were asked from female survey respondent (n=140). A 10-point scale was used with scores for ten questions with 1 indicating that female participants do not have at all the ability to control whereas 10 indicate participants can fully control. These questions are in Figure below: Results show that while most respondents have some level of agency, there is a significant difference between male and female survey notably with the following questions (indicated by yellow and red colour in the last row for SD):

- Question 2. To which extent participant can identify business opportunities to start up a new business
- Question 5. To which extent participant can save money in order to invest in future business opportunities
- Question 7. To which extent participant can bargain to obtain cheap prices when you are buying anything for the business
- Question 8. To which extent participant can protect your business assets from harm by others
- Question 10. To which extent participant can collect the money someone owes you

The scores for these five questions suggested that the ability of female participants from the sample vary considerably on to identify business opportunities, save money, bargain, protect their assets and collect money owed to them. It is noted that lowest mean score is with Question 2 (Identify business opportunities to start up a new business) and Question 9 (Protect your business assets from harm by others). These suggest the female participant in the survey are not confident about identifying new business opportunities and about protecting their business assets from harm by others.

# 4. GENDER ACTION & SOCIAL INCLUSION PLAN

## 4.1 Approach to developing Gender Action & Social Inclusion Plan (GASIP)

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is aligned with the logical framework of the project. This aims to ensure the activities proposed in the GAP are lined up with the overall project design to contribute to achieving the overall goal of the project. Meanwhile, the proposed activities and related performance indicators are based on a review of existing evidence and lessons learned from previous experience. Responsive gender activities are based on the basis of analysis of constraints to gender mainstreaming, bi opportunities to overcome such constraints, and ci priority areas for promoting gender equality in economic performance.

## 4.2 Gender Chain Results

There has been a growing evidence at global level that provides specific transmission pathways from gender equality to improved economic growth (Kabeer et al., 2013; Eloughb et al., 2013). Transmission pathways are linked up with the macroeconomic performance in a manner that increased gender equality increase economic performance. This process facilitates a social change in which social norms and practices are changed, leading to improved economic improvements. In a more specific way, improved education, health and nutrition lead to increased participation of female workers as in the local labour force, resulting in improved productivity (WB 20/20).

Reducing and redistributing unpaid home chores is also a common domestic dimension that allows participants to increase their time for paid work or other activities that take place outside their home base. According to the WB (20/20), when this can be done at country level, short-term economic


---
**Page 158**
---

### 4.3 Responsibility of governmental agencies

#### 4.3.1 Provincial Peoples Committee
- Oversee all operational aspects of provincial PMU for the project, including the GAP implementation.
- Provide overall guidance to provincial PMU with regards to GAP implementation, particularly implementing the GAP as an integral part of provincial annual socioeconomic development program and gender mainstreaming in agro- and agricultural sector.
- Monitor regularly PMU's project activities and ensure that activities set forth in the GAP are implemented effectively and timely.

#### 4.3.2 Project Management Board
- Responsible for overall implementation of the project, including the GAP.
- Ensure funding is allocated sufficiently and timely for implementation of planned activities.
- Work as lead agency for GAP implementation and coordinate with other governmental agencies and non-governmental and functional agencies – at provincial, district and commune levels, to carry out planned activities.
- Maintain regular monitoring of GAP implementation, at quarterly, bi-annually, and annually
- Conduct periodic review and evaluation of GAP implementation to ensure the overall progress towards achieving project objectives.
- Where necessary, update the GAP based on development needs and feedback from EM communities in the project area. These development needs should be in line with the logical framework of the project.

#### 4.3.3 District Peoples Committee
- Support PMU in selecting communes that are potential for planning and carrying out activities proposed in the GAP.
- Ensure the activities set forth in GAP are carried in a manner that is integrated with the overall socioeconomic development plan of the district, and in connection with other annual district development plans.
- Provide overall guidance to continue PGM within the district in GAP implementation.

#### 4.3.4 Commune Peoples Committee
- Collaborate and support PMU in implementing GAP.
- Support PMU in carrying out consultation/ training/ workshops/ with effective participation of female headed households, male headed households, and women headed households.
- Provide regular support and encourage the participation of women and female headed households in all project related activities, particularly consultation meetings, trainings, workshops.

# 5. CONCLUSIONS AND PRIORITY AREAS

## 5.1 Conclusions

### 5.1.1 Potential barriers to economic participation of women

Based on the gender analysis and gender assessment above, a number of key barriers to the participation of women in economic activities have been identified for the project:

- **Social norms**
The social norms in Vietnam have long shaped how men and women within a family divide their responsibilities—domestic and income generation activities. This still exists these days. This norm was originally based on labor division between women and men, based on their natural physical health. While men are generally responsible for physically heavy works, and works considered dangerous or harmful to the body such as the construction of houses, digging wells, carrying heavy loads, etc., women do most of the care work, such as preparing meals, doing chore work, and do home gardening and domestic animal feeding. Because of these responsibility patterns men are considered a person responsible for income generation in works that demand physical strength while women are expected to stay at home. Even when some ethnic groups adopt matrilineal, such as Chín, Khơ, P'ra, Ede, Bana, women are generally decision makers, and are allowed to discuss about how men can do in terms of heavy work, whereas women are responsible for children's education care. Arimura Yamaguchi (2016) also found that the study (n=453) that all that women and girls participating in their survey experience serious gender stereotypes in labor division.

- **Burden of care**
The social norm defines domestic works for women. As such, women spend most of their time at home, taking care of works that are unpaid—works that are easily forgotten, or recognized by other family members as well as community. Chore works typically take women most of their time—day and night. This does not leave them any time for socialized activities, such as attending community meetings, participating in income generating activities, etc., which are usually organized outside their family. During the day, they are also involved in home farming, and/or farming in the field for certain period of time, such as seed preparation, seeding, and crop care, weeding, harvesting, and selling. A study by Arimura Yamaguchi (2016) found that women spend an average 35 hours per week on three main regions of Vietnam. The findings revealed that women worked an average 35 hours per week compared with 21 hours for men. In particular, in areas with poor gender based public services and commercial infrastructure, women tend to give up their activities because they depend entirely on work.

| TOTAL | | | | **13,902** |


---
**Page 161**
---

- **Human capital**
  Rural life is typically difficult. Thus children, at their very young age, are familiar with household chore. Some are involved in farming to provide additional labor to family farming. Boys and girls thus tend to drop out of school early. This leads to a vicious cycle that makes it hard for them to continue education because of limited or even no educational opportunities as young women start to take care of their family while young men are involved in farming/income generation activities to support their livelihoods.
  This create a vicious cycle that is hard to break out in many instances in rural areas, particularly for poor ethnic minorities.

- **Vulnerable groups**
  With regards to vulnerable groups such as family workers, women consistently have a much higher representation than men with up to 60.4% of family labor being female. In contrast, among wage workers and self-employed persons, females have a significantly lower representation compared to males. Specifically, women only account for 43.9% in the wage workers (12.2 percentage points lower than male). This indicated gender inequality in employment. However, it's noted that the gender gap in the proportion of female workers in the agricultural sector has narrowed from 50.8 percentage points in 2019 to 12.2 percentage points in 2022, according to 2022 Labor Force Participation Report of the Vietnam General Statistics Office.

- **Wage and earning gaps**
  There is still a discrimination with regards to women in terms of wage gap, particularly to the same work done by men. According to Vietnam General Statistics Office (2022), about 60% of women in Vietnam are in the labour force. It is noted in the female labour force in rural area is higher than in urban area – 61.8% vs rural vs 38.2% in urban area. The labour force participation rate varied across regions and sectors, with a higher proportion of women working in agriculture (70%) compared to other sectors such as industry and services (54%). Women also have lower rates of labour force participation (78.1%) while two key delta have lower rate: 62.1% for Red River Delta, and 58.5% for Mekong Delta region, for example. In the recent past few years, women had a higher representation than men in the agricultural sector but still lag behind in other sectors such as industry and services (also worker) category. This indicates a progress in addressing gender inequality in job access in Vietnam.

  Gender pay gap still exist. The pay gap between women and men continues to be an important factor behind wage inequality, according to the ILO Global Wage Report 2022-2023. In Vietnam, the average monthly earnings of female workers are lower than that of male workers by about 15% (ILO, 2022). This pattern is the same for 2020 and 2021 (2022 Labor Force Participation Report of GSO). In agriculture, in 2015, for instance, about 52% of farm workers were women. Similarly, about 55% of self-employed farmers are women. The average monthly earnings of female farmers are lower than that of male farmers by an average of 34%. Women's earnings are, on average, below the minimum wage. This source of income is also risky as they are prone to adverse impact of changing weather and highly sensitive to price fluctuation due to market demand (WB 2018).

- **Job market**


---
**Page 162**
---

In rural area, job market is limited. This is especially evident in remote mountainous where form works are dominant. Given this, when searching for jobs, women tend to look for jobs near home while men prefer to go far work as migrant workers. In the context of this project, it is likely that women, including women in ethnic minority communities, are likely to focus on their domestic farming or farming activities (for those who having access to productive land).

- Climate change and natural disasters, and COVID-19
Both climate change and natural disasters such as flash flood, drought, typhoon, are likely to affect farming activities negatively. While climate change is likely to reduce the yield of some crops, that affect crop yield, natural disaster can cause have loss to the crops, which result in partial or complete loss of crop, which push the households back in their effort of making economic progress.

### Opportunities for gender mainstreaming into project activities towards closing gap
Despite the above key constraints to economic participation of women, opportunities are available for improvement under the project. The following section identify five priority areas that the project can focus on: community-based income generation, strengthening women's role and the level of participation of women in income generation activities over the project life. In particular, it highlight the fundamental role women play, particularly through their production of food, water, free and forest degradation management. This is likely to bring about a gradual shift in social norms/gender stereotypes in which women serve as agents of change in this social change process.

## Priority Areas
### Priority One – Enhance women participation in project planning and implementation
Women are traditionally responsible for most of household chores. This unpaid work take them not only more than half time in a day but also restrict them from learning chance to upgrade their farming skills which is essential for improved crop yield, and for being accepted into a value chain. To free up women gradually from traditional household chore, it is essential that their husband share the household chores such as cooking, cleaning, house maintenance, etc. As women have more time to participate in training, community meetings, and farming activities, they contribute to increasing family income – through optimized labor and farm yield. To promote women participation in project planning and implementation, it is important to ensure that women are not left behind when off farm season, or during time when farm work low. It is also important to be sensitive in arranging the timing of such meetings and trainings during the day. Women's workload have diurnal variations and it is important to consider this when scheduling training sessions and community meetings at a time during the day when it does not clash with the time of the day when demands on the women for domestic chores is highest. This is to facilitate sharing of household chore on the part of the male, of work division in the household, and also to ensure that women are not left behind. Training content and capacity building events need to be designed based on women's need and aspiration. As such, these need to be gender sensitive, e.g types/focus of technologies/techniques, training events, promoting ties/villages/communes. Steps that could be taken to promote sharing of household chore include:


---
**Page 163**
---

- **Awareness raising**: Both men and women of the same households (project target groups) need to understand the need to foster women’s participation in learning and more participation is increased through awareness raising activities such as: project planning, larger community meetings, and media campaign (Information, Education, Communication). It is anticipated that encouraging women to participate in learning and income generation activities will be a challenge because many women are expected to stay at home while their husband migrate for works outside the community. But understanding and support from their husband is fundamental to their increased participation in project activities.
- **Access to income generation activities**: Once awareness is improved there is a family support system that encourages female members of households are encouraged to participate in relevant trainings for income generating activities, including loan access to improved production at farm level.
- **Sharing of household chore**: Sharing of household chore is a process that happens at varying pace depending on each household. The sharing process allow female members to spare more time for self-learning and for taking part in additional income generation, as they wish, to increase their participation in project activities.
- **Success case**: Successful sharing should be shared at group level (loan group) to encourage and support other member who still lag behind, and to support in increase awareness for household chore sharing. The sharing of household chore is a process that happens at varying pace depending on each household. The sharing process allow female members to spare more time for self-learning and for taking part in additional income generation, as they wish, to increase their participation in project activities.
- **5.2.2 Paternity Two - Improve women’s skills for improved income and balanced household chore**: When women are freed up gradually from their long-graded domestic role, they can work closely with their husband on a wide range of issues, selecting the service decision, deciding the selling price to a better joint decision making process. When women participate effectively in income generation activities, they are in a better position to negotiate with their husband who would share the home chore load and is more likely to accept their spouse going out for learning and meeting events organized for women. As farming becomes more efficient through improved skills, increased productivity, reduced labour exchange, to boost crop yield and quality, the couple and other family members can rearrange for a more optimised division of labour that they both find acceptable to achieve better income for their family, which in turn will improve the overall well-being of the family. This is because women’s family, and women, in project’s value chain, which contribute to only economic empowerment for women, but also, for the family. In addition, special efforts must be made by the project to organize trainings and capacity building activities that are gender sensitive and inclusive so as to ensure that women’s confidence (and subsequent participation) in setting up and managing enterprises is enhanced. This, over time, reduce their dependency on natural forest product thanks to increased income generation from other sources. The traditional male dominated decision-making pattern will be changed towards more participation of women in family’s income generation activities. A long-term goal would be to slowly change the gender stereotype and gender social norms.


---
**Page 164**
---

### 5.2.3 Priority Three – Create mechanisms that allow more and consistent women participation

Project activities that promote women’s participation should create a condition that requires women participants to enable the household to benefit from project activities. Potential activities include loan programs. As a longer-term, local financial service provider could be supported/encouragement to ensure women participation is a sufficient and necessary condition to qualify for a pro-poor loan. Project activities that promote women’s participation in project activities should also create conditions that require participation of women in early activities. This aims to ensure family and husband consistently support the women to participate in project activities in a longer time and women participation is not at risk during the busy season. The project will encourage female-headed households to participate in women participation for the planning meeting to proceed. If this target is compromised, there is no impact for later meetings that have higher level of women participation. Thus, the target of at least 40% women participation from all households.

### 5.2.4 Focus Group: Facilitators for enhanced female-headed households to join value chains

It is important to support female headed-households (including those whose husbands are far away for earning income) who want to start a business in agri-, agro-business to improve their family income. At country level, the percentage of female headed households is 21.86%. In Central Highland, it is 23.79%. The number of female-headed households in this region has increased from 50% to 64% and have increased over the past ten years – from 18.6% in 2008 to 23.7% in 2018. The project will encourage female headed-households to take part in business development courses to learn how to start and improve their businesses, including how to access credit, how to manage their business for better change, and to meet the market demand. As female headed-household appear to have less access to land compared to male-headed households10, it is necessary these household need additional support to ensure they are not excluded from project activities.

Each agro-commodity (e.g., coffee, tea, rubber, cassava, bean, maize) requires different role of women in farming these commodities, including their time devotion. Mainstreaming gender for each commodity aims to recognize the roles and knowledge of women in farming these crops. It does not take women’s participation as a given or assume that women are naturally excluded from project to identify ways to ensure women can participate in the right trainings that are essential to their existing role, and such as enable women to upgrade their farming skill for better application to contribute to the overall success of their crops.

### 5.2.3 Priority Three – Promote Gender Mainstreaming among Youth

Since the skill of youth and access to land resources are limited, the average income of male and female among youth is not different statistically compared to that between male and female in the group older than youth (more than 30 years of age). Targeting youth group in the project area is one effective way to promote gender mainstreaming. Youth have a higher level of education and are more active in learning and applying new knowledge, they are more capable physically and thus can spend more effective time in farming activities and later on value chains. Their initial results achieved from project support can serve as champion in the social change process within their community and thus can

### 5.2.5 Priority Seven: Enhance Women Participation in FLA, CFEM and PFES

There are limited participations of women in Forest Land Allocation process (FLA), Community Forest Management (CFM) and PFES (Payment for Forest Environmental Services). The project will focus on promoting the unfolding of FLA, CFEM and PFES which have been part of government's program. To ensure that women benefit from these programs and also to enhance their security of tenure of land and benefit from PFES, it is important women need to be included in planning process throughout implementation of these programs, particularly for areas where FLA, CFEM and PFES have not been unfolded yet.

### 5.2.7 Priority Seven – Enhance Security of tenure for women in forest in lands and other resources

Security of tenure for women as reflected by the figures for land holding (36%) suggest the need for specific attention to be accorded to this aspect. It is well acknowledged that tenural security significantly contributes to poverty alleviation and well-being. Efforts by the project must ensure that any land allocations are done in a manner which ensures that women's names are included, and this allocation formalized through land certifications or titling reflecting the names of the wife and husband. Even when community allocations are done, tenural arrangements (e.g., access to plot for individual use) should be made in such a way as to ensure that women have equal rights with their husbands' names. In addition, it is important to assist women to have access to important daily resources such as access to clean water which is more handy (water fetching is time and labour demanding) and access to clean energy for household lighting, cooking, heating and productive enterprises.

# Annex 2 – Actions to be taken to minimize and mitigate GBV risk (including SEA/SH) as part of Gender Action and Social Inclusion Plan (GASP)

## Project PREPARATION

### Actions to Address GBV risk (including SEA/SH)
- **At project level**: social assessment (results reflected in the project document), key stakeholders are screened, identified, and key mitigation measures proposed.
- **Provincial PMUs** sensitized on the importance of identifying and addressing GBV risks, including mitigation measures, and implementation mechanisms.
- **GAP** developed with clear gender equality and inclusion engagement Plan to ensure project affected people and project workers have access to appropriate protection measures, including project’s level GGRAM for GBV.

### Timing for Action
- Project Preparation

### Who is Responsible for Actions
- IFAD and FAO (as lead) and Provincial PMUs (as supporting)

### Ongoing Risk Management
- SEA/SH risks and prevention measures reflected in project’s ESMF, SEP, GASP and gap.

### Estimate Cost & Budget
- IFAD (completed, as part of social assessment).

## PROJECT IMPLEMENTATION

### Actions to Address GBV risk (including SEA/SH)
- **At project level**: *SEA/SH* from consultation meetings (as part of project’s overall Stakeholder Engagement).
- Conduct project-level awareness raising campaign, including sensitization of local Women’s Unions as potential GBV service providers.
- Mainstream GBV related information into project’s monitoring and evaluation framework.
- Report on GBV incidence status in PMMU’s quarterly implementation report.
- Review annual PMMU’s capacity to identify, prevent and respond to GBV within the project cycle.
- At infrastructure scheme (for civil works): Scheme for SEA/SH risk (as part of preparation of site-specific mitigation measures), including provisions related GBV (including SEA/SH) in the bidding documents and contract terms.
Key provisions include: a) zero tolerance for GBV (including SEA/SH); b) clear communication about GBV/SEA/SH risks; c) Code of Conducts for contractor’s workers prepared, understood, and signed by workers prior to commencement of work; d) Grievance Redress disclosed to contractors’ workers.

### Timing for Action
- Year 1

### Who is Responsible for Actions
- Year 1, repeated mid-term.
- Provincial PMUs
- Quarterly Reports submitted to IFAD
- Provincial PMUs
- IFAD project team
- Preparation of infrastructure scheme (before civil works)
- Prior review of tender documents and work contract by IFAD procurement team

### Who is Responsible for Actions
- Provincial PMUs
- Provincial PMUs and Contractors

### Ongoing Risk Management
- Ongoing review during supervision missions and provide guidance and support as necessary.

### Estimate Cost & Budget
- 3,519 USD (project funding).
